Summary 
This addendum contains the following six parts: 
a)
A funding proposal summary titled “India E-Mobility Financing Program” submitted by
Macquarie Alternative Assets Management Limited (MAAML);
b)
No-objection letters issued by the national designated authorities or focal point(s);
c)
Environmental and Social report(s) Disclosure;
d)
Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment;
e)
Response from the accredited entity to the independent Technical Advisory Panel’s
assessment; and
f)
Gender documentation of the funding proposal.
These documents are presented as submitted by the accredited entity and the national designated 
authority(ies) or focal point(s), respectively. Pursuant to the Comprehensive Information Disclosure 
Policy of the Fund, the funding proposal titled “India E-Mobility Financing Program” submitted by 
Macquarie Alternative Assets Management Limited (MAAML) is being circulated on a limited 
distribution basis only to Board Members and Alternate Board Members to ensure confidentiality of 
certain proprietary, legally privileged or commercially sensitive information of the entity.

Note to Accredited Entities on the use of the funding proposal template 
•
Accredited Entities should provide summary information in the proposal with cross-reference to annexes such as
feasibility studies, gender action plan, term sheet, etc.
•
Accredited Entities should ensure that annexes provided are consistent with the details provided in the funding
proposal. Updates to the funding proposal and/or annexes must be reflected in all relevant documents.
•
The total number of pages for the funding proposal (excluding annexes) should not exceed 60. Proposals
exceeding the prescribed length will not be assessed within the usual service standard time.
•
The recommended font is Arial, size 11.
•
Under the GCF Information Disclosure Policy, project and programme funding proposals will be disclosed
on the GCF website, simultaneous with the submission to the Board, subject to the redaction of any information
that may not be disclosed pursuant to the IDP. Accredited Entities are asked to fill out information on disclosure
in section G.4.
Please submit the completed proposal to: 
fundingproposal@gcfund.org

“FP-MAAML-India-[2022/04/25]”

A.21. Executive summary (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages) 
Climate change is one of the biggest challenges being faced by India. Between 1901-2018, India’s average temperature 
rose by 0.7°C4 primarily led by an increase in Greenhouse Gases (GHG) emissions, which have more than doubled 
from 1.2 GtCO2 in 2005 to 2.6 GtCO2 in 2018.5 This rapid growth in GHG emissions has been driven by multiple factors, 
such as high population growth rate, increasing levels of industrialisation and urbanisation. This increase in average 
temperature has already affected the livelihood of millions of people, with more frequent sporadic climatic events such 
as cloud bursts, flash floods, prolonged droughts, etc. becoming a norm. 
By 2100, it is projected that temperatures will rise by approximately 4.7°C to 5.5°C6 resulting in an exponential increase 
in extreme climatic events, vector borne diseases, food shortage, migration, and loss of livelihoods, all of which will 
have adverse impact on India’s economic development. According to Oxford Economics, India could lose 90% of its 
Gross Domestic Product (GDP) by 2100 if active steps are not taken to mitigate the GHG emissions7 (see mitigation 
profile of India under section B.1, as well as discussions under section B.2 and D.4). 
 
4 https://reliefweb.int/report/india/assessment-climate-change-over-indian-region-report-ministry-earth-sciences-moes 
5 https://www.eurekalert.org/news-releases/914976 
6 https://reliefweb.int/report/india/assessment-climate-change-over-indian-region-report-ministry-earth-sciences-moes 
7 https://www.livemint.com/news/india/india-s-gdp-could-fall-90-by-2100-due-to-climate-change-analysis-11594187614729.html

=== PAGE 7 ===
A 
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 3 
 
 
Whilst the Indian government has been taking proactive steps pursuing various mitigation and adaptation activities in 
the form of strong policy support for renewable energy and electric mobility sectors to reduce GHG emissions, further 
support is required to help India achieve its Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs) agreed under the Paris 
Agreement and COP26 pledges. According to a recent report by Lifegate,8 India needs investment of approximately 
USD 2.5 trillion between 2015 and 2030, or USD 170 billion / year, to meet its NDCs by 2030. Further, a recent study 
by Climate Policy Initiative (CPI) reports that India is currently mobilising less than 25% of the investment needed to 
reach this target9.  
 
Additionally, since early 2020, the onset of the COVID-19 pandemic has pushed the government to change its priorities, 
shifting substantial focus to creating an effective healthcare ecosystem to deliver quality primary care to citizens. Due 
to the recessionary impact of the COVID-19 pandemic, along with other socio-economic issues, government finances 
remain constrained. In FY21, India’s fiscal deficit was 9.3%.10 This is projected to be 6.8% in FY22,11 significantly above 
its target of 3.0% set under the Fiscal Responsibility and Budget Management (FRBM) Act 2003 (a law enacted to 
institutionalise financial discipline, strengthen fiscal prudence and improve macroeconomic management).12 This may 
cause a substantial budgetary shortfall, limiting the government’s ability to accelerate and deliver GHG emission 
reducing projects going forwards. 
 
In the above context, the proposed Programme (the Programme) aims to leverage GCF and commercial investor 
capital to accelerate the decarbonisation of the Indian economy by focusing on electrification of India’s transport 
sector. This will be enabled by supporting faster adoption of electric vehicles and growth of associated infrastructure, 
replacing, and limiting the new purchase of, traditional Internal Combustion Engine (ICE) vehicles.  
 
The main objectives of the Programme are as follows: 
1. to provide suitable financial solutions (for example, operationally integrated, performance guaranteed leases 
or structured credit instruments) to electric vehicle owners and operators; 
2. to provide tailored financial solutions to ancillary areas to further develop the electric vehicle ecosystem, such 
as charging infrastructure and batteries; 
3. using financial solutions to mitigate current user concerns, including high upfront costs, residual value risk and 
reliability; 
4. to facilitate development of EV ecosystem through investments, partnerships, and capability development of 
market participants; 
5. to set a high benchmark and continually improve the efficiency of services in the Indian EV domain via 
improvement of systems, processes, and resources; 
6. to provide a competitively priced solutions that enhance the long-term Total Cost of Ownership (TCO) 
competitiveness of EVs vis-à-vis ICE vehicles, in alignment with the Indian government’s policy; and 
7. to mobilise significant amount of nascent institutional capital into the electrification of India’s road transport 
sector.  
 
The Programme is seeking USD 200 million from GCF, and an additional USD 205 million from commercial investors, 
to set up an India focused E-mobility Financing Platform (the Platform).13 It will be managed by Macquarie Asset 
Management (MAM), the world’s largest infrastructure asset manager,14 with an established presence in India. If 
successful, it is anticipated that the Platform will access local and international debt markets for ~USD 1.1 billion to 
further grow its operations, and may draw in additional equity capital from commercial investors as EV-ICE cost parity 
approaches transitioning to an unsubsidised enduring commercial enterprise.  
 
The Programme is innovatively structured to a) address some of the key barriers limiting the accelerated growth of the 
EV sector, b) complement existing initiatives of the government and other private players, and c) align with India’s 
NDCs, National Climate Action Plan (NCAP), COP26 pledges, as well various other policy and regulatory initiatives.

 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 4 
 
The Programme aims to introduce first of its kind financing solutions (operating and finance leases and other 
structured credit solutions) into, potentially, one of the largest EV markets in the world, to reduce high upfront capex 
associated with EVs, impediments around EV charging infrastructure and uncertainty around commercial EV 
performance. Presently, these are not offered at scale by existing market participants. The constrained availability of 
suitable financial products is primarily due to a limited understanding of the EV ecosystem, the relatively small current 
market size and residual value concerns. The proposed Programme is poised to change that. By enhancing the 
availability of credit and introducing new financial solutions in the EV market that accept operational performance and 
other risks, the Programme seeks to increase the attractiveness of EV ownership, help Original Equipment 
Manufacturers (OEMs) / sponsors recycle capital faster and cause EVs to become the preferred mode of transport 
among fleet operators.  
 
Initially the Programme will focus on select segments of the EV ecosystem, such as e-buses, shared fleets and electric 
charging infrastructure. It will then slowly diversify its portfolio and potentially expand into retail segment as more sub-
sectors within the EV value chain gain scale and prominence. Over the implementation period, the Programme is 
expected is to mobilise up to USD 2.1 billion15 of capital into the sector, and targets to reduce ~9.5 MtCO2e over its 
lifetime. 
 
Apart from the above, the Programme will assist India to achieve energy security by reducing its dependence on oil 
imports, which forms ~80% of the total import bill of the country.16 It will also help in the creation of new jobs and 
stimulate economic activity, with a corollary of empowering women and reducing gender bias. Any capital investment 
and support for the local economy will be extremely critical during the times of pandemic, where millions of people have 
lost jobs and are pushed to poverty each day. Lastly, it will promote environmental sustainability and increase health 
benefits through the reductionof pollution. It is also noteworthy that the Executing Entities, together with the Accredited 
Entity, will promote strong corporate governance standards and manage fiduciary risks in line with GCF policies and 
requirements (as agreed in GCF’s Accreditation Master Agreement (AMA) and Funded Activity Agreement (FAA)) as 
well as international best practices for climate impact planning and monitoring, environmental and social management, 
and gender mainstreamed approach. 
 
15 Includes redeployment of internal accruals  
16 Economic Times Publication on “India’s crude oil import – April 2020”.

 PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION 
B.1. Climate context (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages) 
Overall climate rationale  
The aim of this Programme is to facilitate the reduction of greenhouse gases and other emissions from the road 
transport sector in India through the accelerated adoption of electric vehicles. This can be enabled through targeted 
investments across the e-mobility value chain, which can help address some of the key issues impeding the sector, 
such as high upfront capex, technology / obsolescence risk, range and operational performance anxiety, inadequate 
availability of charging infrastructure as well as financing solutions through the existing channels. The envisaged 
Programme can help create an enabling environment for local investment and ultimately catalyse the scaling up of the 
nascent market.  
Climate context 
How has India’s climate already changed? 
India has varied weather and climatic patterns across the country, with an average temperature of 2°C17 in Jammu and 
Kashmir (North India) vis-à-vis 29°C18 in southern India. The amount and frequency of rainfall is equally diverse, with 
parts of Meghalaya receiving more than 4,000 mm19 of precipitation annually, compared to parts of Thar Desert in 
north-western India which receive less than 100 mm20 of rainfall every year. The rainfall in each region is primarily 
affected by two weather systems, South-West Monsoon (June to September) which brings the majority of the rainfall 
in the country, and North-East Monsoon (October to December) which plays an important role in southern India. 
Given the size, topography, and climatic diversity across India, the impact of climate change on each region is varied. 
According to a recent Indian government report, the average temperature across the country increased 0.62°C over 
the last 100 years.21 Even though the temperature increase is lower than the global average, severe impacts are already 
being felt.  
Between 1985 and 2009, western and southern India experienced 50% more heatwaves than in the previous 25 
years.22 Between 1950 and 2015, central India experienced a threefold increase in extreme rainfall and flood events, 
leading to death of thousands and displacement of millions.23 This is in contrast to 6% decline in average precipitation 
during monsoon events between 1951 and 2015, which has been primarily been offset by melting glaciers of the Hindu 
Kush region, which have retreated at an average 18 metres per year over the last four decades.24 Further, with rising 
levels of urbanisation, economic activity, and variations in weather patterns across the country, water is becoming more 
scarce with each passing day. Today, one billion people in India experience severe water scarcity for at least one month 
each year, with 180 million facing water scarcity all year round.25  
How will India’s climate change going forward? 
As climate change continues, its impact will be more profound. For India, the intensity of the impact of climate change 
will increase due to rising levels of greenhouse gases, led by rapid population growth, urbanisation, strong industrial 
growth, and a significant increase in average household income. 
According to a recent report, the level of precipitation, especially during monsoons, and glaciers in the Hindu Kush 
region will significantly reduce over the next few decades. Consequently, the flow of water in some of the main river 
systems, such as Indus, Ganges and Brahmaputra, will fall by 8.4%, 17.6% and 19.6% respectively by mid-century 
compared to the 2000s. This is expected to result in severe water scarcity and displacement of more than 270 million 
people in the Indus and Brahmaputra basins.26 According to the WWF’s Water Risk Filter Tool,27 by 2050, 30 cities in

India are expected to suffer the largest rise in water risk,28 and nearly 36 million people in India will be threatened by 
flooding annually if greenhouse gas emissions are not reduced.29 Additionally, the level of cyclonic storms in the Bay 
of Bengal region is expected is to double by 2070-2100, compared to the baseline period of 1961-1990, leaving millions 
of low-income households susceptible to food shortage, loss of livelihoods and vector borne diseases.  
The economic impact of the above will be significant. Climate change has already reduced the pace of poverty reduction 
and increased the level of inequality in the country. According to Burke and Tanutama (2019), the districts that have 
warmed the fastest have seen their GDPs grow 56% less on average than those that have warmed the slowest. On an 
overall basis, climate change could result in India’s GDP falling 2.6% by 210030, even if the global temperature increase 
is below 2°C,31 and 13.4% if the temperate increase is 4°C.32 Further, without the current costs of global warming, 
India’s GDP would be 25% higher than it’s current levels33. Additionally, Nixon predicts that, if temperatures increase 
by 3°C,34 India’s GDP will be 90% lower in 2100 that it would have been without climate change.  
These factors put India at a critical inflection point wherein there is a need to balance economic growth, population 
health, recovery from the pandemic and GHG emissions to limit climate change impact. India’s emissions reduction 
pathway is paramount to the fight against climate change both locally and globally.  
India’s fight against climate change 
As a result of increasing GHG emissions and exposure to the effects of climate change, the Indian government has put 
several policies and targets in place to support its contribution to reducing global emissions. This is outlined in India’s 
NDCs  and COP26 pledges. Furthermore, the government is willing to take on a more ambitious target based on 
investment and support from both public and private sectors.35  
Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs) 
 
COP26 pledges 
At the COP26 meeting in Glasglow in November 2021, India pledged itself to five unconditional targets in order to 
improve its commitments to climate change and lead the world’s decarbonisation drive. These include: 
1. increase non-fossil energy capacity to 500 GW (gigawatts) by 2030; 
2. meet 50 percent of energy requirements from renewable energy by 2030;

3. reduce the total projected carbon emissions by 1 billion tonnes by 2030; 
4. reduce the carbon intensity of the economy by less than 45 percent; and 
5. achieve net zero carbon by 2070. 
 
The role of transport sector in emission generation 
Present status 
According to an Asian Development Bank (ADB) report, the transport sector accounts for 23% of all energy-related 
CO2 emissions globally.36 However, in India, it contributes only 13% of total carbon dioxide emissions despite being the 
third largest GHG emitting sector in the country. Further, as per IEA, carbon-dioxide emissions from India’s transport 
sector have more than tripled since 1990,37 of which road transport contributes ~90%. This is driven by: 
• 
a significant increase in passenger and freight transport activity – Passenger and freight traffic by inland mode 
have both tripled between 2000 and 2020 to 6 trillion passenger kms and ~1.8 trillion tonne kms respectively, 
which has been predmoniately driven by the road sector,38 and; 
• 
the sector’s high reliance on fossil fuels – The Indian transport sector has grown 50% since 2010, and now 
accounts for 20% of the country’s final energy use (in terms of exajoules). Oil products contribute 95% of final 
energy used in the transport sector, with natural gas, biofuels, etc. comprising of the remaining portion. Further, 
as the energy use has increased over the last two decades, GHG emissions too have increased, implying a 
strong causal relationship between the two (refer to the below charts for more details).  
 
Figure: Energy use by mode in passenger and freight transport in India (exajoules), 2000-202039 
 
Figure: CO2 emissions by passenger and freight traffic in India (MtCO2e), 2000-202040

Going forward 
To limit the impact of growing GHG emissions from India’s road sector, a multi pronged approach is required. According 
to a report by Climate Action Tracker, the electrification of vehicles is “absolutely essential” if India is to achieve net 
zero emissions from its road sector by 2050. The electrification of vehicles has the potential to reduce emissions by 
570 MtCO2e/year, which is 2.8 times more of a reduction than can be achieved through innovative urban planning and 
the use of alternate fuels.41 However, to achieve this ambitious target, all new vehicles sales will need to be electric by 
2035, a mammoth task given the electric vehicle penetration was just 0.7% of total vehicle sales in FY20.  
Figure: Annual emissions from the transport sector until 2050 (MtCO2e) 
 
Whilst both central and state governments have introduced policies and initiatives to promote electric vehicles over the 
last few years, such as the Faster Adoption and Manufacturing of Hybrid and EV (FAME) subsidy scheme I and II, the 
National Electric Mobility Mission Plan (NEMMP), and the vehicle scrappage policy, a lot more needs to be done to 
achieve the emission reduction targets mentioned above. According to a report by Niti Aayog, Govt. of India’s think 
tank, an estimated USD 266 billion of capital is required to be invested in the EV industry over next 10 years.42 To date, 
only USD 5-6 billion has been invested, the majority of which has been over the last 12 months. There are several key 
barriers that need to be addressed before significant private capital starts to invest. These include the high upfront cost 
of EVs, unproven operational performance, limited availability of financing, low product offerings from OEMs and 
inadequate infrastructure buildout, primarily charging stations (refer to section B.2 – Theory of Change for more details), 
all in the context of a very low operating margin business model. Robust solutions are required to address these issues 
before the EV industry can fulfil its role as a significant contributor to India’s climate change mitigation story. 
 
B.2 (a). Theory of change narrative and diagram (max. 1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagram)

43 Mobilising Finance for EVs in India: A Toolkit of Solutions to Mitigate Risks and Address Market Barriers, Niti Aayog and Rocky Mountain 
Institute, January 2021 PP 11. 
44 Mobilising Finance for EVs in India: A Toolkit of Solutions to Mitigate Risks and Address Market Barriers, Niti Aayog and Rocky Mountain 
Institute, January 2021 PP 1. 
45 On 19 Feb 2016, the Ministry of Road Transport and Highway issued a draft notification of Bharat Stage (BS) VI emission standards, 
equivalent to Euro VI. The standards, as proposed, will take effect throughout the country for all light-duty and heavy-duty vehicles as well 
as two and three-wheelers manufactured on or after 1 Apr 2020. The BS VI standard specifies 10 ppm sulphur for gasoline and diesel fuels. 
The Programme intends to support the reduction of greenhouse gas emissions in the transport sector in India, aligned 
to GCF’s results management framework. This is expected to be achieved by providing enhanced and innovative 
financing (leasing and other financing products) across the e-mobility value chain, which will directly or indirectly 
overcome the current barriers to increased electrification of vehicles in India.  
Barriers 
There is a need to address the various challenges that have limited the e-mobility sector’s growth potential and 
prevented it from becoming a mainstream sector. Some of the key factors affecting the EV adoption are:  
1. Availability of financing:43 Improved access to attractive financing products will be a key growth driver for EV 
demand. High financing costs and uncertainty around long-term economics, including residual value, remain both 
real and perceived issues for financial investments. Risks associated with the nascency of the electric mobility 
ecosystem have given rise to investment barriers, such as high interest and insurance rates, short tenure, low loan-
to-value ratios, and requirements for high collateral value or corporate guarantees, all limiting retail / corporate 
customer interest. These issues are most pronounced for electric buses and four wheelers, and charging 
infrastructure, all of which have longer gestation periods and high per unit capex involvement. The envisaged 
Programme will seek to provide bespoke financing products in the target segments across the value chain to 
overcome these barriers.  
The investments made by the Platform (activity II below) will provide new financing options, improving the 
availability of, and risks assumed by, financing.  
 
2. Upfront cost: The high upfront cost of EVs is slowing down its adoption rate, despite lower running and maintenance 
costs associated with EVs vis-à-vis ICE vehicles. Demand for more affordable EV products is also expected, as 
consumers and businesses reduce spending in the short term due to COVID-19. This potential shift in consumer 
preferences may affect manufacturers’ investment and production decisions,44 which may slow the entire EV value 
chain evolution in India. Providing attractive financing solutions for better adoption by lowering the upfront capital 
outlay is required to accelerate growth of the sector, which is in its nascency in India. Furthermore, increased 
investment in the sector is expected to support a greater flow of capital into research and development, which 
should lower the cost of new EVs over time.  
The financing provided by the Platform (activity II below) is intended to target a substantial reduction in the 
upfront cost paid by the customers of EVs. The investments in charging infrastructure section (activity II) 
will increase India’s capacity to support more growth in EVs. Similarly, the increased EVs financed by the 
Platform will support the development of charging infrastructure, by the Platform or otherwise. 
 
3. Infrastructure buildout: Charging infrastructure is the backbone of electric mobility, but is also one of the key 
perceived barriers to EV adoption in India given its limited availability and long charging times. High operating costs, 
uncertain utilisation rates and unproven business models are some of the key challenges holding back existing and 
new EV charging stations operators from expanding their current presence. However, as technology develops, EV 
adoption continues, and financing becomes available, electricity distribution companies (DISCOMs), charging 
service providers, and other stakeholders can focus on building robust charging infrastructure networks. The 
Platform will seek to work with these stakeholders to finance charging infrastructure projects to propel overall EV 
adoption.  
 
4. Manufacturing and supply: EV battery manufacturing in India is still largely dependent on imports due to a lack of 
domestic supply of lithium, nickel and cobalt, the key raw materials and major cost drivers of EV batteries. Domestic 
manufacturing of advanced batteries and cells, electric motors, and other EV components are required to reduce 
the high cost of importing these critical components and equipment from international markets. OEM capital has 
recently focused on the migration of ICE vehicles to Bharat Stage VI (BSVI) standards,45 whilst the industry is 
experiencing lower sales due to COVID-19. This is hampering supply-side investment in EVs. The Patform’s 
investment in the EV space will provide OEMs / operators the necessary liquidity to recycle their capital and invest 
in enhancing their manufacturing and research and development (R&D) capabilities. The Platform will encourage 
this to be done in accordance with latest Environmental, Social and Governance (ESG) standards.

=== PAGE 14 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 10 
 
 
Establishment of the Platform and the financing provided by it, is expected to increase the penetration of 
EVs, support local manufacturing of vehicles in an inclusive and sustainable manner, as well as reduce 
upfront capital commitment from OEMs to invest further into R&D in the e-mobility space.  
 
5. Investor risk appetite and availability of investment opportunities: Increased investment across the e-mobility value 
chain is expected to support market development, grow existing business models, and encourage additional 
research and development. Without this investment, the barriers outlined above are expected to persist. Investment 
into the e-mobility sector in India has not yet achieved scale due to high perceived and real investment risks, and a 
limited number of investment opportunities that meet the risk appetite of large investors. Despite the increase in the 
quantum of investment over last few months, the aggregate investment amount is minimal compared to the potential 
size of the Indian EV market. Risk-taking capital is required to support commercial investors to invest and develop 
the sector to the point where there is increased commercial and maintenance risks appetite for the sector.  
Establishment of the Platform (activity I) provides an investment opportunity for commercial investors to 
invest in the e-mobility sector in India. Further investment and development of the sector through other 
activities will also help to reduce investment risks (real and perceived) associated with the sector.  
 
Activities and Outputs 
The activities of the Platform, as shown in the diagram below, will immediately and permanently increase the flow of 
private sector investment into the EV sector, assisting to overcome the barriers outlined above and enable delivery of 
the intended programme results and desired outcomes. 
1. Platform Establishment and Fundraising: The E-mobility Financing Programme will facilitate investment from GCF 
to the end beneficiaries whilst also crowding-in investment from other investors. MAM will use its vast and 
experienced capital raising capabilities to attract funds from commercial investors alongside GCF, which will be 
used to capitalise a “first of its kind” e-mobility focused financing platform in India. This platform will adopt best in 
class risk management, ESG, Diversity and Inclusion and IT and HR policies, along with other industry leading 
asset management frameworks. GCF’s capital will act as a risk-mitigating buffer providing downside protection to 
commercial investors. This is expected to help meet the risk-return expectations of commercial investors, 
consequently resulting in increased interest and higher availability of capital for the e-mobility sector.  
 
Relevant Output 
Description 
I. Commercial capital 
crowded-in 
• 
By establishing the Platform, investors will have an avenue to invest in the 
e-mobility sector in India which meets their risk / return requirements. 
 
2. Platform Investments, Monitoring and Exit: The Platform will provide operationally integrated financing products to 
overcome the barriers outlined above, either through scaling-up access to existing products, such as vehicle and 
project financing, or developing bespoke structures, such as operating and finance leases in the EV domain, to 
meet the needs of market participants. Financing will be provided across the e-mobility sector which will support an 
increased number of EVs, charging stations and other associated infrastructure across the e-mobility value chain. 
With the Platform being a MAM-managed vehicle, it will adopt best in class investment monitoring, workplace health 
and safety, environmental and social governance and risk management practices, hire an experienced and 
professional management team and leverage MAM’s industry leading standards for portfolio companies to deliver 
the desired results for investors. Once proof of concept is established using equity from GCF and commercial 
sources, it is anticipated that further capital will be mobilised from local and international debt markets, with the 
potential to raise further equity capital to enable continued growth.

46 Please note that the Platform will encourage the potential counterparties to use renewable energy as the primary source of energy to power 
the EV charging stations. To incentivise the use of renewable energy, Platform may provide certain incentives to potential counterparties if 
renewable energy use related targets are met. Irrespective of this, India’s electricity grid is getting increasingly decarbonised. Over the last 
5 years, the contribution of renewable energy in the overall energy mix has increased from 50 GW in January 2017 (15.9% of total capacity 
installed) [Source: CEA Monthly Installation Report, January 2017] to 105 GW in December 2021 (26.6% of total capacity installed) [Source: 
CEA Monthly Installation Report, December 2021], an average increase of ~10.8 GW per annum. Further, the central government has set 
a target to achieve 500 GW of renewable energy by 2030 (50% of all its energy requirements through renewable energy sources), as it 
looks to achieve its commitments under Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs) as per the Paris Agreement and meet its COP26 
pledges. This robust government focus on decarbonisation of India’s power sector will help to reduce the overall level of GHG emissions 
by the country and assist the Program in achieving part of its additionality targets.

3. Wind up of corporate structure: From an exit point of view, as outlined in section B.6, any partial or full exit from the 
Platform will be possible only when the EV financing market in India has or is becoming mature and offers high 
growth potential. At the time of exit, depending upon the market conditions, past performance and future growth 
estimates, the Platform is expected to attract significant interest from strategic and financial investors. Once either 
the SG Master Co. sells its stake in the SG Co, or the SG Co sells its stake in the Platform, within the exit period  
to a new investor, the exit proceeds received will be distributed to the investors, depending upon the cashflow 
distribution waterfall as provided in the Term Sheet. However, the winding up of the structure may take time, 
depending upon the nature of indemnities and warranties that may be offered to the buyer at the time of sale. 
 
Relevant Output 
Description 
I. Commercial capital 
crowded-in 
• 
The sale of the Platform is likely to attract new or existing market players 
who are looking to expand their investment in the e-mobility space.  
 
Outcomes 
This Programme is focused on crowding-in private sector capital to support the scaling-up of investment in e-mobility 
sector across India. There are two programme level outcomes: 
• 
Enabling environment for EV market established: Through the demonstration of innovative financing products, 
increased EVs on the road, and more charging infrastructure, it is expected that there will be an enabling 
environment for EVs to accelerate further, including an increased number of participants, new financial 
products, greater infrastructure, and developed domestic manufacturing and supply capabilities. 
• 
Increased penetration of electric vehicles in India: This is the fundamental outcome sought as a result of all 
the activities. This will be achieved both directly through investments made by the Platform (increasing number 
of e-vehicles and charging stations) but also indirectly through crowding-in commercial capital that is expected 
to expand operations and make independent investments (not linked to the Platform) which in turns will also 
increase the penetration of e-vehicles.  
 
Co-benefits 
 
• 
Decreased pollution: As outlined in section D.3 (co-benefits), greater penetration of EVs is expected to have a 
significant impact on air quality and related health benefits due to lower pollution in Indian cities. 
• 
Direct job creation: There is expected to be significant growth in the EV sector and a corresponding increase 
in financing solutions for such EVs. The Platform will create new jobs as part of this broader sectoral growth. 
Ultimately these outcomes contribute to achieving the GCF Results Area of reducing emissions through increased 
access to low-emission transport.

B.3. Project/programme description (max. 2500 words, approximately 5 pages) 
The Indian E-Mobility Financing Programme’s aim is to accelerate decarbonisation of the Indian economy by focusing 
on sustainable and electrified road transport, enabled through faster adoption of electric vehicles and growth of 
associated infrastructure in the country. 
The main objectives of the Programme are: 
1. to provide suitable financial solutions (for example operationally integrated, performance guaranteed leases or 
structured credit instruments) to electric vehicle owners and operators; 
2. to provide tailored financial solutions to ancillary areas to further develop the electric vehicle ecosystem, such 
as charging infrastructure and batteries; 
3. using financial solutions to mitigate current user concerns, including high upfront costs, residual value risk and 
reliability; 
4. to facilitate development of EV ecosystem through investments, partnerships, and capability development of 
market participants; 
5. to set a high benchmark and continually improve the efficiency of services in the Indian electric vehicle domain 
via improvement of systems, processes, and resources; 
6. to provide competitively priced solutions that enhances competitiveness of EVs in terms of long-term cost of 
ownership vis-à-vis ICE vehicles, aligned with the Indian government’s policy; and 
7. to mobilise significant amount of nascent institutional capital into electrification of India’s road transport sector.

=== PAGE 18 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 14 
 
 
 
47 Securing NBFC license from RBI is an iterative and collaborative process, which needs engagement with RBI. MAM will work with its 
advisors to prepare the right information package, which would include details about the shareholder, capitalization information and 
background on the management team, among other information, which puts it in the best position to secure this license. The process to 
secure NBFC license from the central bank will be initiated only after signing of the FAA. As per advise provided to MAM, the timelines to 
get this approval varies between 6 to 18 months subject to RBI requirements and internal processes 
The Programme is uniquely designed to offer ‘first of its kind’ financing solutions in the Indian e-mobility sector. The 
Programme is expected to create a wide impact in this sector by addressing the key gap hindering the uptake of electric 
vehicles viz. access to scalable and competitive financing solutions. The Programme will provide financing products 
and solutions at competitive rates to multiple domestic businesses and entrepreneurs, facilitating healthy competition 
in the space to further drive down costs and enabling “crowding-in” of commercial capital. 
The Programme is designed with a blueprint for success followed in other nascent technologies at early stages in their 
evolution. For example, in the renewable energy space, formation of Indian Renewable Energy Development Agency 
(IREDA) as the pioneer for financing and promoting self-sustaining renewable energy has vastly contributed to the high 
growth and mature renewable energy market that operates in India today. 
The Programme is also in line with India’s NDCs as well as policy initiatives as outlined in section B.1. 
Part A – Programme description 
Legal Form 
Under the programme, a dedicated E-Mobility Financing Platform will be setup in India, that 
will be managed by Macquarie Asset Management (MAM) (more details provided below). 
This Platform (refer to section B.4 – Implementation Arrangements for more details on the 
proposed corporate structure) will adhere to all local licensing and regulatory requirements, 
including being registered as a Non-Banking Financial Company (NBFC) with India’s central 
bank, the Reserve Bank of India (RBI). A NBFC license will be essential for the Platform to 
provide leasing and other credit solutions in the Indian market to both business and retail 
customers.47 
Currency 
While the investments from GCF and commercial investors will be in USD at the Limited 
Partner (LP) level (refer to section B.4 – Implementation Arrangements), financing provided 
by the Platform to counterparties in India will be in Indian Rupees (INR). 
Target Initial 
Equity Capital 
Commitment 
(Proof of 
Concept stage) 
USD 405 million, including USD 200 million of ‘first loss’ capital from GCF and USD 205 
million from commercial investors.  
The first loss coverage of GCF will be up to its share of capital commitment for all commercial 
investors coming with preferential shares across the proposed structure (refer to section B.4 
– Implementation Arrangement for more details). Further, any capital commitment by 
commercial investors across the chain will be counted as part of their target commitment of 
USD 205 million. Given the ‘market making’ nature of the program, GCF will invest a 
minimum of USD 50 million on the FAA effective date (First Close).  
These funds will be used to capitalise the above legal entity and enable its initial formation 
and growth as a proof of concept. If this is done successfully, it is envisaged that the next 
phase of growth will be funded by capital raises of ~USD 1.1 billion from local and 
international debt markets.  
The current business plan  does not incorporate additional equity injections from new 
investors during the implementation period. However, if the Platform successfully achieves 
its base case business plan, it is anticipated that additional equity will be secured from 
commercial investors to enable continued corporate growth. Determination in this regard will 
be taken later depending upon market conditions and future growth prospects.

Period

and, in reality, it will be driven by actual traction on the ground (the actual timelines may be

faster or slower). GCF will need to set aside the entire committed amount from the beginning

Implementation 
Period 
Implementation period will be 10 years from FAA effective date. It can be further extended

Platform’s 
guiding 
principles 
The Platform will be driven by a few guiding principles as below: 
1. Build a business that will provide “customer-centric” financing solutions to e-mobility 
companies in India. The Platform will build unique capabilities and partnerships that 
will underwrite the technology risks associated with electric vehicles and develop the 
ability to own and redeploy assets in the market if required.  
2. Focus primarily on the Business to Business (B2B) space. This will allow the 
Platform to address the key problem in the sector, which is access to sustainable 
and scalable capital for growing businesses. However, the Platform may 
strategically expand its coverage to the retail customer segment if there is a strategic 
requirement and requisite market conditions.  
3. In the B2B space, the Programme’s focus will be on the entire electric mobility value 
chain. However, given the nascency of the industry, few key sectors within the 
overall value chain, such as electric buses, shared 4W fleet and electric charging 
infrastructure, are expected to act as the anchor for the Programme. Other segments 
may include light and medium commercial vehicles (LCVs and MCVs), commercial 
2W and 3W fleet, old vehicle scrappage, battery recycling, or others. Additionally, 
the Platform will have flexibility to re-orient its financing focus depending on the 
emerging requirements and its ability to drive high impact.  
4. The Platform will seek to develop long-term partnerships with a few large OEMs / 
sponsors / operators who are actively present in the e-mobility space. It will work 
with them to promote strong Work, Health, Safety and Environment (WHSE) 
practices and focus the portfolio deployment in partnership with these large 
organisations, whilst seeking to create alignment around vehicle reliability and 
residual value. 
Target Sub-
Sectors 
As mentioned above, while the mandate of the Platform will be the entire e-mobility value 
chain in India, there are a few key sub-sectors that will be of higher focus initially. These 
include electric buses, shared 4W fleet and electric charging infrastructure. These sectors 
all have a substantial growth potential with an estimated USD 50 billion of financing required 
by 2030.48 
A. Electric Buses  
Electric buses are capital intensive, with a concentrated OEM base and a fragmented 
operator base. The Platform will focus on this space and partner with a few key large OEMs 
actively seeking to migrate to electric buses. The Platform will provide innovative financing 
solutions to the key players targeting the intra-city, government led auctions as well as to 
key players operating in the private, inter-city bus space. The financing solutions will include 
products such as leasing, project financing, receivables financing, and other innovative 
solutions that best help addresses the market need. The Platform will target all segments of 
bus deployment including intra-city public transport, inter-city transportation, school busses, 
office and other commercial applications.  
B. Shared / Corporate 4W Fleet  
Migration to electric vehicles is a key theme for companies that provide mobility as a service 
to private corporates. However, this space faces key challenges of limited financing 
availability from banks due to a lack of suitable business models and / or the inability to 
achieve the utilisation thresholds required to run a sustainable business. 
This sector offers the investment themes preferred by the Platform of a key financing and 
viability gap, as well as of working with a few large players and concentrating the portfolio to 
a limited number of key relationships. The Platform will work towards enabling migration of

=== PAGE 20 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 16 
 
 
shared fleet service providers to electric vehicles across buses and other 4W light 
commercial vehicles.  
 
C. Electric Charging Infrastructure  
To achieve the desired electrification targets for vehicles, charging infrastructure (public and 
private) needs to be rapidly scaled up from current levels.  
This space is currently plagued by supply chain issues, policy uncertainty and limited 
financing, coupled with high upfront costs. The Platform will seek to alleviate a major 
bottleneck in growth of e-mobility space in India by making financing solutions available for 
aggressive and pro-active deployment of charging infrastructure as a shared service for 
multiple owners / operators of electric vehicles. Further, to enhance the additionality aspect 
of the program, the Platform will both encourage and incentivise electric charging station 
operators through appropriate financial incentives (to be decided by the Platform 
management) to directly source renewable energy to meet the power requirement of EV 
charging stations.  
The balance of the portfolio will comprise other segments such as OEM or vendor supply 
chains, vehicle scrappage, battery swapping, commercial 2W and 3Ws, or others. Over time, 
the Platform may also expand into the B2C / retail segment. Note that funding of hybrid 
vehicles will be out of Programme’s scope of target sub-sectors. Moreover, the Platform will 
continuously look at realigning its business focus as the e-mobility sector evolves and new 
business opportunities emerge in this space. 
Leasing and 
Financing 
Solutions  
The leasing and financing solutions to be provided by the Platform will be based on the 
requirements of its customers as no one solution can cater to, and address all the needs of, 
the counterparties in the sector. By offering leasing and / or credit solutions (noting that no 
equity investments will be made by the Platform), the Platform will be able to support a larger 
number of counterparties within the same sector without giving rise to any conflict-of-interest 
issues. Some of the main financing products to be provided by the Platform: 
1. Leasing (Operating and Finance leases): The Platform’s strong preference will be to 
provide leasing solutions to its customers. Most of the financial institutions in India 
have focused on providing credit offerings rather than leasing solutions which 
require developing strong underlying product and technology capabilities. The lack 
of depth of leasing offerings in India force the operators / vehicle owners to invest 
substantial upfront capital as part of overall capex. The problem is even more acute 
in case of electric vehicles, where the upfront capex requirement can be much higher 
than the ICE vehicles. This leasing offering will be most pertinent for customer 
segments like e-busses (private counterparties), 2/3/4W fleets, LCVs and MCVs. 
We expect that, as the Indian e-mobility market matures, the Platform will emerge 
as one of the leading leasing companies in India with end-to-end capabilities on 
asset sourcing, asset deployment / financing, asset maintenance and redeployment 
at the end of the leasing tenure, which also addresses a key barrier of residual asset 
value.  
 
2. Project and vehicle financing (and other relevant credit solutions including Revenue 
Discounting): Project and vehicle financing solutions generally have shorter tenures 
and have higher risk premium as the asset ownership continues to remain with the 
borrower. However, there are additional accounting benefits (such as depreciation 
and interest) that can be valued by the borrowers. 
 
The project and vehicle financing markets for traditional vehicles are highly competitive due 
to the presence of large number of banks / NBFCs and other financial institutions. However, 
Indian financing markets do not currently have any scalable offering for electric vehicles and 
so, depending upon customer’s requirement, the Platform will look to utilise its strong 
sectoral understanding and experience to offer a tailored solution for EVs that is beneficial

Irrespective of the type of financing solution, the key element for any financial product offered 
by the Platform is GCF’s first loss capital, which will allow the Platform to offer the necessary 
product for the higher upfront cost at a lower financing rate and assume right residual value 
risk. This will allow the provision of electric vehicles at competitive cost to ICE alternatives 
in order to scale up the sector. On the back of this structure, the Platform can attract other 
commercial capital that would otherwise be hesitant to invest in this nascent sector. By 
raising a large amount of capital, the Platform can deploy funds across financing solutions 
at scale, thereby driving down costs of electric vehicles in India and building a vibrant 
ecosystem across the value chain. 
Select 
Financing 
Criteria 
Financing solutions provided by the Platform will be subject to various financing criteria, such 
as funding size per counterparty account, single counterparty limit, credit quality, coupon 
type, repayment terms, etc.  
Macquarie 
Asset 
Management 
(MAM) 
Overview 
The Platform as indicated in the structure in section B.4 will be managed by offshore and 
onshore management companies that are owned and controlled by MAM, the asset 
management division of Macquarie Group. MAM has a significant track record in managing 
investments, with >140 portfolio companies across its managed funds globally.49  
MAM overview 
1. MAM’s expertise in asset management: MAM is a global specialist asset manager 
with AUD 735.5 billion of assets under management (AUM),50 aiming to deliver 
positive outcomes to everyone. MAM provides investment solutions to clients across 
a range of capabilities, including infrastructure and renewables, real estate, 
agriculture and natural assets, assets finance, private credit, fixed income, and multi-
asset solutions.  
Macquarie Group is a diversified financial services company headquartered in 
Sydney and listed on the Australian Securities Exchange (ASX), with a market 
capitalisation of AUD 74.4 billion.51 Macquarie Group provides asset management 
and finance, banking, advisory and risk, and capital solutions across debt, equity, 
and commodities. 
Through its infrastructure focused private equity arm within the Private Markets 
division, MAM is the world’s largest infrastructure asset manager52 with equity under 
management (EUM) of AUD 160.1 billion,53 receiving commitments from pension 
funds, insurance companies, and sovereign wealth funds for its products and 
broader platform. 
 
2. MAM’s expertise in India: MAM has been present in India since 2009 when it 
established a ~USD 1 billion infrastructure focused fund in partnership with State 
Bank of India (SBI), India’s largest bank.54 Today, MAM has invested across multiple 
assets in roads, power, telecom towers and airports sectors in India. It is led by a 
team of highly experienced professionals who have robust record of establishing 
new businesses in the country. For example, in March 2018, MAM won the first 
auction of toll roads under the Toll Operate Transfer (TOT) model from National 
Highway Authority of India (NHAI) wherein it was awarded the concession to operate 
687 kms of toll roads over a period of 30 years at a cost of ~USD 1.5 billion.55  
Macquarie Group’s track record in e-mobility sector 
3. Expertise in e-mobility sector: Since 2018, Macquarie Group has been active in the 
e-mobility sector undertaking both debt and equity positions in some of the leading 
e-mobility companies across the world.

MAM plans to leverage the above expertise and experience across its various 
business divisions to establish a high quality e-mobility financing platform. 
 
Macquarie Group’s track record in leasing platforms 
4. Expertise in building and exiting leasing platforms: Since 2006, Macquarie Group 
has successfully built multiple leasing platforms across sectors such as aircraft, 
helicopters, engines, rolling stock, passenger vehicles, computer equipment and 
utility meters. 
 
Platform 
Management 
MAM will hire an experienced management team to manage the Platform once it has been 
established post GCF’s Board approvals. 
Being a MAM managed vehicle, the Platform will also be able to leverage Macquarie’s global 
network, expertise, and brand value across the world, thereby increasing its visibility and 
ability to generate inbound approaches from market participants. 
Governance 
MAM’s main responsibility will be to approve new financing opportunities, have robust 
oversight over the Platform’s performance and drive innovative, leading capital sourcing and 
management solutions, including exit at an appropriate time. Through the Accredited Entity 
(AE), it will apply its fiduciary duties as per GCF’s AMA and the best practice standards it 
applies across its global operations. It will report on the Platform’s performance on a 
quarterly and annual basis. The audit of annual financial statements will be performed by an 
independent auditor, to ensure transparency and control of flow of funds. Impact reporting 
will be performed annually, either in-house or by a third party consultant, with the two GCF 
specific programme independent evaluations (mid-term and final) to be undertaken by the 
AE and shared with GCF. ESG will be carried out as per the Environmental and Social (E&S) 
Document (refer to Annex 6 – Environment and Social Management System) and IFC 
Performance Standards. Further, the Platform will be subject to MAM’s best in class asset 
management practices, which provide guidance on Governance, Money Laundering, Bribery 
and Corruption, Diversity and Inclusion, Cyber-security, etc. related aspects. 
Economics 
The Platform will target investment in opportunities that balance the long-term risk adjusted 
returns for investors with market development and innovative financial solutions for 
counterparties, aiding the accelerated growth of the Indian e-mobility sector. 
Distribution of proceeds to investors will be in accordance with the distribution waterfall as 
detailed in the term sheet provided to GCF.   
 
Part B – Key activities of the Program 
As mentioned in section E.6, the Programme will have 3 key phases: 
1. Activity Set I – Platform Establishment and Fundraising 
2. Activity Set II – Platform Investments, Monitoring and Exit 
3. Activity Set III – Wind up of corporate structure 
 
Activity Set I – Platform Establishment and Fundraising 
This primarily includes the establishment of entities / structure in Singapore and India for efficient inflow / outflow of 
capital to establish India’s ‘first of its kind’ ‘E-mobility Financing Platform’. Some of the key activities include: 
1. setup of LP – GP structure (refer to section B.4 – Implementation Arrangements for more details) for the transfer 
of GCF capital into the e-mobility programme through the Accredited Entity; 
2. establishment of structure and entities in India, with necessary regulatory and legal approvals, to start providing 
financing solutions in the e-mobility space in India;

=== PAGE 23 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 19 
 
 
3. crowding-in of commercial investors, including fundraising efforts and legal execution of their investments, 
either into LP or SG Master Co. or SG Co. or the Platform; 
4. ensuring compliance of the entire structure with all applicable local rules and regulations; 
5. setup of systems and processes across the structure, which includes adoption of risk management, HR, 
cybersecurity, third party vendor management, E&S and Diversity and Inclusion (D&I) frameworks and policies; 
and 
6. hiring and appointing the management team and the Board of Directors (the Board) at various levels, to ensure 
proper functioning of each entity. 
 
Activity Set II – Platform Investments, Monitoring and Exit  
This primarily includes screening and evaluating transactions and counterparties, application of best business 
practices, WHS, gender and environmental standards, diligent monitoring, establishing innovative financial and capital 
management solutions and, ultimately, exit on behalf of investors from the Platform.  
Financing Process 
 
 
The Platform will implement its strategy through a well-defined policy of identifying, evaluating, investing, managing, 
and realising the financing opportunities (where applicable).  
 
Key investment and financing decisions of the Platform will be approved by SG Co. (as shareholder) with guidance 
from the Singapore Manager. This will be supported by: 
1. day-to-day management of the Platform by the experienced professionals employed by the Platform; and 
2. strategic support and oversight to the Platform by the Singapore Manager in consultation with Macquarie India 
Advisory Company (IAC). 
 
Throughout each stage of the financing process, risk assessment will be a key focus for every financing solution 
provided by the Platform. This risk focused approach will begin at the earliest possible stage, even before transaction 
screening and will continue throughout the life of each financial solution. 
 
 
 
 
A. 
Opportunity Sourcing 
 
Access to deal flow will be one of the key factors in the success of the program. Access to a wide range of potential 
opportunities will enable the Platform to assemble an appropriately diversified portfolio. The Platform expects 
opportunities to arise from several sources, including:

=== PAGE 24 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 20 
 
 
Management team  
Management team members will have many years of work experience in 
infrastructure, vehicle financing and leasing / credit sectors across the world. They 
will therefore have long-standing relationships with sponsors, operators, banks, and 
intermediaries who will provide a regular flow of opportunities.  
Macquarie relationships 
Macquarie is a leading infrastructure advisor and investor with global coverage. It 
holds significant roles on a number of globally relevant forums, such as the Climate 
Finance Leadership Initiative and the Global Committee for Adaptation. This will 
bring opportunities and provide personal introductions for the Platform to 
prospective financing counterparties.  
Strategic partnerships 
Macquarie has unparalleled group-level relationships with a range of infrastructure 
sponsors, operators and banks, all of which will enhance Platform’s access to deal 
flow.  
MAM has developed strategic partnerships with several domestic and international 
financial institutions (banks and NBFCs) through its existing portfolio of 
infrastructure assets in India. It also has close relationships with key individuals 
within the e-mobility, automotive and energy sectors, which will enable detailed 
discussions that are important for originating and refinancing transactions. 
 
Further, these opportunities are expected to arise through circumstances which may include companies:  
• 
bidding for new central / state government tenders; 
• 
partnering due to the large amounts of capital required to construct or manufacture the assets; 
• 
looking to recycle capital to expand the existing business in the sector; or 
• 
restructuring or repositioning their business activities. 
 
In certain cases, due to MAM’s position and relationships in the market, the Platform may have the ability to finance 
assets / counterparties on an exclusive basis. 
MAM executives and the Platform management team will develop / have strong relationships with many of the key 
stakeholders in the sector, including corporate sponsors (such as utilities and suppliers), professional advisors and 
lenders. These relationships are important, not only for deal origination, but also execution, on-going management, and 
exit. 
B. 
Opportunity Screening 
 
After becoming aware of a financing opportunity, the Platform will apply its Opportunity Screening process to test the 
characteristics of the opportunity against the Financing Selection Criteria of the Platform.  
The Platform’s investment screening process will typically include assessment of: 
• 
the financing thesis and alignment to Financing Selection Criteria; 
• 
the creditworthiness, operational capability and ESG performance of the counterparty (lessee / borrower), with 
particular regard to their financial position, expected outlook, and sensitivity to external factors; 
• 
the capability of existing management of the lessee / borrower, including recent performance, expertise, 
experience, and incentives to perform; 
• 
the revenue model of the business (including whether underpinned by contracts or regulation), the stability of 
the cashflows of the business and possible factors influencing future revenue and cashflows; 
• 
an initial assessment of the environmental and social categorisation of transactions (refer to section G.1 – 
Environment and social risk assessment for more details); 
• 
a high level overview of expected terms to be provided under the financing; and 
• 
an initial assessment of GHG emissions reduction, ESG risks and other investment risks. 
 
Based on the Financing Selection Criteria and in accordance with delegation of authority within the Platform (to be 
established once the Platform is operational), the Platform management team will negotiate the term sheet with the 
counterparty. Once there is broad alignment on the commercials and additional analysis has been undertaken by the

=== PAGE 25 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 21 
 
 
Platform management team, a detailed discussion paper will be presented to the Board of the Platform (or an 
empowered team as mandated by the Board) for approval and advice. The Platform management will also regularly 
seek advise from Macquarie’s IAC and necessary approvals from the Singapore Manager as required.  
 
C. 
Due Diligence (DD) 
 
The management team will engage with the counterparty and undertake, in conjunction with specialist experts and 
advisers, due diligence as required to ensure all aspects of the transaction are sufficiently reviewed. The management 
team will also take an active role in the negotiation and setting of lease or loan terms and conditions as reflected in the 
documentation.  
Key risk considerations will typically include:  
Due Diligence (DD) Items 
Description 
Enhanced Counterparty / 
Promoter DD 
Assess Counterparty / Promoter’s financial strength, reputation, ESG 
performance and track record of honouring contractual commitments. 
Financial Modelling 
Counterparties typically provide a financial model which can be used for future 
cash flow and financial statement analysis. The financial model will be 
stressed for several different scenarios based on the management team’s 
view of key drivers, with input from Platform’s professional advisors if 
required.  
Financial DD 
Tax, accounting, and financial statement diligence as required to ensure 
financial robustness. 
Technical DD 
Third party consultants and other expert reports (either undertaken in-house 
or provided by the counterparty) providing details around technical aspects of 
the asset, including whether technology is commercially proven, 
environmental exposures, asset condition, construction risk (including network 
expansion), operations and maintenance complexity and experience, 
contracts, and performance damages and ESG. 
Legal DD 
Dependent on the nature of a transaction and contract. Typically, would 
include diligence on key entities, contracts, regulatory regime, security, and 
enforcement analysis.  
Additionally, legal advisers / in-house experts will draft robust leasing / loan 
documents that ensure strong enforceability of contracts, preserve the rights 
of the lessor / creditor in the event of asset deterioration and provide an 
appropriate redressal mechanism. It is possible that these markets may form 
the basis of market standards in the future. 
Insurance DD 
Summary of insurances in place and adequacy of the insurances 
(construction risk, business interruption, operational risk, third party liability 
insurances). Often the lease / credit agreement will specify a required level of 
cover.  
Commercial or Market DD 
Economic and market reports (comprising of industry structure, barriers to 
entry, product substitution and certainty of supply). Only applicable for first 
time financing in a segment where there is an underlying demand risk, tariff 
risk or where there is a significant cost component that may be subject to 
market dynamics. 
 
Note that the above list of due diligence items may not be applicable for all transactions. Any decision in relation to the 
due diligence process (and appointment of relevant third party advisers) will be undertaken by the management team 
in consultation with Macquarie India Advisory Company and the Singapore Manager.

=== PAGE 26 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 22 
 
 
 
D. 
Financing Decision and Execution 
 
The results of the due diligence process will be set out in a financing paper and presented to an empowered team 
within the Platform (as per the delegation of authority) and the Singapore Manager (for specific cases based on size of 
the transaction) for guidance and decision regarding the financing. Once a decision is made and financing is approved, 
the management team and its legal advisers will work with the counterparty to negotiate and agree the leasing / loan 
documents. These documents will detail out the various rights of the lessor / lender, lessee / borrower’s reporting 
requirements and clearly layout the redressal mechanism in case of any default or conflict.  
 
E. 
Ongoing monitoring and management 
 
The Platform will take a proactive approach to asset management by following a structured framework as approved by 
the Platform Board, with guidance from the Singapore Manager and Macquarie India Advisory Company, which protects 
the best interests of the investors and ensures that Platform is protected against any risk (including fiduciary or 
reputational risks). All of Platform’s financing solutions will be continually monitored and maintained. The general 
principles of the finance and impact monitoring process are:  
• 
The Platform’s management team will take the primary responsibility for monitoring performance of the 
financing instrument and the impact created by it. The team will be responsible for tracking both financial and 
impact performance of the instrument and will update the Board of the Platform and the Singapore Manager 
on a quarterly basis on lessee / borrower’s financial and non-financial covenants. 
• 
Monitoring by the management team will be dynamic (following general relevant market data via information 
subscription services), and periodic (prompted by ongoing proactive engagement with the lessee or borrower 
as well as the lessee / borrower’s reporting obligations) including internal quarterly monitoring meetings and 
reporting. 
• 
Material changes to the lessee / borrower’s business or credit quality, and the impact created by it, will be 
discussed internally and escalated to the Singapore Manager where it will be determined whether it is 
sufficiently material or not.  
• 
Requests for waivers or amendments to facility terms, if any, will be reviewed and approved post detailed 
discussions with the Singapore Manager as and when required. Further, at the quarterly meeting, the 
Singapore Manager will be notified regarding all waivers and amendments granted in the previous period. 
 
Further, as part of the monitoring process, the Platform will utilise a heightened review process called Watchlist. If a 
management team member sees evidence of potential stress, business practice concerns or other factors that could 
have a material impact on the financing solution provided to the counterparty, then it will decide whether to recommend 
that lease or loan be placed on Watchlist.  
The counterparty will be placed on Watchlist when it is deemed, they are, or are expected to come, under stress.  
The Watchlist process described above will seek to ensure heightened scrutiny and early action in the case of 
counterparties that are at high risk of a material change in credit profile or loan / lease restructure. In an event of default, 
the Platform will take the necessary action, which is likely to result in the best return to the investors. These actions 
could include managing the financing instrument through the work out process, asset repossession or sale of the 
instrument. 
F. 
Realisation and exit 
 
During the term of the Platform, the Singapore Manager will consider appropriate strategies to provide liquidity from 
the Platform which achieves an exit, provides liquid return to the investors and generates capital for re-investment (refer 
to section B.6 – Exit and Sustainability for more details). Within its Investment Period, the Platform will seek to create 
a sustained, positive impact through its robust deal sourcing, execution and monitoring capabilities, focus on 
sustainability and good corporate citizenship and high performing product portfolio, all of which is anticipated to be 
attractive to a future investor. The Platform is expected continue to make a positive impact on the Indian road transport 
sector, even after the exit of the initial set of investors in case the potential buyer(s) continue the mandate in the future.

B.4. Implementation arrangements (max. 1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagrams) 
Platform structure, governance, and flow of funds56 
 
Proposed Structure and Governance 
An offshore pooling vehicle (LP) is proposed to be set up in Singapore to aggregate funds from GCF and commercial 
investors via a Limited Partnership Agreement (LPA). This Singapore LP will set up a Singapore private limited 
company (SG Master Co.) which will, in turn, establish another wholly owned Singapore private limited company (SG 
Co.) which will invest in the Platform in India by way of equity, equity like, hybrid securities, debt instruments and other 
similar instruments57 from shareholders for efficient capital structuring.58 
Proposed Structure 
 
 
Accredited Entity 
GCF will enter into a Funded Activity Agreement (FAA) with MAAML, the AE, which will detail the key implementation 
arrangements, commercial terms, and legal obligations, including mechanics to pass down GCF policies and 
requirements to the offshore entities in Singapore and onshore entities in India. These key terms are summarised under 
the Term Sheet. The AE will transfer the funds to the LP on behalf of GCF, as the investor of record. 
The AE will oversee the implementation of the Programme on behalf of GCF. It will ensure that: 
1. all GCF’s policies are implemented at the Platform levels and in underlying financing documents;

=== PAGE 28 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 24 
 
 
 
59 Note that there will be no direct legal arrangement between the Platform, Singapore Manager and Macquarie India Advisory Company. SG 
Co pursuant to shareholder rights in the Platform will review the information received and based on the advice from the Singapore Manager, 
approve relevant shareholder matters. All recommendations to the SG Co’s Board would be reviewed and proposed by the Singapore 
Manager under the management agreement. Further Singapore Manager will engage Macquarie’s India Advisory Company to provide non-
binding investment advisory services. 
2. the GCF account is managed and maintained in compliance with the requirements under the AMA and FAA; 
and 
3. GCF is provided access to the Platform’s performance vis-à-vis the Programme’s targeted outcomes and 
results.  
In order to satisfy its obligations under the AMA and the FAA to be entered into with respect to this Programme, 
MAAML will enter into a deed of undertaking with each Executing Entity). 
 
LP 
The offshore pooling vehicle will be domiciled in Singapore as a Limited Partnership (LP). The Singapore GP, as 
general partner, together with GCF and the other Investors, as limited partners, will enter into a partnership agreement. 
The capital commitment / contribution of each limited partner in the LP will have different class of interests: 
1. Class A interests for commercial investors. Class A would benefit from first-loss benefit provided by Class B 
investor. Class A investors would not have participated in the LP without this first-loss benefit due a perceived 
unfavorable risk / reward dynamic;  
2. Class B interests for GCF, against which GCF would provide first-loss benefit to the Class A investors, making 
the risk / reward dynamic for Class A investors acceptable; and 
3. Class C interests for Singapore GP / Singapore Manager or provision by way of management agreement, for 
the purposes of receiving carried interest distributions from the LP. 
 
The LPA will detail terms of investment (including the ownership interest of each LP), in line with the FAA signed 
between the GCF and the AE. Note that the proposed structure will not change as commercial investors become part 
of the LP, SG Co. or the Platform, on or after First Close. The AE will analyse the LP’s compliance with GCF’s business 
model by reviewing several documents provided by other Executing Entities (EEs) in the structure. 
Singapore GP and Singapore Manager 
A 100% Macquarie Group owned, Singapore private limited company will be established to act as the GP to the LP. 
The GP, under a management agreement, will delegate its investment management responsibilities to the Singapore 
Manager, which will be a Capital Markets Services licensed entity. The Singapore Manager will have an overall 
responsibility for the management and operation of the LP under the management agreement. Some of its key 
responsibilities include: 
1. managing LP’s investment, including identifying representatives for the Board of the Platform (where 
appropriate); 
2. identifying and assisting with the implementation of strategic enhancements at the Platform level; 
3. undertaking other matters involved in the management and administration of the Platform; and 
4. determining appropriate risk and liquidity strategies for the LP and the Platform. 
 
To ensure that Singapore Manager’s responsibilities are appropriately resourced, the Singapore Manager will enter 
into a non-binding investment advisory agreement with Macquarie India Advisory Company (IAC).59  
Singapore Master and SG Co. 
The LP will establish a Singapore private limited company (SG Master Co.) as a wholly owned subsidiary, which will 
in turn incorporate another Singapore private limited company (SG Co.) to make investments via equity, equity-like 
hybrid securities, debt instruments and other similar instruments from shareholders in the Platform. SG Master Co. is 
primarily being set up to provide flexibility to investors to exit the investment at Singapore level and avail India – 
Singapore tax treaty benefits, subject to meeting relevant conditions. SG Master Co. and SG Co. will also be a party to 
the GP management agreement, or enter into a separate management agreement with the Singapore Manager.

3. providing assistance in relation to adequate implementation of risk management systems in order to identify, 
measure, manage and monitor appropriately all risks relevant to the LP’s investment strategy.
