Singapore Manager and Singapore GP, will issue a drawdown notice to the LP and the LP will in turn issue a 
drawdown notice to the AE (for GCF as prescribed under FAA). 
4. Upon the receipt of drawdown notice, the AE will remit the funds to the LP from its fiduciary GCF account, 
which, in turn, remit the funds to SG Co. through SG Master Co.  
5. SG Co. will utilise the funds received to capitalise the Platform. 
6. Note that the AE will drawdown the capital as committed by GCF over the Investment Period as defined in the 
Term Sheet. 
 
Fund flows for distribution 
Upon receipt of distributions from the Platform, SG Co. will remit the funds to SG Master Co. which, in turn, will remit 
the funds to the LP. The LP will then make distributions to fiduciary GCF account as per the distribution waterfall 
mechanics in the LPA. 
Please note that we do not expect any conflict of interest issue to arise between the executing entities and the AE, as 
the mechanics for payment rights of GCF via the AE as against those of commercial investors will be well defined and 
documented as part of the FAA and LPA.  
B.5. Justification for GCF funding request (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages) 
Climate vulnerabilities and fiscal situation 
According to Climate Action Tracker, India’s GHG emissions, excluding Land Use Change and Forestry (LULUCF), 
are estimated to be 2.8-2.9 GtCO2 in 2020. They are expected to increase by ~3.5% over next 10 years, reaching 3.8-
4.1 GtCO2 by 2030. This represents a 27-35% increase in emissions from 2010 to 2020 and a 76-87% increase by 
2030. 62  Further, India’s average temperature is expected to increase by >4.4°C by 2100 (relative to average 
temperature between 1975-2006) in the worst-case scenario, according to a report by the Indian government’s Ministry 
of Earth Sciences.63 This leaves India highly vulnerable, prone to extreme climatic events such as flash floods, cyclonic 
storms, droughts, heatwaves, etc. and more susceptible to crop damage, forced migration, increases in social issues 
and poor economic growth. Whilst India’s NDCs under the Paris Agreement are 2.0°C compatible (indicating that India’s 
climate commitment in 2030 is a significant share of global effort based on its responsibility and capability), a significant 
amount of capital is required to achieve it. According to the Indian government’s preliminary estimates, India requires 
~USD 2.5 trillion of capital between 2015 – 2030, which is equivalent to annual requirement of USD 170 billion per year 
at 2014-15 prices (8% of India’s FY15 GDP). Further, as per IFC, total capital required to achieve the climate targets 
was much higher at USD 3.1 trillion between 2018-2030. Given the size of the required investment, a robust support 
from domestic and foreign investors is essential.  
Additionally, with the onset of COVID-19 pandemic since March 2020, the finances of the Indian government have 
become further stretched (fiscal deficit was 9.5% of India’s GDP in FY21 vs 4.6% in FY2064 and is expected to be in 
8.3% in FY22 according to Fitch65), forcing the Indian government to prioritise investments in healthcare infrastructure 
and research and development of affordable medicines and vaccines. As a result, the availability of capital from GCF 
during these testing times for the Indian population and economy will be significantly important as it will 1) assist the 
country to be on track to its achieve its set targets under the Paris agreement, 2) reduce pressure on the government’s 
limited resources while it focuses on keeping its citizens safe and healthy, and 3) provide the necessary capital to the 
nascent and cash starved e-mobility sector to drive innovation and achieve scale.  
GCF value add

=== PAGE 34 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 30 
 
 
Under the proposed transaction structure, GCF’s USD 200 million investment will be used to provide “first loss” capital 
protection to commercial investors, de-risking them by absorbing higher risk in case of the Platform’s underperformance 
due to the multiple risk factors as highlighted in section F – Risk Assessment and Management. GCF’s concessional 
capital is expected to have a multi-fold impact on the entire e-mobility value chain in terms of: 
1. Crowding in capital: Catalysing investments from various institutional investors who otherwise would have been 
reluctant to participate in the nascent e-mobility sector in India. 
2. Drive TCO parity: Help achieve TCO parity for electric vehicles at a faster rate than currently estimated, thereby 
assisting the government in limiting its financial subsidy support for the sector. 
3. Improve availability of capital: Assist in easing the availability of finance for electric vehicles and e-mobility 
focussed companies and / or customers, which is presently one of the biggest barriers impeding the growth of 
the sector. Successful implementation of this enterprise will likely lead to competitors in time. 
4. Help offer differentiated products: Enable the Platform to offer bespoke leasing and structured credit solutions 
at attractive terms such as long tenure, competitive interest rates, high Loan to Value (LTV) ratio, reduced 
requirement of collateral or other forms of security and acceptance of operating and residual value risks, thus 
addressing several requirements of customers which are unmet by current product offerings in the market. 
5. Assist in capital recycling: Help e-mobility focussed companies recycle their capital and invest either in 
technological advancements or expansion of existing manufacturing facilities (refer to section C.3 – Capacity 
building and technology development / transfer for more details). 
6. Drive replication in other geographies: Demonstrate a business model that could be deployed in other markets 
to accelerate EV adoption. 
7. Establishment of sustainable, inclusive business practices: The Platform will encourage counterparties to 
adhere to GCF standards and policies, on environmental and gender aspects. Increasing sensitisation and 
proactiveness on such aspects by counterparties is expected to have a profound impact on the local community 
and environment, and set a good example for other businesses / competitors in the industry. For more details 
refer to Annex 06 – Enivronment Social Management System and Annex 08 – Gender Assessment Report. 
 
Apart from raising equity capital from institutional investors, the Platform will leverage on its Macquarie Group affiliation 
and networks to identify and hire an experienced management team and raise large amounts of third party debt capital 
from domestic (INR denominated) and / or foreign banks / financial institutions (USD denominated) depending upon 
market conditions, thus compounding the effect of GCF’s concessional capital. 
The Platform is anticipated to have a positive impact on India’s post pandemic economic recovery, carbon emission 
reduction targets, air quality, health outcomes, new job creation and gender inequality. 
B.6. Exit strategy (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page) 
Exit strategy 
The aim of the Programme is to build a leading E-mobility Financing Platform which can continue as a ‘going concern’ 
post the exit of investors. GCF and commercial investors’ (‘Investors’) capital commitment will be for a period of up to 
10 years, subject to defined extension options. The form of exit from the investment will be dependent upon the global 
and India’s macro-economic environment, growth prospects, competitive landscape and status of rules and regulations 
governing the EV sector, future business potential of the Platform, ease of exit and returns offered to the Investors. 
Exit can be in the form of: 
1. Partial or Full Platform trade sale to: 
o 
Strategic investors such as banks and NBFCs who are looking to expand their exposure to the EV 
sector and / or are looking to diversify their existing asset portfolio. 
o 
Financial investors such as impact funds, infrastructure or sector agnostic private equity funds, 
pension and sovereign wealth funds that may be looking to enter the market. 
2. Initial Public Offering (IPO): The Singapore Manager may also look to provide exit to existing investors through 
the IPO route which will provide opportunity for retail investors to participate in the company’s long term of 
objective of decarbonising the transportation sector of India. 
  
Sustainability

=== PAGE 35 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 31 
 
 
 
66 IPE Real Assets 2021 – Top 100 Infrastructure Investment Managers. 
67 https://www.macquarie.com/assets/macq/investor/reports/2022/macquarie-group-hy22-interim-report.pdf

MAM, with support from GCF and commercial investors, is looking to build ‘first of its kind’ E-mobility Financing Platform 
in India, with the aim to create long term value and provide a sustainable and successful pathway for other companies 
and investors in the EV sector, even after GCF and commercial investors have exited the Platform.  
To achieve this, the Programme has identified key areas related to building of 1) a sustainable business by instilling 
best practices (ESG and financial processes including monitoring, reviews, etc.), and 2) a sustainable EV ecosystem 
as critical for long term value creation and development of future investments’ foundation. 
A. 
Building a sustainable business by instilling best governance practices 
 
MAM is recognised as the largest infrastructure asset manager globally66 with an AUM of AUD 735.5 billion as at 30 
September 2021.67 It employs 1,92168 investment and portfolio management professionals across the world who have 
relevant experience in building successful platforms and unique business models leading to scaled operational 
platforms. 
As an entity managed by MAM, the Platform will leverage on MAM’s experience to implement best practices and 
frameworks developed over several decades to build a sustainable company. This will include establishment of an 
empowered and effective Board and management team, operational checks and balances, effective systems of internal 
control and risk management, promoting a ‘governance culture’ of transparency and accountability, and putting in place 
remuneration policies that reward achievement of objectives and long-term shareholder value creation.  
Further, post initial investment, MAM will be actively engaged with the Platform, driving long term sustainability and 
value creation. This will happen through active performance oversight, which includes monthly performance monitoring, 
news tracking, periodic assessment of counterparty’s credit quality, active engagement and assistance to Platform 
management and impact assessment (refer to section B.3 – Programme Description for more details).  
Additionally, the Platform will also implement a ‘Watchlist’ framework which will ensure that any stress or other factors 
that could have a material impact on the counterparty viability, are highlighted in advance so that appropriate remedial 
measures can be taken where possible.  
Establishment of a robust oversight culture will seek to ensure that there is low probability of default and / or fraud 
among the lessees / borrowers which, in turn, will result in: 
1. a strong sense of comfort to new players, investors and financiers looking to enter the market; and 
2. ‘proof of concept’ affirmation for existing portfolio companies’ business models. 
 
B. 
Building a sustainable EV ecosystem  
 
Development of new financial products 
The Platform will look to offer bespoke financial products in the EV sector, which will include operating and finance 
leases. Apart from this, the Platform will consider offering other structured credit / financing instruments depending 
upon the requirements of the counterparty. As these products are scaled-up, there will be increased capacity from 
existing financial institutions, such as banks and NBFCs, to understand the key risks in EV financing, assess the 
performance of the financial products and develop relationships with key market players. All this will support potential 
replication of the financing products and development of newer products as the market heads towards maturity. 
Reduce upfront EV costs through long-term financing at attractive rates 
Currently, most EV companies generally find it difficult to raise capital from financial institutions. Even if companies can 
raise capital, the terms of that capital are usually unattractive and biased towards the financier making it a significant 
barrier impeding the growth of the sector. This Platform aims to change this paradigm through:  
1. differentiated product offering: The Platform will look to provide differentiated financing products (operating and 
financial lease including variations that assume certain reporting and performance risks) at attractive rates that 
help reduce upfront capex and ongoing costs for OEMs, operators, and sponsors; 
2. focusing on the entire value chain: Support the roll-out of EVs, charging infrastructure and other e-mobility 
infrastructure;

=== PAGE 36 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 32 
 
 
 
 
3. longer tenure: Make long tenured financing products a norm rather than an exception; 
4. acceptance of residual value risk: Accept residual value risk to clearly demonstrate the useful life of EVs (>10 
years in most cases) and promote greater market acceptance; and 
5. increasing private sector participation: Increase participation of OEMs, operators, and sponsors in government 
tenders (wherein the concession agreements are usually awarded for 8-10 years). 
 
Catalysing further investment through capacity building and replicable models 
The risk-sharing models developed with local OEMs and financiers will have compounding effects outside the direct 
impact from the Platform itself: 
• 
Increased investment in the sector is expected to instil greater confidence in the market, increase competition 
and drive down upfront and operating costs, thereby encouraging a cycle of further investment and market 
maturation. 
• 
Increased capacity of local OEMs will support higher investment from the private sector in enhancing the 
manufacturing capabilities and developing new business models that propel long-term presence in the sector. 
 
Lastly, the Platform will ensure continuous engagement with various stakeholders such as Niti Aayog (nodal agency 
for electric vehicles in India), Ministry of Environment, Forest, and Climate Change (MoEFCC), OEMs, Operators, other 
financing companies (such as banks and NBFCs) and consultants to: 
1. drive further policy improvements; 
2. help address challenges affecting the accelerated growth of the EV sector; 
3. encourage innovation and competitiveness; and 
4. help drive long term sustainability of the sector.

A total capital commitment of USD 405 million is expected from GCF and commercial 
investors. This amount will be invested over a period of 5 years across the various 
segments of the e-mobility value chain.  
What is the purpose of third party debt? 
In addition to equity capital from GCF and other investors, the SG Master Co., the SG Co, 
and / or the Platform will also look to raise third party debt (either INR and / or USD 
denominated) for future business expansion and create multiplier effect on equity investors’ 
capital commitment. The decision to raise third party debt at either of the levels will depend 
on the market conditions, commercial considerations and ease of compliance.  
How will debt be serviced? 
Any third party debt that is drawn by the Platform will be serviced through the principal and 
interest payments that the Platform will receive from various lessees and borrowers.

=== PAGE 38 ===
C 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 34 
 
 
 
 
How will the Platform ensure financial discipline and compliance with debt servicing 
obligations? 
Firstly, the maximum leverage that will be raised by the Platform will be as per industry 
benchmarks. 
Secondly, in order to secure a competitive third party credit rating (local or global) and 
attractive financing terms for this debt, the Platform will 1) establish robust credit and risk 
assessment frameworks, seeking to minimise payment defaults, 2) have a diversified 
portfolio by sub-sector, financing solution and credit profile of counterparties, 3) have a 
professional and experienced management team, 4) adopt the strong governance and risk 
framework generally followed by MAM managed companies, and 5) leverage on 
Macquarie’s global expertise to overcome any initial challenges.   
How much quantum of co-financing capital will the Platform be able to mobilise? 
As mentioned in section B.3, the Programme will look to offer bespoke financial products 
such as operating leases, finance leases and structured loans depending upon the 
requirement of the counterparty. By offering such unique financing solutions, the Platform 
expects some level of either equity investment, mezzanine debt or secured debt to be raised 
by the counterparties. The exact amount of this funding may vary across each lessee / 
borrower.

The focus of the e-mobility Programme is to provide bespoke financial solutions to EV focussed companies in 
India. This Programme will create an enabling environment through the flow of capital into domestic OEMs, 
operators and start-ups, which have otherwise been unable to raise long term financing at attractive terms. This 
Programme will invest in the e-mobility sector at scale which will demonstrate new financing solutions to the 
market, build capacity of counterparties in developing financing solutions and support government tenders and 
initiatives. Furthermore, it is expected that various domestic OEMs, operators and / or start-ups who receive 
the capital from the Programme will: 
1. recycle capital and invest either in technological advancements or expanding existing / establishing 
new manufacturing facilities; 
2. re-invest capital in existing business to make it more sustainable; 
3. invest in further development of supply chain; 
4. be encouraged to participate in vertical or horizontal integration; and 
5. encourage new financiers (debt and equity investors) to take additional risk. 
 
All the above factors cumulatively are expected to help reduce the upfront vehicle and maintenance costs, thus 
driving down the EV TCO below that of ICE vehicles, and help in the overall development of the e-mobility 
sector.

 EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA  
This section refers to the performance of the project/programme against the investment criteria as set out in the 
GCF’s Initial Investment Framework.  
D.1. Impact potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page) 
As a signatory to the Paris Agreement, India has committed to reducing the emissions intensity of its GDP by 33% to 
35% below 2005 levels by 2030 (UNFCCC, 2015). While India includes a sector-specific target for the power sector in 
its NDC and COP26 pledges, there is no such target for the transport sector. Decarbonising the transport sector in India 
can help to substantially reduce the impacts related to GHG emissions and other air pollutants and has several 
additional potential co-benefits, such as major improvements in public health, particularly in urban areas, improved fuel 
savings and reduced noise pollution.  
Electrification of vehicles plays an essential role in decarbonising the transport sector69 as it offers promising GHG 
emission abatement potential, in comparison to other alternates, by 2050 i.e., towards 1.5°C scenario proposed by 
IPCC (refer to the below diagram). However, a mix of financial, behavioural and infrastructure incentives are needed to 
develop the e-mobility sector and associate infrastructure to achieve the promised targets.  
 
 
 
The proposed Programme is expected to have a direct GHG reduction potential of ~2.3 MtCO2e over the 
implementation period and ~9.5 MtCO2e over the lifetime. Note that, to improve the additionality of the program, the 
Platform will seek to encourage counterparties to use natural coolants in the vehicles instead of hydrofluorocarbons. 
However, on a conservative basis, the impact of this has not been considered as part of the additionality analysis. 
The Programme, through its implementation approach, is poised to lead the pathways to decarbonise the transport 
sector in India, pursuant to India’s NDCs and COP26 pledges. Discussions on the Programme’s leverage towards 
sustainable development, including contribution to 2030 Agenda for Sustainable Development, are elaborated in the 
subsequent section (refer to section D.3 – Sustainable development for more details). 
D.2. Paradigm shift potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page) 
The E-mobility Financing Programme aims to encourage a wide range of initiatives and aspects which are related to 
sustainability. For instance, the Programme aims to support the acceleration of India’s EV sector as a key 
mechanism in decarbonising the transport sector. Financing solutions provided by the platform will aim to create a 
paradigm shift within the Indian context by supporting the private sector to drive forward the e-mobility sector, which 
can serve as an example for other similar markets. The Platform’s ability to drive reduction of ~9.5 MtCO2e emissions 
over the lifetime, will contribute towards making India more resilient to the impacts of climate change, and provide a 
pathway that is consistent with national adaption strategies and plans (refer to section B.1). 
The Platform will also contribute to the Indian regulatory framework and other policies launched by the Indian 
government and several state governments by regularly engaging with them and providing inputs on how to increase 
 
69 Decarbonising the Indian transport sector pathways and policies, Dec 2020, Climate Action Tracker.

=== PAGE 41 ===

the effectiveness and impact of policies / regulations enacted. For instance, the Platform will work with the government 
to slowly reduce subsidies and other tax incentives over time as the sector achieves maturity and substantial crowding-
in of private capital, which would help achieve TCO parity across various segments of the e-mobility value chain. 
Additionally, the Platform will provide support to government think-tanks, such as the Niti Aayog, and other government 
bodies in formulating new policies leveraging MAM’s global presence in leading EV markets such as China, USA, UK, 
and Germany, that will drive the development and success of the Indian EV ecosystem and EV financing market.  
Contribution to an enabling environment for private sector investment 
The EV sector is currently partially supported by an enabling policy environment, however, several barriers to 
investment (as outlined in section B.2 – Theory of Change) currently exist. The Platform aims to shift the e-mobility 
sector from a nascent industry to a mainstream sector which does not rely on concessional funding.  
Contribute to achieving cost parity: The Platform, through its ‘first-of-kind’ financing solutions, aims to provide operating 
and finance leases to OEMs, sponsors, and operators. These financial solutions, priced at attractive rates with long 
tenures, will assist the counterparties to deleverage their balance sheets and recycle their limited capital, thereby 
encouraging further investments in technological advancements / R&D. Higher expenditure on R&D is expected to help 
reduce the high upfront EV cost and achieve TCO parity far before the current estimate of 2030 for different electric 
vehicles as provided in the diagram below.70

Contribute to providing supportive infrastructure: By offering financial solutions to key parts of the ecosystem, the 
Platform is expected to support overall market development, especially in the area of charging infrastructure. For 
example, financial products to enable more widespread deployment of charging infrastructure assets / projects, 
especially in dense city areas, can reduce behavioural barriers, such as range anxiety for consumers. Supportive 
infrastructure such as battery swapping stations and quick charge stations would enhance EV adoption and make it 
more economically viable to own an EV. Strong supportive infrastructure has been shown to be a leading contributor 
to greater penetration growth in more mature EV markets.71 
Potential for knowledge sharing and learning 
The core focus of the Platform is to achieve maturation of the e-mobility market and development of EV financing 
market in multiple ways: 
• 
Local and sustainable industry growth: This Programme intends to partner with local OEMs and other 
companies such that the e-mobility technical and market capacity is developed locally. The advantages of local 
development of EVs include lower costs, new job creation (refer to section to D.3 – Sustainable Development) 
and better logistical coordination. The Platform would also work with counterparties to encourage inclusive, 
socially responsible business practices. This would support the long-term growth of the sector and reduce the 
need for concessional or public funding over time. 72 This also contributes to the Indian government’s vision of 
an Atmanirbhar Bharat (Self-Reliant India) and ‘Make in India’ initiative, as the Programme encourages the 
growth and development of local businesses and industries.

• 
Financing structures: The focus on financing and leasing structures, rather than equity ownership of companies, 
through this Platform is expected to support the development of innovative business models across a broad 
range of local companies in India, where there are currently significant financing gaps. EV leasing is the 
predominate financing model in mature markets73 and propagation of this structure is expected to encourage 
increased investment from private sector participants as the market matures. The Platform will look to offer long 
term leasing solutions at attractive terms (such as low interest rates, high LTV ratios, limited security and 
undertake asset ownership risk) which are not offered by any of the existing financial institutions in India. Below 
is a brief comparison of the types of products available in the market.

• 
Most banks and NBFCs are generally reluctant to provide financing solutions to EV companies due to the high 
technology risk and unproven business models (due to a lack of operating history). The Platform aims to change 
this paradigm as the learnings from the Programme will act as guiding precedents on how to price the product, 
manage various operational and financial risks, and what protection measures financial institutions need to seek 
to ensure appropriate risks adjusted returns on their capital investment. 
 
• 
Institutional investment: Institutional investors generally invest in mature markets and have a lower risk appetite 
compared to public investors. The Platform intends to approach institutional investors to crowd-in investors to 
a sector / geography that they may have not previously considered due to the sector’s nascent stage of 
development. It is intended that by providing exposure through a platform with a risk buffer provided by GCF’s 
concessional capital, institutional investors will build their capacity and better understand the market as it 
matures, such that further investment can be considered with less or no reliance on concessional finance over 
time.  
 
Potential for scale-up and replication  
Financing solutions developed by the Platform will be designed for the Indian context, with an understanding of what 
has worked best in other mature e-mobility markets. Once established, the structures may have an ‘example’ effect 
whereby other financiers can replicate the products in the market to generate greater investment in the sector.  
A successful electrification of India’s EV market, at scale, provides a path for other geographies with similar markets to 
follow, including other South- and South-east Asian developing countries who are similarly faced with significant 
population and economic growth that will increase the contribution of emissions from the transport sector. The Platform 
itself could also be replicated in other markets to similarly support early-stage investment and providing financing 
support in-line with country ambitions.

Social co-benefits 
Social and gender impacts 
Through its partnerships with select domestic OEMs, the Programme aims to promote universal access and safety 
measures for women, children and people with disabilities, including proper lighting, in-vehicle cameras, vehicle 
monitoring systems and other safety measures, especially in buses. The Platform will look to encourage the 
engagement of women and men of all ages as stakeholders in the design, development and implementation of 
strategies and activities to be financed. A report by the World Research Institute (WRI) states that, the manufacturing 
and delivery of electric vehicles will require far lesser mechanical tasks that are perceived to be heavy-duty. Instead, 
digital and IT related skills that are more cerebral and ‘strength neutral’ will be needed. This opens the possibility of 
promoting inclusion and gender diversity in the auto industry workforce, which has historically been lopsided. Some 
vehicle manufacturers in India have set up women only shop floors, but mainstreaming and additional systematic efforts 
are required. Studies show that gender parity improves problem-solving, leading to increased profits. This is unlikely to 
become a reality for India without concrete actions, which may include: (i) Stronger marketing of skilling courses for 
women, (ii) Incentivising industry players who maintain gender balance in diverse roles across the sector, and (iii) 
Establishing labour and on-the-job welfare systems for equal benefits, job growth and safety for women working in the 
transport industry.74 The Platform therefore will strive to maximise the impact of its funding for adaptation, mitigation 
and promotion of environmental, social, economic, and development benefits, taking not only a gender-sensitive, but a 
gender-responsive approach in line with the GCF’s Gender Policy (refer to Annex 8 – Gender assessment and 
programme level action plan for more details). 
Environmental co-benefits 
Air pollution and health outcomes 
The transport sector in India is linked with emissions of other pollutants, such as fine particulate matter (PM2.5), known 
to have a damaging impact on human health. This is caused by (among other things)75 vehicles with low emission 
standards, compounded by increased congestion in cities, causing pollutants to be trapped. India has 21 of the top 30 
most polluted cities76 and it is estimated that over 84% of India’s population are exposed to high concentrations of air 
pollution. This causes over 2 million deaths annually, and associated healthcare costs equalling ~5.4% of GDP.77 India’s 
capital, Delhi, was ranked as the worst city in the World Air Quality Report 2019,78 and half of the 50 most polluted 
cities were also in India. The adoption of electric vehicles, especially in urban areas, will reduce pollution compared to 
adoption of ICE vehicles and, over time, replace older, polluting vehicles, thereby improving risk to the health of the 
population.  
Economic co-benefits 
Energy security 
The Indian government’s push for EVs is directly linked with its stated intent to enhance energy security in addition to 
GHG emissions reduction. Since the early 2000s, India’s crude oil imports have risen steeply, of which 40% of total oil 
consumption comes from the transport sector.79 Oil imports contributed USD 102 billion to the Indian trade deficit in 
2020, covering over 80% of the country’s oil needs. 80 An increase in the penetration of EVs directly and indirectly 
through this Programme will decrease India’s oil import requirement, decreasing oil imports which is estimated to save 
USD 330 billion between 2017 and 2030, as well as corresponding GHG emissions.81  
Economic impact and job creation

India’s middle income class group is expected to increase from 158 million households in 2018 to 300 million 
households by 2030.82,83 During the same period, the number of urban residents is expected to increase from 473 
million to 600 million.84 This is poignant for the e-mobility Programme, which primarily aims to target public buses and 
corporate fleet segments which are mainly used by middle income groups and urban residents. Further, through its 
investments, the Programme will aim to provide a safe, reliable, comfortable, and affordable transportation system, 
which will increase the adoption rate among urban residents and middle-income groups, thereby accentuating the 
Programme’s intended impact.  
Additionally, as per a recent Niti Aayog report, European Climate Foundation has estimated that any reduction in oil 
demand by more efficient electric cars is expected to create 500,000 to 850,000 more domestic jobs by 2030. This 
report explains that oil production and distribution has a very low employment intensity, with just four jobs per million 
Euros of value added. This is far below the 24 jobs per million Euros in the general economy. Therefore, any shift in 
expenditure from buying imported oil to other expenditure choices would generate additional employment.85  
The below chart highlights how a transition to EVs results in cumulative benefits to the economy, primarily through 
increased job creation.86  
 
Thus, the Programme, through its partnerships with leading domestic OEMs, operators and sponsors, will have a 
significant impact on the local economy through new job creation in both rural and urban areas, and supporting urban 
mobility for economic growth.

D.4. Needs of recipient (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page) 
India’s vulnerabilities 
Between 1901 and 2018, India’s average temperature rose by 0.7°C, resulting in extreme weather events and putting 
pressure on the country’s already scarce resources, thereby threatening the livelihood of millions of people. In recent 
times, with increases in sporadic climatic events such as cloud bursts, flash floods, unseasonal rains and prolonged 
droughts, crop failures have become widespread, water shortages more chronic (India already ranks as #13 on the 
WRI’s National Water Stress Rankings Across Globe) and migration and related conflicts elevated across the country. 
Changes in rainfall patterns and sea level can have severe impact on coastal cities. Cyclone Amphan in 2020 in the 
coastal state of West Bengal was one of the strongest cyclones on record in the Bay of Bengal, killing ~100 people and 
causing USD 14 billion worth of damage.90 
 
The average temperature in India is expected to rise by 4.7°C to 5.5°C by 2100 under the Representative Concentration 
Pathway (RCP) 8.5 scenario (a scenario in which emissions continue to rise throughout the 21st century),91 with extreme 
climatic events and natural hazards rising in terms of intensity, spatial extent, and frequency across India. If there is no 
 
87 OECD has estimated the poor outdoor quality will negatively impact Indian GDP growth by ~1% between 2010 and 2060 in a “do nothing” 
scenario (~£15bn equivalent). 
88 Greenstone et al, 2015; CSTEP, 2018. 
89 Sustainable Development Department, World Bank 2013. 
90 https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CycloneAmphan 
91 https://reliefweb.int/report/india/assessment-climate-change-over-indian-region-report-ministry-earth-sciences-moes

change to current climate mitigation policies, India may lose the equivalent of 2.8% of GDP by 2050.92 The most severe 
impact of this is expected on low and middle-income groups, women, children, and the elderly. Around 28% of the 
population, or 364 million people, live below the national poverty line of USD 2 per person per day.93 1.77 million people 
are homeless94 and 6.5% of the population are unemployed according to the Centre for Monitoring Indian Economy 
(CMIE).95 Lower GDP would mean fewer jobs, which will have a disproportionate impact on youth and women. Today, 
many households in the rural and urban areas do not have adequate provision of basic infrastructure and services, 
such as access to electricity, safe water, and mobility. Given these factors, there is an urgent need to undertake low or 
zero carbon and climate resilient infrastructure investments in India. According to a recent report by Lifegate, India 
needs ~USD 2.5 trillion of investment between 2015 and 2030, or USD 170 billion/year in-order to meet its NDCs by 
2030.96 The government’s budget alone will not be sufficient to finance the required funding for climate action. While 
the Indian government has doubled its spending on green projects since 2016, its budget is increasingly focused on 
socio-economic development activities rather than climate mitigation and / or adaption activities. Furthermore, the 
COVID-19 pandemic, national lockdown and job losses have exacerbated the strain on the government’s finances as 
the need to build healthcare infrastructure takes priority. In a post Covid-19 scenario, there will be an even greater need 
for the private sector to drive capital flow in the e-mobility sector.  
Economic stimulation 
The need for economic stimulation is also critical as the country faces the ongoing COVID-19 pandemic. The pandemic 
has triggered a shift in the priorities of the government towards the creation of an effective healthcare ecosystem, 
primarily focused on delivering quality primary care to citizens. Confronted with the ever-growing threat of physical 
climate risks, the economy requires a more focused approach to policy development and funding. This is iterated by 
an analysis by Oxford Economics that suggests that India’s GDP could fall by 90% by 2100 if current policies are not 
improved and climate finance is not scaled up.97 Additionally, a McKinsey report states India is set to face significant 
impacts of climate change, which will also have severe impacts on economic development.98  
Financial pathways 
According to a recent report by Niti Aayog, over the next decade, India’s EV transition will require a cumulative capital 
investment of INR 19.7 trillion (USD 266 billion) in vehicles, charging infrastructure, and batteries. This analysis 
suggested that, by 2030, the market size for the financing of EVs in India has the potential to be INR 3.7 trillion (USD 
50 billion). As the amount of investment required for India’s mobility transition is significant, mobilising the investments 
to finance OEMs, battery manufacturers, charging stations and end consumers will require targeted and systemic policy 
support, in addition to shifts in market design, business models, and financial structuring. There are financing barriers 
currently in place which restrict the large-scale penetration of EVs. In a recent report, Niti Aayog and Rocky Mountain 
Institute outline the following key challenges to scaling up finance for EVs in India:99 
1. High interest rates: Electric vehicles face an additional risk premium compared to ICEs when seeking financing , 
adding to operating costs.  
2. Low LTV ratios: Due to the risk of borrower default and low EV resale value, low LTVs are offered in financing 
packages which increase the equity contribution required by operators.  
3. 
Specialised products: Most financing institutions do not currently offer specialised products such as leasing for 
EVs. Operators face high interest rates, low LTVs and shorter repayments compared to loans for ICE vehicles. 
Additional collateral may also be required, which does not support innovation by newer or smaller companies. 
The above issues get more pronounced in case of EVs as the upfront capital cost of acquisition of EVs are significantly 
higher than their peer ICE vehicles and the residual value is uncertain. Therefore, this Programme aims to provide the 
Indian transportation sector with a new and focused EV financing platform that can: 
1. play a catalytic role to accelerate the creation of India’s EV leasing market by offering customised operating 
and financial leases. By providing this solution, the Platform will be willing to undertake asset ownership,

performance and residual risks, a capability currently not available with any of the existing banks and 
NBFCs; 
2. ensure overall development of the entire EV value chain by reducing upfront capex deployment, increasing 
availability of finance and offering attractive lease / credit terms by leveraging on GCF’s concessional capital; 
3. help mobilise the massive quantum of capital from various institutional investors towards India’s EV sector and 
assist in reducing the need for government financial subsidy over time; and 
4. bring focus to a sector which has, until now, received muted response from existing banks and NBFCs. It is 
important to note that, whilst select banks, NBFCs, fintechs and sustainability financiers have started providing 
vehicle financing loans / structured credit solutions to electric vehicles: 
a) most banks and NBFCs are reluctant to participate in the sector due to small present market size, 
limited performance history and high residual value risk; and 
b) fintechs and sustainability financiers do not have the balance sheet strength to create meaningful 
impact, and are primarily retail (B2C) focused with two and three wheeler customers as the target 
segment. 
 
 
Although current policies, incentives, and financial assistance have caused some movement in the EV sector, it still is 
not adequately backed to overcome challenges faced by OEMs in accessing capital for expansion, weak business case 
for charging infrastructure and high up-front cost associated with EVs. Therefore, this Programme intends to drive 
climate mitigation (positive externalities) and economic stimulation (creation of a multiplier effect) by providing 
innovative financial solutions, investing in charging infrastructure, crowding-in institutional investors and supporting 
growth of the domestic industry to reduce costs. 
D.5. Country ownership (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page) 
This Programme directly contributes to India’s NDC target of reducing carbon emissions by 35% by 2030 from 2005, 
including through transforming 30% of all vehicles to EVs.100 EV promotion has been supported by significant policy 
development at both the national and state levels. The Indian government has already committed subsidies of up to 
USD 1.4 billion to enable an accelerated roll out of EVs under the FAME Phase II scheme. The Programme will 
participate in the FAME scheme and other government policies and schemes where appropriate, which includes 
requirements for a predefined level of domestic component manufacturing.  
At least 10 states have policies that are being implemented, and another six are drafting relevant policies101. Although 
most of these policies prioritise public transport, paratransit, and job creation, each of these states have also set targets 
and other incentives (depending on the state) for supply-side (manufacturing) and demand-side (consumer and 
charging infrastructure investments).102 For example, this Programme will seek, directly or indirectly, to engage in 
tenders released by the states for public buses, charging infrastructure or otherwise, and aim to be involved in 
stakeholder discussions to support further policy development.  
Stakeholder engagement  
Whilst we have been in active discussions with multiple stakeholders across the value chain, including government 
entities such as Niti Aayog, the Ministry of Road Transport and Highway (MoRTH), the Ministry of Environment, Forest, 
and Climate Change (MoEFCC) and the Ministry of Power, most of the efforts have been largely concentrated towards 
engaging with Niti Aayog, the Indian government’s think thank, which is the key driver of all e-mobility initiatives in India. 
It has been playing a major role in shaping industry policies, guidelines, charging standards and best practices for the 
Indian EV ecosystem.  
In addition, several discussions have been held with other industry stakeholders, including leading domestic OEMs, 
bus operators, charge infrastructure providers, vehicle scrappage companies, banks, NBFCs, and independent experts. 
The discussions have been focused on:  
• 
better understanding the present market dynamics; 
• 
future growth potential of the EV sector;

The key constraint we have identified during this dialogue has been access to the right kind of capital. The proposed

 Engagement with National Designated Authority (NDA)  
There has been an active engagement with NDA since the Concept Note stage, regularly updating it about the various 
aspects of the e-mobility programme as envisaged by MAM.  
The AE secured a No Objection Letter for the Programme from Indian government on 15th September 2021 
(refer to Annex 01 – No Objection Letter for more details).  
Capacity and successful track record to execute investments in India 
For over twenty-five years, MAM has partnered with clients, governments, and communities to finance, manage, 
develop, and enhance essential infrastructure. Today, MAM’s portfolio is relied on by more than 100 million people 
every day and employs ~170,000 people globally.103 Our team of highly experienced professionals invest in businesses 
that underpin economies and communities, aiming to add real and lasting value for our clients and the people our 
assets serve. 
MAM launched its first India focused fund with ~USD 1 billion of capital commitments in 2009 in partnership with the 
State Bank of India and IFC.104 Since then, MAM has invested via its multiple Asia funds across roads, airports, 
communications, energy, and renewable energy in the country.  
MAM’s India portfolio is managed by a team of highly experienced investment and asset management professionals in 
the infrastructure and impact investing space, based out of Mumbai in India. The team is well supported by MAM’s 
global network on asset management, legal, ESG, HR, IT, finance and tax related matters.  
Given MAM’s prescence across India through its portfolio, the investment team has a robust mechanism of stakeholder 
management ably supported by senior management of portfolio companies, third party consultants and advisers. This 
mechanism ensures that MAM India is continuously engaged with all stakeholders, such as central and state 
government ministries and agencies, consultants / advisers, competitors through industry forums and end users, to be 
abreast of the latest trends, developments and policy changes envisaged by the government. Through its strong 
relationships across the industry, MAM India can play a vital role in the overall development of the sector and growth 
of the Indian economy. 
D.6. Efficiency and effectiveness (max`. 500 words, approximately 1 page) 
The e-mobility sector in India requires a significant amount of capital to accelerate the transition to EVs and avoid the 
multi decade technology lock-in of conventional vehicles. However, as a nascent sector, commercial investors do not 
have the risk appetite to deploy the capital at the pace required given the real and perceived investment risks outlined 
in this proposal. Although there is strong government support, the market requires additional intervention from 
concessional capital to help the market mature and create an enabling environment for large-scale commercial 
investments.  
Role of GCF capital 
Capital provided by GCF will serve to crowd-in all other investors into the Programme that would otherwise have not 
explored the opportunity to invest in the e-mobility sector in India due to their risk appetite, nascency of the sector, or 
small investment ticket size.  
The Programme aims to provide ‘first of its kind’ financing solutions in the market, such as operating and finance leases, 
through a new investment vehicle in India. GCF’s capital will sit in a ‘first-loss’ position which provides a cushion to the 
 
103 https://www.macquarie.com/assets/macq/investor/results-and-presentations/2021/macquarie-group-fy21-presentation.pdf

potential downside investment risks for commercial investors. This has been sized based on the risk appetite of potential 
investors, including what level of downside risk can be accepted by these types of investors.  
GCF’s capital will be provided in the form of an equity instrument which is able to take a high-risk position in the 
investment structure in line with GCF’s elevated risk appetite. Further, GCF’s involvement will not only increase the 
amount of capital invested in the sector, but also allow the Platform to provide financing that is not currently available 
in the market (i.e. a higher risk appetite than current financiers are able to provide). Over time, current financiers / other 
market participants will replicate the Platform’s business model and leverage its learnings to provide wide array of 
financing solutions to large number of counterparties, thus assisting in overall development of the EV ecosystem.  
Role of the Platform 
As described in this proposal, there are several interested parties and existing players (such as OEMs / operators, etc.) 
who are seeking to grow in the e-mobility space, however they are currently inhibited by high upfront costs and limited 
availability of financing solutions. This Platform, through its innovative financing solutions such as operating and 
financial leases, helps to mitigate these issues. Further, it generates efficiency by enabling OEMs / operators to help 
recycle capital which can be deployed by them to drive technological advancements, market development, growth of 
associated infrastructure within India.  
Structure and mobilisation 
Pooling of GCF and other investors outside of India establishes the waterfall of cashflow returns from the underlying 
Platform in a way that is familiar to international and large-scale investors (refer to sections B.4, C.1 and C.2 for more 
details).

E.7. Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page) 
Investment reporting 
All investors of the Platform will receive quarterly business update reports including: 
1. updates for key financings, where relevant; 
2. performance metrics; and 
3. drawdowns and distributions during the quarter. 
 
Financial reporting 
All investors of the Platform shall receive: 
1. Within 120 calendar days of the end of the relevant accounting period, annual audited accounts of the Fund for each accounting period prepared in 
accordance with the accounting principles agreed with the auditors, which shall include, without limitation, a balance sheet, profit and loss account, 
statement of changes in Investors’ capital for such period, summary of the financial information received from or in respect of investments and summary of 
material risks and their management. 
2. Quarterly reports within 60 calendar days at the end of each calendar quarter, providing an update on the performance of the Platform, a statement of 
drawdowns and distributions, anticipated capital calls during the next quarter if any, details of related party transactions, valuation, etc. 
 
ESG reporting 
The Platform will circulate with all investors an annual report on the ESG performance of all the counterparties to whom it has provided the financing solution. The 
Platform team will take inputs from the counterparties and compile the annual report for applicable reporting period as required by Investors. 
Impact reporting

=== PAGE 62 ===
E 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 58 
 
 
 
 
 
An annual impact reporting will be performed. The mid-term and final year verification of climate impact will be undertaken by the independent third party provider. 
Monitoring and evaluation arrangements 
With regards to monitoring and evaluation of the mid-term FY27 and final impact numbers (subject to modification basis exit from the Platform), an independent 
evaluator will be appointed in FY27. 
Midterm evaluations 
At the time of midterm evaluation, significant developments may not have been achieved as the Platform will take time to reach maturity. Therefore, the focus of 
the evaluation will be on the implementation of the investment strategy. This will also cover aspects like the quantum of credit extended by the Platform, GHG 
avoidance, private sector capital catalysed, and estimated number of people served. 
Final evaluation 
For the final evaluation, it is expected that development effects will be fully realised (adjusted to the exit timelines). Therefore, an external agency will be hired to 
conduct evaluation of various aspects in line with evaluation best practice such as relevance, coherence, effectiveness, efficiency, impact, and sustainability (which 
align to the OECD DAC evaluation criteria). 
• 
Relevance: the extent to which the investments are suited to the priorities and policies of the Platform. 
• 
Coherence: the interaction of the Platform with other interventions in the the e-mobility sector in India. 
• 
Effectiveness: the extent to which the objectives of the Platform were achieved. 
• 
Efficiency: the extent to which activities were cost-efficient and timely. 
 
Impact: To assess the positive and negative changes driven by the Platform, directly or indirectly, intended, or unintended because of the financing solutions:  
• 
The focus will be on local social, economic, environmental, and other development indicators as mentioned in the previous section (at output, outcome, and 
impact level). 
• 
Comparison of baseline and end line data. 
• 
Assessment of other factors which would have been influenced the impact of the Platform. 
 
Sustainability: GCF and commercial capital will build the Platform as a high quality, long lasting business which can continue to operate and move ahead towards 
its objective of decarbonising the transport sector in India, long after the Investors have exited the company.

 RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT 
F.1. Risk factors and mitigations measures (max. 3 pages)  
The risks associated with the Programme include deployment, credit / counterparty, technology, country, and foreign 
exchange risks inherent to all financial institutions providing leases (operating and financial), vehicle financing and other 
similar solutions. Whilst it is difficult to provide an exhaustive list of these risks, a summary of key risks has been detailed 
out below, which relate to financing by the Platform in the e-mobility sector in India. Specific risk factors will be assessed 
throughout the life of GCF’s investment in the Programme with strict monitoring and mitigation. However, no guarantee 
or assurance can be given that the investment objectives of the Programme will be achieved, or that other risks not 
outlined below do not apply to the program.  
For probability: High has significant probability, Medium has moderate probability, Low has negligible probability 
For impact: High has significant impact, Medium has moderate impact, Low has negligible impact 
Prohibited practices include abuse, conflict of interest, corruption, retaliation against whistleblowers or witnesses, as well as 
fraudulent, coercive, collusive, and obstructive practices 
Selected Risk Factor 1 
Deployment Risk 
Category 
Probability 
Impact 
Technical and operational 
Medium 
Medium 
Description 
MAM is currently in the process of building a strong pipeline which is in various stages of discussion, however, given 
the Platform is not viable without GCF and / or commercial capital, this risk has not been mitigated as yet. Whilst there 
is optimism about a robust deal flow and growth of the sector over the next few years, deployment of capital may be 
affected by several factors, including investment priorities of counterparties, increased competition from other financial 
institutions, change in government policies and volatility in macroeconomic factors which may have adverse impact on 
the Platform’s ability to deploy the funds.  
Mitigation Measure(s) 
MAM believes that the Platform’s financing program, once established and backed by a highly experienced 
management team with support from MAM senior professionals’, will moderate this risk. No guarantee or representation 
is provided that the Platform will be able to deploy capital and / or achieve the expected financial returns. However, 
given the program’s wide mandate, the Platform will be able to provide financing solutions to a wide range of 
counterparties across e-mobility value chain thereby reducing the risk of the Platform to deploy the committed capital 
within the agreed timeframe. The focus of the Platform will also evolve as the e-mobility market matures in India and it 
will be guided by the Board of Directors of the Platform as well as MAM senior professionals directly involved with this 
programme.  
Selected Risk Factor 2 
Credit and Counterparty Risk 
Category 
Probability 
Impact 
Credit 
High 
Medium 
Description 
There is a risk that the counterparties of the financing instruments will not be able to maintain their financing payments 
which could result in the instrument defaulting and ultimately a potential loss for investors.  
The Platform will be primarily providing financing instruments which will be generally illiquid in nature. These financing 
instruments may be difficult to sell or otherwise liquidate, particularly in stressed conditions, and as such the risk of loss 
may be greater than the risk of listed debt securities.  
Mitigation Measure(s) 
The Platform will conduct extensive due diligence on potential financing opportunities, including the creditworthiness of 
counterparties, the impact of the financing payments compared to expected revenue from use of the asset, and 
performance of the financing instrument under various stress scenarios. The Platform will seek to target contracts with 
credible counterparties with long term visibility to cashflows (preferably over life of the investment). A risk buffer to loss 
from anyone financing instrument or counterparty will be provided by diversification across different financing 
instruments, sub-sectors, and counterparties. The Platform will apply its robust monitoring process described in section 
B.3 – Programme Description to manage the risk of default and deliver the best outcome for the Platform and ultimately

=== PAGE 65 ===
E 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 61 
 
 
 
 
the investors. It is anticipated that any re-possessed assets will be redeployed, although the terms of redeployment 
may vary on a case by case basis. 
Selected Risk Factor 3 
Technology Risk 
Category 
Probability 
Impact 
Technical and operational 
High 
Medium 
Description 
The e-mobility sector is still in its nascency in most markets, particularly in India wherein technology across the entire 
EV value chain is still evolving. Thus, there is a substantial risk that the Platform may provide financing for EVs or other 
sub-sectors based on currently available technology, which is then superseded, reducing any asset value held by the 
Platform or by counterparties. This could reduce financial returns for investors or the credit quality of counterparties.  
Mitigation Measure(s) 
The Platform will apply its technical expertise and leverage MAM’s experience in conducting detailed due diligence on 
underlying technology, monitoring the latest technical advancements and trends in the sector, and analysing any 
potential impact on the financing instruments before taking any exposure. Further, whilst pricing the financial solutions, 
particularly leases, the Platform will try to account for any technology risk through a detailed estimation of expected 
residual value at the end of the lease term, which will provide the Platform the necessary cushion against any 
accelerated obsolescene of the EV technology.  
Spearately, this development in technology would also mean that there has been positive advancements in EV 
technology which could support higher EV penetration and help meet the Programme’s objectives faster.  
Selected Risk Factor 4  
Country and Regulatory Risk 
Category 
Probability 
Impact 
Governance 
Low 
Medium 
Description 
Any detrimental change in laws and regulations relating to the EV sector, methods and rules of taxation, compliance, 
foreign investments, restrictions on currency conversion, imports, etc. may impact the Platform’s ability to provide 
financing solutions at attractive terms, impact its ability to deploy capital and / or restrict it to provide financing solutions 
to certain e-mobility sub-sectors.  
Mitigation Measure(s) 
The Platform aims to provide financing instruments that are aligned to government priorities in the e-mobility sector and 
development of local industry. The government has put in place long-term policies to support the ongoing growth of the 
sector which should continue to support deployment opportunities in the sector, viability of the business models and 
creditworthiness of the financing counterparties. The Platform’s ability to deploy across the e-mobility value chain will 
provide diversification against the risk of regulation or policies changing in any particular sub-sector. 
Selected Risk Factor 5 
Exit Risk 
Category 
Probability 
Impact 
Other 
Medium 
Medium 
Description 
There is a risk that, at the point of GCF’s expected exit from the program, the Platform cannot be sold down to another 
investor due to poor performance of underlying financing instruments, credit worthiness of the financing counterparties, 
high technology and regulatory risks, unfavourable market conditions and unattractive pricing. 
Mitigation Measure(s) 
The Platform intends to build a diversified portfolio in terms of exposure to multiple e-mobility sub-sectors, 
counterparties and financing solutions, which mitigates the concentration risk if any and acts as a hedge against any 
foreseen risk. Apart from this, the Platform will be led by a highly experienced management with strong background in 
screening and evaluating potential opportunities and performance monitoring. Further, the Platform will implement best 
in class governance practices and will have strong oversight and support from MAM, who have significant experience 
in developing and executing an exit strategy for the benefit of investors. The risk is also managed by the expected

=== PAGE 66 ===
E 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 62 
 
 
 
 
growth in the sector ajnd strong policy alignment, which provide strong fundamentals for market development and 
create a case for a robust exit for the investors. 
Selected Risk Factor 6  
Bribery an Corruption Risk 
Category 
Probability 
Impact 
Reputational 
Low 
Low 
Description 
Senior management of the financing counterparties may be involved in committing fraud or the adoption of other 
deceptive practices which may 1) have a negative impact on Platform’s reputation, 2) stifle further debt and equity 
capital flow into the sector, and 3) undermine the Platform’s due diligence efforts and its ability to generate desired 
returns from the asset. 
Mitigation Measure(s) 
Macquarie Group has a robust Bribery and Corruption framework which will be implemented by the Platform as part of 
upfront establishment. Upfront and ongoing monitoring of financing counterparties will be performed, including 
enhanced due diligence where required. Compliance with the Bribery and Corruption policies and procedures will be 
reported to the Platform Board and to the Accredited Entity on an ongoing basis.  
The Platform will establish a project-level grievance mechanism which will be available for the reporting of complaints 
and allegations of impropriety, wrong-doing or other related issues in the project. This will be made available on the 
GCF and AE website once established. 
Selected Risk Factor 7  
Foreign Exchange Risk 
Category 
Probability 
Impact 
Forex 
Low 
Low 
Description 
The GCF will make commitments in USD, however the Platform will receive investments and provide financing in INR 
terms. There is a risk that the exchange rate volatility may result in a lower amount of investment capital being available 
in INR terms to the Platform, or returns to GCF and commercial investors are negatively impacted at the time of exit. 
Mitigation Measure(s) 
No hedging instruments will be put in place for equity investments made by the investors in the Program. MAM believes 
that the Programme will be able to generate robust returns for investors both in INR and USD terms, and will be able 
to easily absorb INR’s depreciation vis-à-vis USD, which historically has been in the range of 2.5-3.5% over a 5 year 
period.  
In case, SG Master Co., SG Co. and / or the Platform undertake USD denominated currency debt then the respective 
entities may have to undertake a hedging instrument as per the lenders’ requirements. Any such requirements / 
conditions will be actively managed and monitored by the Platform’s management team. 
Selected Risk Factor 8 
AML/CTF 
Category 
Probability 
Impact 
ML/FT 
Low 
Medium 
Description 
There are risks associated with financing counterparties which could have links to terrorism, money laundering or 
subject to sanctions. Instances of these practices may undermine the Programme in achieving its aims both from an 
operational and reputational perspective. 
Mitigation Measure(s) 
The Platform will conduct the day-to-day management of Anti Money Laundering (AML) / Counter Terrorism Financing 
(CTF) and sanctions in line with the requirements set out in subsidiary agreements, which will flow down GCF 
requirements under the AMA and FAA. The AE will monitor compliance with GCF’s requirements under these 
agreements. As entities managed by MAM, the Executing Entities will be also subject to Macquarie policies which align 
to international best practices. These policies were reviewed by GCF as part of the accreditation process for MAAML. 
In line with Macquarie policies and the existing AMA between MAAML and GCF: 
• 
Programme activities will not be undertaken in any jurisdiction which is subject to, or affected by, United Nations 
Security Council resolutions. 
• 
No individual or entity that is listed on any UN Security Council sanctions list (including the UN Consolidated 
Sanctions list) will be involved in any manner with the project or its activities either as a counterparty, 
implementer, or beneficiary.

The programme will establish the Platform, which will provide financing within the e-mobility sector. There may be 
environmental and social risks associated with sub-projects as outlined in section G1 and the ESMS. 
Mitigation Measure(s) 
The Platform will implement robust governance, policies and procedures to identify, diligence and manage E&S risks 
in direct operations, in the investment process and monitoring of underlying sub-projects. The Platform will assess the

Selected Risk Factor 10 
Gender Risk 
Category 
Probability 
Impact 
Reputational 
Low 
Medium 
Description 
The Platform will be established and invest in sub-projects in the e-mobility sector which may have different impacts on 
women and men. There are identified impacts and opportunities for gender mainstreaming at a national and sector 
level (transport). However, there is limited sex-disaggregated data available in the e-mobility sector or transport sector 
more broadly to determine some potential risks or impacts. There is a risk that the Programme has an unexpected, 
outsized negative impact on women or that gender mainstreaming is not promoted as effectively as possible. 
Mitigation Measure(s) 
There are identified areas in the Gender Action Plan where gender mainstreaming activities can be implemented, 
including the focus areas of improving knowledge within e-mobility sector on gender sensitivity, safety to prevent 
gender-based violence, and opportunities to provide employment opportunities. Other actions will be identified over 
time through stakeholder engagement with women’s groups, financing counterparties and the result of sex-
disaggregated data.
