 GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS 
G.1. Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)  
Governance of E&S 
E&S considerations will form an intrinsic part of the Platform’s operations and will be considered at all stages of the 
investment process. This will include consideration of E&S risks and impacts that are more prevalent in the e-mobility 
sector (such as materials or supply chain factors) or specific transaction types (such as heightened counterparty and 
procurement risks for leasing structures).  
As a financing intervention, the Platform will not be able to directly implement activities regarding the operation of the 
EVs. However, this is mitigated through upfront diligence and structuring by the Platform as part of a transaction and 
by activities flowed through to the financing counterparty. 
Financing counterparties will be involved in the implementation and management of E&S in sub-projects, including 
complying with any requirements included in the financing documentation set by the Platform or ESMP developed as 
part of the sub-project development. The Platform will be responsible for considering the counterparty’s capacity to 
implement E&S management, what requirements are integrated into financing documentation to meet GCF and 
international standards, and overseeing that counterparties appropriately implement these requirements.  
A dedicated E&S specialist will be hired by the Platform to lead the implementation of the Environmental and Social 
Management System (ESMS) at the Platform level. They will also provide or arrange training for Platform staff and, 
where applicable, other stakeholders such as financing counterparties. 
The Platform will have oversight from MAM, which has dedicated risk and sustainability teams that are responsible for 
setting and implementing MAM’s ESG strategy and framework, including the management and operations of the 
Platform. 
1. MAM’s risk team is responsible for ensuring all operational risks, including ESG risks, are identified and 
managed. Reporting to the MAM Chief Risk Officer, MAM’s risk team is independent of MAM’s portfolio 
management functions and is supported by specialist expertise within MAM, including the MAM sustainability 
team and Macquarie Group risk functions.  
2. MAM’s sustainability team is responsible for setting MAM’s overall sustainability strategy and ESG framework, 
providing specialist expertise on environmental and certain social issues, and supporting asset management 
teams in harnessing ESG opportunities across the portfolio. 
Supported by centralised policies and processes, MAM’s risk and sustainability teams support MAM’s transaction and 
asset management teams to effectively address ESG risks and opportunities throughout each phase of the lifecycle. 
E&S risk assessment and classification 
The focus of the Programme is establishing a Platform to provide financing to predominately corporate counterparties 
(in the initial years) for electric vehicles, building charging infrastructure and / or other investments along the e-mobility 
value chain. The Programme has been categorised as Intermediation-2 to reflect that the Platform will act as an 
intermediary to the operation of the e-mobility investments, which will have maximum categorisation of Category B, 
based on an assessment using the Macquarie’s ESR Toolkit which is based on IFC Performance Standards.  
The key E&S risks for each sub-project that have contributed to this classification are outlined below, along with why 
their impacts are limited or mitigated. For the avoidance of doubt, this is not an exhaustive list of considerations. Each 
sub-project will be reviewed on a case-by-case basis to ensure E&S risks are considered holistically. 
 
Capacity of the 
financing 
counterparties 
(PS1: Risk 
Management) 
The counterparty will be involved in direct implementation of E&S management for sub-
projects. There is a risk that the counterparty does not have the capacity or the governance in 
place to manage potential E&S risks.  
The Platform will review the policies, procedures and capacity of the counterparty as part of 
its upfront due diligence, and will monitor the implementation of E&S management as part of 
the ongoing monitoring of the sub-projects.

=== PAGE 69 ===
F 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 65 
 
 
 
 
Air quality and 
indirect 
emissions 
(PS3: Resource 
efficiency) 
EVs do not have any emission from its operations as only electricity is consumed. This is a 
significant advantage over the petrol / diesel driven vehicles as vehicular emission contributes 
to a significant proportion of pollution in all major cities, including India. 
Indirect emissions will arise in the generation of power, which is used for charging of batteries 
in the EVs, given India’s grid still has reliance on conventional energy sources. However, this 
will be mitigated through India’s ambitious program to decarbonise its grid over time through 
the integration more renewables, which will support the decarbonisation of the transport sector 
alongside electrification. In this way, the impacts will decrease over time. Opportunities to 
encourage green-only charging points will also be explored. 
The Platform will use best efforts to investigate and pursue commercially feasible opportunities 
to reduce the indirect emissions associated with its operations. This may include installation 
of onsite renewables (e.g., rooftop solar), dispatchable storage, procurement of renewable 
electricity. 
The Platform will incorporate into its policies and stakeholder management program the 
support of and participation in government programs that support grid decarbonisation. 
Hazardous waste 
(including e-
waste) 
(PS3: Resource 
efficiency) 
EVs do not require handling of hazardous materials because they do not contain engine oil 
and similar lubricants, which are associated with conventional vehicles.  
However, lithium-ion batteries that include other chemical moieties like cobalt, graphite and 
nickel are an important component of an EV. At the end of the battery lifespan, there is a 
battery waste, comprising significant amounts of chemicals such as cobalt, electrolytes, 
lithium, manganese oxide and nickel. These substances, if not recycled or treated adequately, 
can contaminate soil and groundwater. Further, lithium itself spontaneously reacts with 
moisture and can lead to major landfill explosions. 
As a nascent sector, there are limited regulatory frameworks for the end-of-life treatment or 
recycling of e-waste related to EVs, however these are rapidly evolving and options for re-use, 
recycling and safe disposal are being developed globally. Although current policies related to 
e-waste in India do not currently include guidance on the safe disposal of lithium-ion batteries, 
the Platform will align to international best practice in the disposal of the batteries for each 
sub-project.  
Regulations and best practices in the nascent e-waste management sector are rapidly 
evolving. It is recognised that the e-waste management approach adopted by the Platform and 
sub-projects will be subject to these evolving regulations and best practices.  
Generally, the Platform will seek that commercially feasible best practices and e-waste 
management principles are employed to minimise adverse environmental and social impacts 
associated with used batteries. An e-waste management strategy relevant for each sub-project 
will be developed.  
The Platform will develop a vehicle scrappage policy aligned with government guidelines and 
will require counterparties to comply with the same. Government approved scrappage facilities 
with focus on recycling and reusing materials recovered from the scrappage. 
Supply chain and 
labour issues 
(PS2: Labour, PS4: 
Community) 
As with any manufacturing sector, the supply chain can be prone to E&S issues if working with 
non-reputable counterparties, such as child labour, SEAH, inadequate working conditions, 
health and safety lapses and corruption. Required standards and compliance can vary from 
country to country and across different counterparties. There are known supply chain issues 
related to mining of lithium-iron and other materials for batteries, which involves extraction of 
metals from jurisdictions where E&S regulations may not be as strict. 
The Platform will conduct due diligence on counterparties, including their labour practices and 
supply chain, as part of the upfront assessment of a transaction which would impact on the 
ultimate financing decision to proceed. This will mitigate against any significant adverse social 
impacts that would otherwise require a Category A classification such that the Platform only 
works with credible counterparties with adequate policies and measure in place to manage 
these risks.  
The Platform intends to mitigate this through the following outcomes: 
• 
A Board-approved human rights policy that outlines the Platform’s commitment to 
avoiding causing or contributing to adverse human rights impacts (including modern

=== PAGE 70 ===
F 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 66 
 
 
 
 
slavery) and the Platform’s commitment to seek to prevent or mitigate adverse human 
rights impacts directly linked to the Platform’s operations, products or services. The 
human rights policy will also support the UN Guiding Principles on Business and 
Human Rights, the OECD Guidelines for Multinational Enterprises and the ILO 
Fundamental Conventions and Recommendations. 
• 
Undertaking supply chain due diligence for select suppliers of the proposed entity. 
• 
Excluding suppliers with negative reports. 
• 
Each sub-project will have in place, commensurate with its human rights risk profile, a 
process to screen and undertake due diligence on suppliers to a human rights risk 
ascertain track record. 
• 
Actively engage in stakeholder consultations regarding regulatory developments in this 
sector to support greater transparency and controls. 
• 
Participating in workshops and trainings with financing counterparties regarding 
supplier governance and best practices in relation to this issue. 
Social impacts 
(PS4: Community) 
The addition of EVs to public transport will see new buses with better features and quality of 
transportation. Advanced safety features, on-board cameras, universal access, etc. are 
expected to benefit society in general. However, as the transition to EVs continues, there may 
be unintended societal impacts, such as requirements for reskilling of jobs in the conventional 
sector. These are not expected to be direct impacts from sub-projects as a result of the 
financing provided. As such, the impacts are expected to be minimal. However, the approach 
of the financing counterparty to managing the transition of their staff (where applicable) will be 
considered as part of the upfront assessment and diligence.  
The Platform intends to mitigate this through the following outcomes: 
• 
Consider, as part of the planning phase of each sub-project, the social impacts, 
including job creation for new / reskilled workers, improved amenity and aesthetics due 
to reduced noise and pollution, enhanced low-cost mobility and connectivity for 
communities and improved quality of life and security for customers. 
• 
Review any likelihood of significant retrenchment as a result of the transaction 
(considered unlikely). 
• 
Support or be involved in workshops and training for the e-mobility sector organised 
by financing counterparties. 
Gender-based 
violence 
(including SEAH) 
(PS4: Community) 
Gender-based violence and the related safety considerations in public transport are known 
issues in India. There is potential for gender-based violence to occur on the buses or other 
vehicles financed by the Platform. This is not within the control of the Platform, however there 
are a number of safety regulations, such as CCTV cameras, panic buttons, etc., that are 
required. The Platform will seek to monitor the compliance of vehicles in meeting these 
regulations to mitigate against significant impacts from this risk. The regulations, best practice, 
obligations and other mitigating features will be reviewed as part of each sub-project to limit 
the impacts of this risk. 
Mitigants for these risks are outlined in more detail in the Gender Action Plan. Some examples 
from the Gender Action Plan include: 
• 
the Platform will review the GBVH policies of counterparties as part of the upfront due 
diligence; 
• 
the Platform will monitor compliance with requirements in e-buses regarding safety 
features such as emergency call button, CCTV cameras, easy access for disabled; 
and 
• 
the Platfrom will support initiatives such as increasing number of woman conductors 
or drivers on buses or women only buses. 
Land 
procurement 
It is envisaged that, in rare cases, sub-projects that will be considered for financing may involve 
land procurement, such as the setting up of charging infrastructure. The footprint is likely to be 
small. Resettlement, significant biodiversity impacts or impacts on indigenous peoples or 
cultural heritage are not expected due to the nature of the size and location of charging points,

=== PAGE 71 ===
F 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 67 
 
 
 
 
(PS5: Land 
resettlement, PS6: 
Biodiversity, PS7: 
Indigenous 
peoples, PS8: 
Cultural heritage) 
targeting predominately urban environments where there is likely existing commercial use 
where charging is convenient for users. Where resettlement is required, a Resettlement Action 
Plan will be developed using the principles outlined in the ESMS for this Programme. 
Any limited biodiversity, indigenous peoples or cultural heritage impacts identified in E&S 
diligence will need have mitigating actions in the ESMP for that sub-project. 
Any major resettlement, biodiversity, indigenous peoples or cultural heritage would be 
classified as a Category A project or not able to be mitigated and the Platform will not invest 
in these activities. 
 
Integration into investment processing and monitoring of underlying sub-projects 
E&S risks will be assessed during the due diligence process and feed through into the analysis for all transactions. 
A robust E&S due diligence process will ensure that these risks are identified and appropriately incorporated into the 
financing decision. Integrating E&S into the process will ensure that the fundamental and credit analysis of the 
underlying financing captures all risks and potential impacts, to ensure the borrower or lessee’s ability to repay its 
obligations is effectively assessed. A summary of E&S risks, mitigants, classification and actions will be prepared for 
each sub-project as part of the financing decision and will be approved as part of each project.  
The Platform will screen opportunities using Macquarie Group’s Environmental and Social Risk Assessment Tool (ESR 
Tool). The environmental and social criteria and categorisation are based on International Finance Corporation (IFC) 
performance standards. The system is kept up to date in line with industry best practice. The results of the ESR Tool 
will be used to shape the scope of due diligence (generally tailored to the location, sector, and risk profile of the 
opportunity) and identify material ESG risks and information gaps relevant to the financing opportunity. The ESR Tool 
provides a risk categorisation based on IFC performance standards as Category A, B or C. This categorisation will also 
be approved as part of the financing decision.  
The Platform will monitor sub-projects against risks identified during upfront due diligence, in addition to against broader 
sectoral changes and themes, to identify new or emerging risks over the lifetime of the financing. Where (if any) a 
material E&S risk is identified, issues will be escalated to the Platform’s Board immediately. 
G.2. Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)  
MAM has completed a gender assessment study and developed a Platform level Gender Action Plan for the India e-
mobility Financing Program. Refer to Annex 8 – Gender assessment and programme level action plan for more details.  
It is understood that gender related issues in the e-mobility sector are common to those in the broader urban mobility 
sector in India. Issues are specific to gender-based violence and harassment, economic disparity, and limited public 
transport options. However, recent flow of capital in safe and well-designed transport infrastructure can increase 
economic empowerment of women by improving mobility and access to better avenues of employment, education, and 
healthcare. Introduction of new electric vehicles fleets replacing older public transportation systems will enhance the 
safety features due to the required design modifications under tender and concession contracts. Therefore, the growth 
of the e-mobility sector and sub-sectors has the potential to lead to changes in gender dimensions by supporting safer 
public transportation options. Moreover, as a new industry, there is potential to promote the implementation of gender 
inclusive design and practice, as well as applying broader workforce policies, such as bridging the pay gap, involvement 
of more technically qualified women and opening more avenues of employment for women. 
The key issues identified in the gender assessment, and the actions the Platform will implement to address these 
issues, are outlined in the table below.

G.3. Financial management and procurement (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page) 
MAM will be responsible for the monitoring and review of Platform performance. It will work closely with the Platform’s 
management team to define their operating and financial policies and processes, and ensure compliance through 
industry leading MAM asset management standards. MAM, through Board representation, will participate in key 
financial, commercial and governance matters of the Platform. Further, it will seek monthly reviews with the Platform’s 
management team to discuss business updates / progress, review financial performance, ESG updates and review of 
governance, commercial and other operational matters. On a quarterly basis, the investment team will also conduct a 
detailed formal analysis of the performance of the Platform for the purpose of reporting to the GCF and commercial 
investors.  
MAM will seek following reporting from the Platform’s management team: 
• 
monthly reporting on financial and operational metrics; 
• 
reporting on E&S matters;

=== PAGE 74 ===
F 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 70 
 
 
 
 
• 
quarterly Board meetings; 
• 
regular site visits; 
• 
compliance with shareholders agreement; and 
• 
annual audited accounts. 
 
MAM will submit following reports to the LP Investors: 
• 
quarterly investor report (including Platform performance and financial information at consolidated LP level); 
• 
annual audited accounts and NAV statements; 
• 
annual ESS report including annual climate and sustainability impact assessment; and 
• 
GCF specific mid-term and final assessment reports. 
 
Note that ESG will be carried out as per GCF ESS Policy and IFC Performance Standards. 
Procurement 
Procurement of goods and services at LP, SG Master Co., SG Co. and Platform levels will be carried out in accordance 
with the AE’s policies and regulations as agreed under the AMA.  
One time setup costs relating to LP, SG Master Co. and SG Co., and highly commoditised services requiring minimal 
professional expertise, will be procured on Quality Cost Based Selection basis. Various professional / consultation 
services required on an annual basis will be procured on Quality Based Selection basis.

not be disclosed in full by the GCF, as certain information is being provided in confidence. Accordingly, the accredited 
entity is providing to the Secretariat the following two copies of the funding proposal, including all annexes: 
• 
full copy for internal use of the GCF in which the confidential portions are marked accordingly, together with an 
explanatory note regarding the said portions and the corresponding reason for confidentiality under the 
accredited entity’s disclosure policy, and 
• 
redacted copy for disclosure on the GCF website.  
 
The funding proposal can only be processed upon receipt of the two copies above, if containing confidential 
information.

=== PAGE 85 ===
GCF/B.32/02/Add.03 
Page 81 
 
Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment of 
FP186 
Proposal name: 
India E-Mobility Financing Program 
Accredited entity: 
Macquarie Alternative Assets Management Limited (MAAML)  
Project/programme size: 
Large 
I. 
 Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel  
1.1 Impact potential 
  
 
 
                    Scale: Medium-High 
1. 
Overview - The E-Mobility Financing Platform funding proposal (Platform) assessed 
here is proposed by Macquarie Alternative Assets Management Limited (MAAML) as an 
accredited entity (AE). This is a mitigation programme that focuses on promoting sustainable 
and electrified road transport in India. GCF is requested to provide USD 200 million in equity, as 
first loss capital to de-risk commercial investors, leading to a total commitment of USD 405 
million towards an E-mobility Financing Platform. The programme aims to leverage the GCF and 
commercial capital to accelerate decarbonisation of the Indian economy by focusing on the 
electrification of India’s transport sector. It will do this by providing financing solutions 
intended to bridge the overall cost of ownership between conventional vehicles and electric 
vehicles (EVs). 
2. 
Climate change is one of the biggest challenges faced by India. Between 1901–2018, 
India’s average temperature rose by 0.7°C due to increased atmospheric concentrations of 
greenhouse gases (GHG). India has also seen a rapid growth in its own GHG emissions resulting 
from multiple factors such as high population growth and increasing levels of industrialization. 
It is projected that temperatures will continue to rise in India resulting in greater frequency of 
extreme climatic events, vector-borne diseases, food shortage, migration, and loss of livelihoods, 
all of which will have adverse impacts on India’s social and economic development.  
3. 
In response, the Government of India has been pursuing various mitigation and 
adaptation activities including policy support for renewable energy and electric mobility 
sectors, to reduce GHG emissions. However, the scale of investment needed to help India 
achieve its climate goals as reflected in its nationally determined contribution (NDC) under the 
Paris Agreement will require the mobilization of funds beyond the public treasury. Sectors that 
can produce mitigation impacts such as the electric mobility sector will need to attract private 
capital at scale and in an accelerated manner. According to some estimates, India will need 
investment of approximately USD 2.5 trillion between 2015 and 2030 to meet its NDC by 2030. 
4. 
In the above context, the aim of the Platform is to facilitate the reduction of GHGs and 
other emissions from the road transport sector in India through accelerated adoption of electric 
vehicles. The funding proposal presents the creation of a Platform that would deploy targeted 
investments (in the form of operating and finance leases and other structured credit solutions) 
across the e-mobility value chain to overcome barriers that have historically limited the sector. 
India has the potential to be one of the largest electric vehicle markets in the world, but market 
growth has been hampered by high upfront capital expenditure associated with EVs, 
impediments around EV charging infrastructure and uncertainty around commercial EV 
performance. The funding proposal is intended to be complementary to Indian government 
efforts to accelerate its nascent e-mobility market by attracting new funding sources into the 
space, addressing market barriers, and financing the underlying infrastructure needed to scale 
up the EV sector.

=== PAGE 86 ===
GCF/B.32/02/Add.03 
Page 82 
 
5. 
The funding proposal seeks USD 200 million from GCF and an additional USD 205 
million from commercial investors to capitalize the E-mobility Financing Platform. It will be 
managed by MAAML which is part of Macquarie Asset Management (MAM), one of the world’s 
largest infrastructure asset managers, with an established presence in India. The proposal 
further envisages accessing local and international debt markets for approximately an 
additional USD 1.1 billion in debt leverage.  
6. 
Initially the Platform will focus on select segments of the EV ecosystem such as e-buses, 
shared fleet and electric charging infrastructure and will over time seek to diversify its portfolio 
by expanding into the other segments (including retail) as more subsectors within the EV value 
chain gain scale and prominence. Over the implementation period, MAAML expects the Platform 
to mobilize up to USD 2.1 billion of capital into the sector and reduce ~9.5 MtCO2eq over the 
project lifetime.  
7. 
Impacts - The FP and the accredited entity have been able to credibly demonstrate that 
decarbonising the transport sector in India can help to reduce the impacts related to GHG 
emissions and other air pollutants. Additionally, the Platform can generate several additional 
potential co-benefits, such as major improvements in public health, particularly in urban areas, 
improved fuel savings and reduced noise pollution.  
8. 
Electrification of vehicles plays an essential role in decarbonising the transport sector as 
it offers the most promising GHG emission abatement potential relative to other alternatives. In 
2021, India became the world’s third largest passenger vehicle market with the sector 
representing an increasing source of GHG emissions and other pollutants. Transportation 
activity reached over 6 trillion passenger kilometres, which represents three times increase 
from 2000 to 2020. This trend is expected to continue unabated as approximately 60 million 
more households are expected to enter the consuming class by 2025. At the same time, the 
transport sector in India is reliant on fossil fuels and contributes significantly to oil import 
demand. Transportation accounts for 20 per cent of the final energy use in India, and has grown 
by 50 per cent since 2010, comprising primarily oil products (95 per cent). India is presently the 
third-largest consumer of oil and fourth-largest oil refiner with well understood implications for 
India’s energy security.  
9. 
Transportation is the third most GHG emitting sector, contributing about 13 per cent of 
the total carbon dioxide emissions in India. Due to the dimension and scale of the Indian 
economy, its emissions from transport alone are equivalent to the total emissions of a mid-sized 
European country. Therefore, accelerating decarbonization of the Indian transportation sector 
(moving to EVs and away from fossil fuels and internal combustion engines) could significantly 
reduce emissions and generate co-benefits with substantial impact. However, a mix of financial, 
behavioural and infrastructure incentives is needed to develop the e-mobility sector and 
associated infrastructure. The funding proposal credibly demonstrates that the Platform could 
help achieve this target.  
10. 
Currently both EV market acceptance and EV industry dynamics are at an early stage. 
The EV adoption rate is less than 1 per cent and the domestic Original Equipment Manufacturer 
(OEM) are just starting to launch EV models. For instance, although India is the world’s second 
biggest bus market with approximately 100,000 units sold annually, over 95 per cent of them 
are fossil fuel reliant. At the same time, this represents a large market sector that could be 
converted to EVs if market financing barriers are addressed, and it is not surprising that the 
Platform proposes an initial focus on the passenger bus subsector of transportation. 
11. 
The independent Technical Advisory Panel (TAP) notes that from a GHG emissions 
standpoint, a successful shift away from fossil fuel vehicles to EVs is dependent on the GHG 
intensity of the relevant electric grid. In the case of India, coal still accounts for approximately 
57 per cent of the electricity mix. EV transportation will require a source of electricity for 
charging. The underlying source of electricity generation will affect the actual emissions 
avoided by the programme.

=== PAGE 87 ===
GCF/B.32/02/Add.03 
Page 83 
 
12. 
However, we also recognize that India has committed to an ambitious deployment 
programme of renewable energy resources and has already achieved its 2030 target of deriving 
40 per cent of the installed capacity from non-fossil fuels. The total non-fossil based installed 
electricity capacity stands at 156.83 GW, which is 40.1 per cent of the total installed electricity 
capacity. At the UN Climate Change Conference in 2021 (COP26), India announced a goal of 
reaching 500 GW of non-fossil fuel capacity and meeting 50 per cent of its energy requirements 
from renewable sources. All indications are that India is committed to a decarbonization path 
for its grid, but the scale of potential impact of this funding proposal will be heavily influenced 
by how rapidly and how deeply India’s energy generation is decarbonized. The underlying 
assumption in the funding proposal is that there will be increased penetration of renewables in 
the electricity grid and/or availability of charging sites using only renewables. The proposal 
intends to contribute to this decarbonization by encouraging its counterparties to use 
renewable energy for charging stations, by providing appropriate financial incentives linked to 
the carbon intensity of the energy utilized. 
13. 
The Platform expects to finance an estimated 18,000 e-buses (72.8 per cent of the total 
capital deployment), 22,000 four-wheel vehicles (7.5 per cent of the total capital deployment), 
65,000 three and two-wheel vehicles (3.5 per cent of the total capital deployment) and plans to 
invest and establish 10,500 charging stations over the life of the programme. As a result, over 10 
years the Platform is expected to reduce approximately 9.5 million tons of carbon dioxide 
equivalent over the project lifetime from the replacement of internal combustion engine 
vehicles across India by the funding of comparable electric transportation vehicles and by 
funding electric charging infrastructure. 
14. 
The assumptions made in the funding proposal on the pace of grid decarbonization are 
less aggressive than India’s 2030 commitment of 500GW of energy consumption coming from 
renewables. If India achieves its 2030 targets, then actual impacts could be higher. Alternatively, 
the Platform may end up underachieving the impact targets if a lower proportion of 
counterparties use renewable energy for charging stations, and the decarbonization of the grid 
decelerates.  
15. 
In addition to GHG mitigation impacts, millions of people are expected to directly benefit 
from better air quality during the lifetime of the programme and beyond.  
16. 
Another area of independent TAP concern involves the probable scale of the funding 
proposal programme. The speed and scale of the programme assumes that it can leverage, in 
addition to GCF funds, USD 205 million equity co-financing to be mobilized from commercial 
investors and that the platform will also raise substantial third-party debt (primarily from 
domestic sources) in the ratio of 1: 1 (debt: equity). This amount of co-financing will be vital for 
the Platform to achieve its intended climate impact. Based on the AEs demonstrated experience, 
early market soundings and its market feasibility study, we are sufficiently satisfied that these 
targets for raising capital can be achieved (with the catalysing effect of GCF funding), however, 
final realization may be difficult or delayed, due to unforeseeable market factors and shortage of 
liquidity in the debt capital market.  
17. 
We also note that the speed and scale proposed in the funding proposal rely on 
continued support from the Government of India for the EV sector and the continuing 
programme of regular government tenders for EV buses, charging infrastructures and other e-
mobility subsectors. Thus, continued government support is critical for the programme and any 
major policy change on the part of the Government could be detrimental to expected impacts. 
However, we have been sufficiently satisfied that support of the e-mobility sector is embedded 
in India’s long-term policy. 
18. 
Overall impact potential is assessed as Medium-High. The realistic possibility of 
materially contributing to the accelerated decarbonization of the Indian transport sector allows 
for a high rating. However, the relatively high percentage of GCF funding required to mobilize 
other equity co-investment and uncertainties about the pace of India’s efforts to decarbonize 
the power grid pushes this funding proposal into the territory of Medium-High.

=== PAGE 88 ===
GCF/B.32/02/Add.03 
Page 84 
 
1.2 
Paradigm shift potential 
 
 
 
                    Scale: High 
19. 
The E-mobility Financing Platform aims to support the acceleration of India’s EV sector 
as a key mechanism in decarbonizing the transport sector. By enhancing the availability of 
credit and by introducing new financial solutions in the EV market that accept operational 
performance and other risks, the programme seeks to make EV ownership more attractive, help 
OEMs recycle capital faster and cause electric vehicles to become the preferred choice and mode 
of transport among fleet operators.  
20. 
Financing solutions provided by the Platform will aim to create a paradigm shift within 
the Indian context by supporting the private sector to drive forward the e-mobility sector which 
can serve as an example for other similar markets. The Platform is expected to directly cause 9.5 
MtCO2eq in emissions reductions. Moreover, by demonstrating the commercial viability of the 
EV sector and financing critical EV infrastructure, the Platform can help catalyse a sector that 
would result in even greater emission reductions outside the Platform envelope and support a 
growth pathway that is consistent with national climate strategies. In this way, the paradigm 
shift potential is embedded in the potential for scaling up the sector and replication of the EV 
financing tools across India, and possibly other developing countries that could benefit from e-
mobility gains.  
21. 
By 2027, India will be the most populous country on the planet with nearly 1.5 billion 
people. At the same time India’s middle class is set to grow rapidly from around 80 million 
today to 580 million people by 2025. This growth is expected to drive significant demand for 
transportation capacity, creating a substantial opportunity to meet this growth with lower 
carbon options to the combustion engine. This opportunity is reflected at the passenger, 
commercial and mass transport subsectors of mobility. This transition will require the supply of 
finance targeting EVs and finance for the infrastructure of e-mobility. According to the funding 
proposal, the estimated size of the EV finance market is expected to reach USD 50 billion in 
2030. The Platform could serve a paradigm-shifting role by demonstrating financial viability, 
attracting new sources of capital to the sector and funding infrastructure improvements.  
22. 
The Platform also has high potential for replicability. The proposal aims to develop a 
leasing solution to address the existing barriers. Leasing as a model for the EV sector in India 
has several advantages. First, the residual value and other perceived risks of EV ownership are 
absorbed by the Platform as opposed to end users. This could accelerate take-up of leasing but 
more importantly from a paradigm-shifting perspective, demonstrate for customers the 
difference between actual financial risk and perceived risk. Second, leasing allows EV 
acquisition costs to be spread over a longer tenor. In this way, leasing mitigates EV acquisition 
barriers associated with the higher upfront cost of most EVs.  
23. 
The Platform also intends to contribute to greater knowledge-sharing in India by the 
accredited entity regularly engaging with Indian government institutions (national and state 
level) and providing inputs on how to increase the effectiveness and impact of policies/ 
regulations enacted. According to MAAML, they along with various MAM and Platform 
representatives will work with the Government to slowly bring down the subsidies and other 
tax incentives over time as the sector achieves maturity and substantial crowding in of private 
capital which would help achieve parity across various segments of the e-mobility value chain.  
24. 
The Platform will also work with several large OEMs, to provide leasing solutions to the 
EV operators. These financial solutions priced at attractive rates along with long tenors will 
assist counterparties in de-leveraging their balance sheets and recycling their limited capital, 
thereby encouraging further investments in local technological advancements. The Platform will 
also provide capital to other actors in the EV value chain, as the market evolves, especially in the 
areas of charging infrastructure.  
25. 
By supporting the long-term growth of the EV sector, the Platform should contribute to 
reducing the need for concessional or public funding over time. For instance, the financing of 
accelerated deployment of charging infrastructure can reduce behavioural barriers (such as

=== PAGE 89 ===
GCF/B.32/02/Add.03 
Page 85 
range anxiety for consumers). Supporting key infrastructure has been shown to be a leading 
contributor to greater EV market penetration in more mature markets. 
26.
By raising capital in local markets and engaging with local financial institutions as well
as international and national equity providers, the Platform could contribute to familiarizing
and embedding new finance providers in the e-mobility sector in India.
27.
The proposed financing platform also brings to India MAM’s best practices on
incorporating climate due diligencein the financing of the e-mobility sector. From the
perspective of managing climate-related physical (e.g., flooding, elevated temperatures) and/or
transition risks which can affect their subprojects under the financing programme, MAAML is
aware of such risks and together with the broader Macquarie Asset Management has the
experience to manage it. When the independent TAP asked MAAML for more information, they
responded that “MAM incorporates climate-related physical and transition risk in its diligence
of each of its portfolio companies to ensure that these risks are integrated into business
planning.”1 More broadly, the Macquarie Group reports under the Task Force on Climate-related
Financial Disclosures (TCFD).2
28.
Overall paradigm shift potential is assessed as High. There are of course risks regarding
an actual paradigm shift around transport in India, particularly relating to behavioural biases
toward EV purchases and the need for continued government support. However, considering
the size and scale of opportunity for India and the realistic possibility of pushing this large, high
GHG emitting sector onto a low carbon pathway we can attribute an assessment of High.
1.3 
Sustainable development potential 
 Scale: Medium-High 
29.
Social and Gender benefits: The funding proposal states that gender policy will be
embedded at the Platform level. The policy will include commitment to gender analysis in the
assets/projects funded by the Platform. For instance, the programme aims to promote universal
access and safety measures for women, children and people with disabilities including proper
lighting, in-vehicle cameras, vehicle monitoring systems and other safety measures, especially in
buses. According to the funding proposal, the Platform will seek to encourage the engagement of
people of all ages as stakeholders in the design, development and implementation of strategies
and activities to be financed. The policy will also outline how gender metrics will be monitored,
evaluated, and assessed. The Platform intends to collaborate with local and international
stakeholders and partners to address systemic and structural barriers to gender equality in
local communities.
30.
Health benefits: The Indian transport sector has a high percentage of vehicles with low
standards for emissions and suffers from significant levels of traffic congestion. For these
reasons (among others) the transport sector in India is linked with emissions of pollutants such
as fine particulate matter known to have a damaging impact on human health. The elevated
levels of emitted air pollutants have resulted in India having 21 of the world’s top 30 most
polluted cities. It is estimated that over 84 per cent of India’s population is exposed to high
concentrations of air pollution resulting in over 2 million deaths annually and significant
associated healthcare (approx. 5.4 per cent of GDP). The transition to electric vehicles,
especially in urban areas, can reduce air pollution. This is particularly important considering the
expected growth of the overall transportation sector. If future growth is concentrated around
internal combustion engine technologies, then the already dangerous levels of pollution can
significantly worsen health outcomes.
31.
Economic co-benefits: The Platform is expected to create an estimated 100 new direct
jobs at the Platform level. Greater job creation is expected to come from increased investments
in the e-mobility space which should indirectly lead to new jobs across the sector. According to
1 A summary of Macquarie’s supplier governance policy. 
2 A summary of Macquarie’s supplier governance policy.

=== PAGE 90 ===
GCF/B.32/02/Add.03 
Page 86 
 
Indian government statements, the EV sector is expected to create 10 million jobs in the coming 
decades. Additionally, as per a recent Niti Aayog report, the European Climate Foundation has 
estimated that any reduction in oil demand by more efficient electric cars is expected to create 
500,000 to 850,000 more domestic jobs by 2030. The report explains that oil production and 
distribution have exceptionally low employment intensity of just four jobs per million euros 
value added compared to 24 jobs per million euros in the general economy. Therefore, any shift 
in expenditure from buying imported oil to other expenditure choices would generate 
additional employment.  
32. 
India’s middle income class group is expected to increase to 300 million households by 
2030 (from 158 million) and the number of urban residents overall is expected to top 600 
million. The e-mobility Platform will primarily target the public buses and corporate fleet 
segments which are mainly used by middle income groups and urban residents. Through its 
investments, the programme aims to improve the safety, reliability and affordability of the 
transportation sector leading to an increased adoption rate among urban residents and middle-
income groups.  
33. 
The Indian government’s push for EVs is linked with its stated intent to enhance energy 
security as well as reduction of GHG emissions. Since the early 2000s, India’s crude oil imports 
have risen steeply with 40 per cent of total oil consumption coming from the transport sector. 
Oil imports contributed USD 102 billion to the trade deficit in 2020 covering over 80 per cent of 
the country’s oil needs. An increase in the penetration of EVs directly and indirectly through this 
programme will decrease India’s oil import requirement as well as corresponding GHG 
emissions. 
34. 
Social and environmental co-benefits: The independent TAP clarified if the funding 
proposal has a negative list of suppliers or if it has undertaken an assessment of potential risk 
on EV battery component sources (i.e., lithium, cobalt, magnesium, etc.) that are said to be 
engaged in child labour, human rights abuse, and environmentally unsustainable extraction 
activities. The AE has responded that they “have have not done a detailed assessment of the 
source of battery materials imported or sourced in India as there are a number of suppliers and 
given the nascency of the market, the profile is not static,” and that they “do not have an existing 
list of suppliers that would be automatically excluded. The risk identified is to highlight the 
known risk in the industry that should be considered as part of our ESS [environmental and 
social safeguards] risk assessment for each sub-project and supplier. MAM and Macquarie have 
existing, robust policies and procedures on upholding supplier governance that will be applied 
at the Platform.”3  
35. 
Alignment to Sustainable Development Goals: The funding proposal programme has the 
potential to promote and accelerate the country’s National Development Agenda to implement 
United Nations Sustainable Development Agenda 2030 as mirrored in the Sustainable 
Development Goals. 
36. 
The independent TAP assesses this programme as having a medium-high sustainable 
development potential with significant social, economic and health co-benefits. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
1.4 
Needs of the recipients   
 
 
               Scale: Medium-High 
37. 
India’s average temperature rose by 0.7°C since the last century resulting in extreme 
weather events and putting pressure on the country’s already scarce resources thereby 
threatening the livelihood of millions of people. India’s average temperatures are projected to 
continue to rise under the relevant Representative Concentration Pathway scenarios resulting 
in increased frequency and intensity of climate events. These events are expected to 
determinately impact the economic and social well-being of India if there is no change to 
 
3 A summary of Macquarie’s supplier governance policy.

=== PAGE 91 ===
GCF/B.32/02/Add.03 
Page 87 
 
current climate mitigation policies. The most severe impacts are expected to be borne by low 
and middle-income groups, women, children, and the elderly. Around 28 per cent of the 
population or 364 million people live below the national poverty line of USD 2 per person per 
day.  
38. 
Considering the above, there is a recipient need to undertake low or zero carbon and 
climate resilient infrastructure investments. According to the funding proposal, India needs 
~USD 2.5 trillion of investment between 2015 and 2030 to meet its NDC by 2030. Public funds 
will not suffice to finance the required climate action. Initiatives such as the Platform are needed 
to fill the finance gap by directly mobilizing private sector funds into climate-friendly sectors 
like e-mobility and funding a more rapid maturation of the sector to indirectly unlock additional 
private sector finance. In a post COVID-19 scenario, there will be an even greater need for the 
private sector to drive capital flow in the e-mobility sector.  
39. 
India has made a commitment to growing the e-mobility sector and has put in place 
several incentive and financial support programmes. However, according to the funding 
proposal, India’s EV transition will require a cumulative capital investment of USD 266 billion in 
the coming decade to fund vehicles, charging infrastructure, and batteries. This analysis 
suggested that by 2030 the market size for financing EVs in India has the potential to be USD 50 
billion. It will be necessary to attract a large amount of finance that will be critical to address 
existing barriers to finance flows and to demonstrate the investability of the sector. The 
Platform will help overcome some of these financing barriers including (1) high upfront costs; 
(2) unwillingness of banks and financial institutions to take EV asset risks; and (3) low loan to 
value ratios (among other barriers).  
40. 
The independent TAP assesses this programme as rated Medium-High in terms of needs 
of recipients. 
 
1.5 
Country ownership 
 
 
 
 
 
        Scale: High 
41. 
MAAML and the broader MAM teams have had extensive engagement with India’s 
national designated authority regarding the proposed e-mobility programme. It secured the no-
objection letter for the funding proposal from the Government of India on 15 September 2021. 
42. 
This programme directly contributes to India’s NDC targets of reducing carbon 
emissions from 2005 to 2030 by 35 per cent including through transforming 30 per cent of all 
vehicles to EVs. EV promotion has been supported by significant policy development at both the 
national and state levels. The Government of India has already committed subsidies of up to 
USD 1.4 billion to enable the accelerated roll-out of EVs under the Faster Adoption and 
Manufacturing of Hybrid and Electric Vehicles (FAME) Phase II scheme. Where appropriate the 
Platform will participate in the FAME scheme and other government policies and initiatives 
which include requirements for a pre-defined level of domestic component manufacturing. 
43. 
The Platform is aligned with India’s climate priorities and strategy. It directly 
contributes to India’s NDC GHG emissions reduction targets. It is complimentary to and aligned 
with publicly funded programmes to increase the e-mobility sector, including India’s FAME I 
and II.  
44. 
The funding proposal provided evidence of support across various government entities 
and other relevant stakeholders including leading domestic OEMs, bus operators, charge 
infrastructure providers, and Non-Banking Financial Institutions.  
45. 
We assess the country ownership of the funding proposal as High.

=== PAGE 92 ===
GCF/B.32/02/Add.03 
Page 88 
 
1.6 
Efficiency and effectiveness 
 
 
               Scale: Medium-High 
46. 
The nascent e-mobility sector represents a significant opportunity to reduce GHG 
emissions at scale in India and place the country on a transportation development path that 
avoids locking in fossil-fuel based technologies for many decades. This transition to EVs will 
require a significant amount of capital and a willingness for early investors to address 
infrastructure barriers and demonstrate the financial viability of the sector. Notwithstanding 
country buy-in reflected in strong government support, the market requires additional 
intervention from concessional capital to help the market mature to where it can readily attract 
large-scale commercial investments. Commercial investors currently do not have the risk 
appetite to deploy the capital at the pace required given the real and perceived investment risks 
outlined in the funding proposal.  
47. 
The Platform aims to provide financing solutions in the market such as operating and 
finance leases through a new investment vehicle in India. GCF capital will sit in a ‘first-loss’ 
position which provides a cushion to the potential downside investment risks for commercial 
investors. The ratio of GCF and non-GCF funding and the amount of GCF funding has been sized 
based on the perceived risk appetite of potential investors including what level of downside risk 
can be accepted by these types of investors. The funding proposal provides that GCF will 
contribute the first USD 50 million in equity at first financial close with the remaining GCF funds 
being committed alongside and subject to third-party commercial investor commitments. GCF’s 
capital will be provided in the form of an equity instrument which is able to take a higher risk 
position in the investment structure in line with GCF’s risk appetite. Further, the involvement of 
GCF will not only increase the amount of capital invested in the sector but also allow the 
Platform to provide financing solutions not currently available at scale in the market (i.e., a 
higher risk appetite than current financiers are able to provide). 
48. 
The Platform is expected to be funded by a combination of equity and debt. From our 
review of the funding proposal and discussions with the AE and Secretariat team, we have a 
reasonable level of comfort that there is commercial investor interest in the Platform as 
designed and that MAM has the capacity to raise the commercial funds alongside the GCF 
contribution. Capital provided by GCF will serve to crowd into the Platform commercial 
investors that would otherwise have overlooked the opportunity to invest in the e-mobility 
sector in India due to their risk appetite and nascency of the sector, among other reasons.  
49. 
In terms of efficiency of impact, the funding proposal has set a fundraising target of USD 1,478 
million in commercial debt and equity. This represents USD 1,278 million in additional co-financing 
to GCF financing. The Platform is expected to have a positive catalytic effect with a co-financing ratio 
of GCF funds of at least 1: 6.4. In other words, each GCF dollar invested should mobilize at least 6.4 
dollars in co-financing through commercial and private investors. The leverage effect beyond the 
anticipated investments of the programme through capacity-building is likely to lead to an even 
higher mobilization. The leasing solution is still nascent in India. If the Platform proves that this 
business model can work in the country, then private sector investors will be able to see the 
opportunity and enter the market.  
50. 
The estimated cost per tonne of carbon dioxide equivalent is USD 21.0 based on the 
requested GCF amount of USD 200 million.  
51. 
The funding proposal is assessed with a rating of Medium-High in terms of efficiency and 
effectiveness.

GCF/B.32/02/Add.03

II.  
Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel  
52. 
Based on the assessment above, the independent TAP endorses this funding proposal.  
53. 
We note that GCF funds will be pooled into a limited partnership through the accredited 
entity (MAAML), which will be the investor of record. The success of the investment proposal 
and its climate impact will depend on multiple factors including: 
• 
setting up a financial organization in India with a robust management team; 
• 
securing all remaining regulatory approvals from the India’s Central Bank to provide 
finance lease and other credit solutions; 
• 
successfully raising commercial equity into the Platform and being able to access/raise 
debt at the levels described in the funding proposal to leverage its Platform equity; and 
• 
The Platform products finding market acceptance and sufficient investable 
opportunities to support the target returns of commercial investors and GCF. 
54. 
The success of the Platform will depend on the above factors and others identified 
by the Secretariat. The independent TAP encourages the Secretariat to build in enough legal 
and governance flexibility in final deal documentation to be able to adapt to commercial 
realities if the expected parameters turn out to be materially different. Similarly, based on 
these numerous factors, the funding proposal programme will require close ongoing 
monitoring during the implementation phase.

1. 
INTRODUCTION 
Macquarie Alternative Assets Management Limited (hereinafter referred to as ‘MAAML’ or ‘AE’), has 
developed a Funding Proposal (‘FP’) for submission to the Green Climate Fund (‘GCF’), to seek their 
investment in a dedicated electric mobility (e-mobility) program focusing on India (hereinafter referred 
to as the ‘Program’). AE is part of Macquarie Asset Management (‘MAM’), one of the 4 operating 
business groups of Macquarie Group.  
MAM is is a global specialist asset manager with assets under management at 31 December 2021 of 
AUD 750.1 billion, aiming to deliver positive impact for everyone. MAM provides investment solutions 
to clients across a range of capabilities, including infrastructure & renewables, real estate, agriculture 
& natural assets, asset finance, private credit, equities, fixed income and multi-asset solutions 
ERM India Private Limited (ERM) has been appointed by MAM, to conduct a Gender Assessment 
Study and to develop a Gender Action Plan. The Gender Assessment Study and Gender Action Plan 
for the Program forms part of GCF’s requirements for all Funding Proposals. This document presents 
the Gender Assessment. 
1.1 
A brief overview of the ‘Dedicated E-Mobility Program’ 
MAM is looking to partner with GCF and other co-investors to establish an India-focused electric 
mobility program to facilitate decarbonisation of the road transport sector through rapid scale-up of the 
electric vehicles sector in the country.  
The Program will look to provide financing to private sector counterparties across the e-mobility value 
chain (BEV and FCEV) such as e-buses, 2/3/4 wheeler segments, supporting infrastructure & 
services like charging infrastructure, vehicle scrapping and after-sales services.  
The Program will comprise of three main activities as outlined in the Funding Proposal presented to 
the GCF: 
1) Establishment of a financing platform in India (hereinafter referred to as the ‘Platform’), including 
its legal entity, policies, procedures and broader governance. 
2) Providing financing by the Platform to corporate counterparties in the e-mobility space, which 
involves upfront due diligence and structuring as well as ongoing monitoring. 
3) Sale of the Platform (to facilitate GCF and other investors exit) with the expectation that the 
Platform will continue to operate in the market. 
For the purposes of this Program, ‘sub-projects’ will refer to each transaction at the Platform level 
where a commitment of financing is made to a counterparty in the e-mobility space. Please see the 
Gender Action Plan for more details.  
1.2 
Objective and Scope of work 
The objective of the assignment is to undertake a Gender Assessment and formulate a Gender Action 
Plan for the Program with consideration of its targeted sectors. The scope of the exercise includes:  
◼ 
assessment of general social baseline conditions in India in which the Platform will operate; 
◼ 
focused interventions that are in practice for the transport and e-mobility sectors from a gender 
perspective; 
◼ 
identification of areas that can be best targeted by the Platform for gender mainstreaming 
activities; and   
◼ 
formulation of a Gender Action Plan for the Program that highlights key performance indicators 
and timelines.   
                              96

=== PAGE 99 ===
The reference framework for the exercise was taken to be the GCF Guidance Note (Form 09 updated 
1 February 2019). 
1.3 
Applicable Reference Framework 
The applicable reference framework for the assignment is presented below: 
■ 
applicable national legislation and regulations pertaining to social and gender aspects; 
■ 
GCF Manual on Mainstreaming Gender in Green Climate Platform Projects1; and 
■ 
GCF Gender Toolkit and Guidance Note (Form 09). 
1.3.1 
Need for Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan 
Gender mainstreaming is central to the GCF’s objectives and guiding principles. The aim is to address 
it continually by engaging women and men of all ages as stakeholders in the design, development 
and implementation of strategies and activities to be financed. The GCF Governing Instrument states 
that: “The Fund [GCF] will strive to maximize the impact of its funding for adaptation and mitigation 
promoting environmental, social, economic, and development benefits, taking not only a gender-
sensitive, but a gender responsive approach.” 
Gender mainstreaming, as defined by the United Nations Economic and Social Council, refers to a 
strategy for making women’s, as well as men’s, concerns and experiences integral dimensions of 
policies and programs in all political, economic and social spheres. Gender mainstreaming is essential 
to ensure that women and men benefit equally from a certain system, and inequality is not 
perpetuated. 
There is still a heavy linking of the term “gender” with “women’s issues”; however, the evolved 
understanding on gender and gender mainstreaming is that Gender represents equality of both the 
sexes.  
However, GCF has established on the basis of prevalent practices and past experiences that women 
and girls are disproportionately affected by the impacts of climate change and associated severe 
weather events. Women’s mortality from climate-related disasters is higher than that of men, and 
climate-induced impacts due to natural disasters like floods and earthquakes exacerbate women’s 
poverty and unpaid domestic and care work burdens. Gender inequality, exacerbated by climate 
change, is linked, as are other development areas, to vulnerability and risks. The greater vulnerability 
of women to climate change stems from societal norms and discrimination that result in the 
imbalanced division of labour, lower incomes, and lesser livelihood opportunities; less access and 
control over land and other productive assets; fewer legal rights; lesser mobility and lesser political 
and professional representation.  
1.3.2 
GCF’s Gender Policy 
The GCF Board adopted the Gender Policy and Action Plan in March 2015 and an update to this 
policy was issued in February 2019, to respond to a fast-changing landscape related to gender in the 
context of climate change. Its rationale is rooted in the GCF’s mandate to encourage a paradigm shift 
towards low-emission and climate-resilient development pathways. The updated policy emphasizes 
gender responsiveness rather than gender sensitivity. Being gender-responsive means that instead of 
only identifying gender issues or ensuring a “do no harm” approach, a process will substantially help 
to overcome historical gender biases. Gender responsive approaches are essential to this, 
including decisions and actions by women and men.  
 
GCF’s Gender policy aims to: 
1 Published in 2017 
                              97

=== PAGE 100 ===
◼ 
Achieve greater, more effective, sustainable, and equitable climate change results, outcomes, 
and impacts, through the adoption of a gender sensitive approach 
◼ 
Build resilience to climate change equally for men and women, as well as to ensure that men 
and women equally contribute and benefit from activities supported by the Fund 
◼ 
Address or mitigate risks for women and men associated with adaptation and mitigation 
activities financed by the Fund; and 
◼ 
Reduce the gender gap of social, economic, and environmental vulnerabilities exacerbated by 
climate change. 
 
For the purposes of this Gender Equality Policy, GCF requires that women and men, including 
marginalized and vulnerable groups, will be provided with an equal and equitable opportunity to be 
fully and effectively engaged in meaningful consultations and decision-making throughout the project 
cycle. 
 
The GCF’s Gender Policy is based on the following six Fundamental principles. 
Figure 1.1 
Principles of GCF's Gender Policy 
 
Source: Green Climate Platform Proposal Toolkit 2017 
 
The GCF’s paradigm shift is rooted in the acknowledgement of women’s critical role in climate 
responses, as agents of change sharing in the benefits, and, in several cases, disproportionately 
affected by its negative impacts. Marginalised communities also bear disproportionate negative 
outcomes and are also included in this shift. 
 
According to GCF, “Gender assessment refers to methods used to understand relationships between 
men and women, their access to resources, their activities, and the constraints they face relative to 
each other. A comprehensive gender analysis/assessment entails the examination of the different 
roles, rights, needs, and opportunities of women and men, boys and girls in a given project/context. 
Gender analysis needs to take place early in the planning process so that an understanding of gender 
roles and power relations is built into the project.” 
1.4 
Methodology 
In order to achieve the above-mentioned scope, the following methodology has been adopted. 
                              98

=== PAGE 101 ===
1.4.1 
Kick off call with MAM 
ERM organised an Inception call with the Client to understand the current investment context, get a 
general understanding of the planned working of the Platform and expected investments. ERM also 
discussed the role of MAM in the Program as the investment manager, applicability of an ESMS, a 
Gender Policy or any other ongoing/proposed interventions that will be undertaken by the Program 
that may particularly focus on addressing gender related aspects. 
1.4.2 
Detailed Gender Assessment 
A desk-based research of aspects related to women in India and specially in urban mobility has been 
undertaken to understand the nature and extent of data and studies capturing aspects like female 
urban ridership, challenges faced by urban females due to limited mobility and while using public 
transport, general scenario of employment of women and specifically in the urban transportation 
sector.  
1.4.2.1 National Context guided by GCF Guidance Note  
An assessment of the nationwide gender related baseline scenario and associated impacts, risks, 
opportunities and trends in this sector was undertaken using secondary data.   
Key steps followed for the assessment are: 
◼ 
Profiling the social and development status of women and men (education, literacy, work force 
participation, unemployment, role in formal/informal economy, division of labour, access to 
resources and opportunity, decision making roles and capacity, as relevant) based on secondary 
data at the national level 
◼ 
An assessment of the legal status, beliefs, perceptions, prevalent gender stereotypes, as relevant 
for the key sectors 
◼ 
An assessment of relevant issues like gender-gap, inequality, gender-specific vulnerabilities (with 
respect to climate change adaptation, social, socio-economic factors, technical capacity, and 
resource access etc.) has been undertaken based on relevant secondary literature and published 
research.  
Please note: In the absence of latest data on gender issues such as employment and literacy in the 
transport sector? the report has relied on Census of India, 2011 data and has supported the findings, 
wherever possible with other latest available studies. 
1.4.2.2 Establishing a Baseline Scenario for Gender Mainstreaming in E-mobility  
The assessment has sought to identify a baseline scenario for gender issues in the e-mobility sector 
and ultimately identify where the Program could best implement gender-based interventions.  
Given the lack of data on gender and e-mobility sector, especially in developing countries, the report 
has relied on secondary review, data and analysis available for gender and transportation sector in 
general. It should also be noted that there is a general lack of gender disaggregated data on urban 
mobility and hence also in the e-mobility sector.  
Consequentially, there is little available research on strategies, interventions and case studies on 
specific preferences (of women and men, young and old, or those with disabilities) regarding urban 
public transport usage, sector employment trends, health and safety, ergonomics, or accident risks. 
Sex-disaggregated data has been provided where available.  
Whilst this is one limitation of the Gender Assessment, collecting sex-disaggregated data is also an 
identified area for improving gender sensitivity in this sector. 
                              99

=== PAGE 102 ===
1.4.2.3 Stakeholder Engagement 
Specific stakeholder engagement as part of this gender assessment has not been completed. This is 
primarily because the proposed Platform has not yet been established and the broad scope of the 
proposed transactions involve multiple e-mobility sectors and are not based in a specific region in 
India. It is proposed that targeted, gender-focused stakeholder engagement is completed once the 
Platform is established to build upon the desk-based assessment and interventions outlined in the 
Gender Action Plan. Please refer to the Gender Action Plan for more details. 
1.4.3 
Formulation of the Program-level Gender Action Plan 
The Gender Action Plan has been formulated to include strategies and action items identified for the 
Platform. The action plan includes key indicators and timelines, for effective monitoring of outcomes. 
1.4.3.1 Limitations in development of Action Plan 
◼ 
The Platform is not yet established, including the defined policies, procedures and governance. 
Actions have been identified to integrate gender mainstreaming frameworks as part of this 
establishment. 
◼ 
There is limited ability to set baseline scenarios given the lack of data available in the sector, the 
broad mandate across sectors and geography, and the Platform is not yet established so there is 
no benchmarking applicable of its existing practices. Activities have been outlined to support 
establishment of baseline. 
 
 
                              100

=== PAGE 103 ===
2. 
THE BROADER GENDER LANDSCAPE IN INDIA 
The Constitution of India conveys a powerful mandate for equality and rights of women in its 
Preamble, Fundamental Rights, and Duties and also provides for specific provisions for affirmative 
actions. India is also a signatory to a number of UN Conventions, primarily Convention on Elimination 
of all Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW), Beijing Platform for Action and Convention 
on Rights of the Child where the commitment of the nation to protect and empower its women and 
girls is quite pronounced. The recent endorsement by India, of the ambitious 2030 Sustainable 
Development Goals (SDGs) will further change the course of development by addressing the key 
challenges such as poverty, inequality, and violence against women, which is critical for the global 
success of the goals as well. 
However, according to the World Economic Forum’s Global Gender Gap Report 20212, India has 
seen its gender gap widen from 66.8% in 2019 to 62.5% in 2020. It now ranks at 140 out of 156 
countries. Similarly, according to the Human Development Index (HDI) report prepared by United 
Nations Development Programme in 2020, India was placed at 123rd position in the Gender Inequality 
Index. This is largely due to women’s inadequate representation in politics, technical and leadership 
roles, decrease in women’s labour force participation rate, poor healthcare, lagging female to male 
literacy ratio and income inequality. However, like most of the developing countries, India struggles 
with gender inequality issues beyond just equal economic growth and access to educational resource 
opportunities. Gender inequality exists in the form of socially constructed, predefined gender roles 
firmly anchored in India’s sociocultural fabric that has deep cultural and historical roots. Sociocultural 
influences have spill-over effects across all domains, including workforce ratio, and social and political 
contexts.  
Further, the COVID-193 pandemic has raised new barriers to build inclusive and prosperous 
economies and societies. In addition to the pressures of providing care in the home, the crisis has 
halted progress towards gender parity in several economies and industries. As per the Global Gender 
Gap Report 2021, preliminary evidence suggests that the health emergency and the related economic 
downturn have affected women more severely than men, partially re-opening gaps that were being 
closed. 
This section provides a broad baseline at the national level to set up a context for the gender scenario 
in the country at large and bring out the differences with respect to overall development indicators 
(demography, education, and health for example) and various policies introduced by Government of 
India to address the differences. Specific aspects of the focus sector, comprising of urban public 
transport, ridership, safety and risk, worker participation, other travel needs, gaps, and related aspects 
are included in the subsequent section. 
2.1 
Demographic Profile 
In India, as per Census 2011, the total population is 1,210.8 million with a decadal population growth 
rate of 17.7%. The decadal population growth of males has been registered as 17.1% and for females 
has been registered as 18.3%. It has also been established through the Census 2011 data that about 
31.1% of Indian population lives in urban areas; while the rest live in rural areas.  
The sex ratio (number of females per 1,000 males), in the country as per Census 2011 is 943 and has 
improved from a figure of 933 in 2001. In rural areas, the sex ratio has increased from 946 females 
per 1,000 males to 949 females per 1000 males and the corresponding increase in urban areas has 
been of 29 points from 900 to 929 females per 1000 males. The gender balance in the country is 
2 This report benchmarks the evolution of gender-based gaps among four key dimensions of Economic Participation and 
Opportunity, Educational Attainment, Health and Survival, and Political Empowerment and tracks progress towards closing

skewed towards males as depicted from the sex ratio figures and the general socio-cultural 
environment in the country. One of the reasons for this difference can be attributed to the trend of 
migration of males in search of employment from villages to nearby towns and cities, while their 
families continue to stay in their native place. 
The share of youth in the total population has been continuously increasing from 30.6% in 1971 to 
34.8% in 20114. The percentage of male youth population to total male population follow the pattern 
of overall youth and was also on a steady rise till 2011, but the share of female youths to total female 
population showed a slight decrease in 2011 which may have been due to a declining sex ratio during 
this period.  
2.1.1 
Educational Attainment 
 
According to the 2011 Census of India, the overall adult literacy rate was 73%. The literacy rate for 
females and males were 64.63% and 80.9% respectively. According to the Global Gender Gap 
Report 2021, almost one third of women are illiterate (34.2%) compared to 17.6% of men in India.  
The trend continues despite intervention by the government such as Right of Children to Free and 
Compulsory Education Act, 2009. This gap is attributable to a combination of social, economic, and 
cultural factors. Even as the benefits of female education will lead to a more productive workforce, 
lower fertility and lower infant mortality rates, the costs such as tuition fees, transport, and school 
supplies as well as opportunity costs of forgone child labour, which are privately borne by households, 
continue to influence this choice. The underinvestment in women’s schooling results in lower literacy 
and lower educational attainment, and further, to lower employability for semi-skilled and skilled jobs. 
Social restrictions on women’s mobility also prevents an educated woman from entering the labour 
force and supporting the household financially. 
 
The figure below shows the glaring difference in India’s female literacy, not only with the developed 
countries but also with middle- and lower-income countries like Sri Lanka and Zimbabwe. According 
to this, female literacy in India is 22 percentage points below the world average 5. These numbers are 
a reminder that India is still a long way from meeting Goal 4 of the UN SDGs (Sustainable 
Development Goals) of ensuring “inclusive and equitable quality education” and “lifelong learning 
opportunities for all” by 2030.

2.1.3 
Workforce Participation 
According to the Periodic Labour Force Survey (PLFS) (2017-18), Ministry of Statistics and 
Implementation, India, India’s workforce has fewer women than it did six years ago: no more than 
18% of women in rural areas are employed, compared to 25% in 2011-12. However, in urban areas, 
the percentage of women in salaried jobs has increased from 35.6% in 2004 to 52.1% in 2017 but 
continue to be under-represented compared to their presence in self-employed or casual work 11. 
Figure 2. presents participation of women in workforce from 1993 to 2017.

As per literature reviews, there are various reasons for this decline including social and cultural 
barriers to women's employment and household work expectations from women (particularly 
mothers). Notably, another key reason is the lack of reliable, safe, and cheap public transportation, as 
per the study by the Institute of Transportation and Development and the NGO, Jagori, shows. This is 
further discussed in detail in Section 3 below.  
 
Further, various government policy interventions have also not been very helpful (See section 2.3). 
Women also withdraw from the workforce due to an ‘income effect’, that is an increase in the 
husband’s income which pushes up the household income.  It is also understood that women are 
withdrawing from the workforce to pursue higher education, while others contend that there is a lack 
of good jobs for women12,13.  
 
In addition, the burden of unpaid work falls disproportionately on women in India because tasks such 
as cooking, cleaning, fetching water and firewood are highly gendered. Patriarchal norms dictate that 
women also perform care work, validate men’s failure to assume domestic responsibilities and thus 
entrench women’s unequal social status14 
 
According to Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development (OECD) data, Women in 
India currently spend up to 352 minutes per day on domestic work, 577% more than men (52 minutes) 
and at least 40% more than women in South Africa and China.  
 
According to the same report Periodic Labour Force Survey (PLFS) (2017-18), 24.5% of women were 
part of the labour force, which means they are either employed or looking for work, as compared to 
75.5% of men. However, as per the survey by Business Standard (2020)15, after lockdown was 
imposed, 13% fewer women than a year ago were employed or looking for jobs, compared to 2% 
fewer men. The lockdown affected urban women more than rural women.
