2.2 
Health  
Health is one of the important indicators defining the status of development of a nation. Its 
improvement is characterised by lower incidences of deaths and diseases and an improvement in the 
overall health indicators, including maternal mortality rates and infant mortality rates. 
In India, infant mortality rate16 (disaggregated by sex and place of residence) has been reported as 37 
per 1000 live births which is comparatively higher than the urban total of 25 per 1000 live births and 
lower than the rural total 41 per 1000 live births. In case of females, the infant mortality is higher in the 
rural area with a figure of 43 per 1000 live births than in urban areas of 28 per 1000 live births. The 
female infant mortality has been higher than male mortality rate which highlights a certain bias 
prevalent in the society, more so in the rural areas. The percentage change in the average level of 
infant mortality between 2004-06 and 2014-16 at the national level has declined by 36.7%. 
Reproductive, maternal, new-born, child and adolescent health services and national programs to 
curb incidences of diseases such as polio, HIV, TB, and leprosy have played pivotal roles in improving 
health outcomes for the Indian population. There are noteworthy improvements in health indicators 
such as life expectancy, infant mortality rate (IMR) and maternal mortality rate (MMR) due to 
increasing penetration of healthcare services across the country, extensive health campaigns, 
sanitation drives, increase in the number of government and private hospitals in India, improved 
immunisation, and growing literacy. Initiatives such as Janani Shishu Suraksha Karyakarm, Janani 
Suraksha Yojana17. During 2011 to 2015, the percentage of urban mothers who received medical 
attention at the time of delivery has increased from 87.9% to 93.5% and from 60.7% to 74.2% for rural 
mothers. 
2.3 
Gender Institutional Architecture and Government Schemes targeted at 
Women’s development 
At the national level the Ministry of Women and Child Development acts as a nodal agency for 
working towards realization of constitutional and international commitments to gender equality and 
social justice in India. State Resource Centres for Women under the National Mission for 
Empowerment of Women (NMEW) provides support to the respective State/UT Women and Child 
Development Department for coordination and implementation of the various policies and regulations. 
They are further supported by Panchayats (in rural areas) and Urban local bodies. Civil society 
organizations, associations, and federations also provide inputs and help in implementation and 
monitoring of policies and programmes impacting women.  
There are various gender focused legal regulations in India that aim to address the vulnerabilities and 
cultural biases associated with both the genders, through provision of certain measures that support a 
safe and equal professional platform for women. These regulations are presented below. 
◼ 
Equal Remuneration Act, 1973 provides for payment of equal remuneration to men and women 
workers for the same work of similar nature without any discrimination. In order to ensure social 
security to the workers including women in the unorganised sector, the Government has enacted 
the Unorganised Workers’ Social Security Act 2008.  
◼ 
The Maternity Benefit Act, 1961 and The Maternity Benefit (Amendment) Act, 2017 regulate 
employment of women in certain establishments for a certain period (26 weeks) before and after 
childbirth and provides for maternity and other benefits. 
◼ 
The Sexual Harassment of Women at Workplace (Prevention, Prohibition and Redressal) Act 
(POSH), 2013 has been enacted, which covers all women, irrespective of their age or 
employment status and protect them against sexual harassment at all workplaces both in public 
and private sector, whether organised or unorganised. 
16 Infant Mortality Rate is defined as the infant deaths (less than one year) per thousand live births 
17 http://www.indiaenvironmentportal.org.in/files/file/NHP%202018.pdf  
                              106

=== PAGE 109 ===
The Government of India has been launching programmes like Beti Padhao, Beti Bachao campaign 
(launched in 2015) for improving the Gender Parity Index (GPI)18 by promoting survival, protection, 
and education of girls. It aims to address the issue of declining child sex ratio through a mass 
campaign targeted at changing social mind set and creating awareness about the criticality of the 
issue. Furthermore, Mahila E-Haat has been launched by the Government in 2016 for promoting 
women entrepreneurs, with the objective to provide an e-marketing platform by leveraging technology 
for showcasing products made, manufactured and sold by women entrepreneurs, SHGs and/or 
NGOs. 
Mahatma Gandhi National Rural Employment Guarantee Act (MGNREGA), 200519 is one of the 
important schemes launched in India which ensures participation by women in the economic activity 
by stipulating minimum 33 per cent participation by women.  
2.4 
Climate Change Impacts on Women in India 
One of the biggest challenges that we are facing in 21st century is climate change.  As per the latest 
report of the United Nations’ Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC), human activities 
have already caused warming of 1.0 degree Celsius as compared to pre-industrial times. By 2030, or 
latest by mid-century, global warming is likely to reach 1.5 degrees Celsius. Thus far, with a 1°C rise 
in global temperatures, India has already experienced extreme weather events such as floods in 
Kerala, wildfires in Uttarakhand and heat waves in the north and the east, demonstrating its 
vulnerability. Its impacts vary among regions, generations, age, classes, income groups, and 
gender20. However, climate change exacerbates existing structural and social gender inequalities in 
ways that has led women and girls to experience climate change the worst and first.  
 
The IPCC (International Panel on Climate Change) found that gender inequalities are further 
exaggerated by climate-related hazards, and they result in higher workloads for women, occupational 
hazards indoors and outdoors, psychological and emotional stress, and higher mortality compared to 
men. 
 
It is not only in India but worldwide, women have less access than men to resources such as land, 
credit, agricultural inputs, decision-making structures, technology, training and extension services that 
would enhance their capacity to adapt to climate change21. As per one of the reports by United 
Nations22, women represent a high percentage of poor communities that are highly dependent on 
local natural resources for their livelihood, particularly in rural areas where they shoulder the major 
responsibility for household water supply and energy for cooking and heating, as well as for food 
security.  
2.4.1 
Impacts of Pollution 
 
According to the World Health Organization (WHO)23, air, noise pollution is a global environmental 
health emergency and India sits on the frontline of this global crisis. Fourteen of the world’s 20 most 
polluted cities are still found in India and an estimated 99.9 percent of India’s population live in areas 
where air pollution exceeds WHO air quality guidelines. In 2019, air pollution was estimated to be 
18 Gender Parity Index (GPI) in education is a valuable indicator which reflects the discrimination against girls in access to

responsible for 1.6 million premature deaths in India and $95 billion USD in economic losses (3 
percent of India’s GDP24).  
2.4.1.1 Transportation, Pollution and Gender  
Vehicle transport is a key source of air and noise pollution and greenhouse gas emissions in Indian 
cities, and one in which power dynamics, occupational differences, as well as socioeconomic and 
cultural differences related to gender shape exposure for women and men.  
Mobility Patterns 
As per various studies, 25gender inequalities often shape women’s and men’s mobility and use of 
transport globally and in India. If a family owns a car, the male head of the household is more likely to 
use it, leaving women and children to rely on alternatives, mainly walking and public transport. As per 
census of India,201126a gendered comparison of five large cities in India showed, on average, 37 
percent of women walked to work compared with 27 percent of men. Data from the Delhi morning 
rush hour demonstrates the implications of walking near roadways for exposure to increased levels of 
air pollution.  
Occupational Differences 
As per a study by WHO27 in India (especially in rural areas), women are more vulnerable to roadway 
emissions because they have reduced lung function due to indoor air pollution from domestic duties, 
such as the use of biomass fuels for cooking. As per a study conducted in 201328 cited found the risk 
of chronic respiratory morbidity among street sweepers was 4.24 times higher than that in the 
comparison group, and the risk increased significantly with increasing length of service. Women in the 
informal sector often also work in street vending or minding small retail shops that are frequently 
located in high-traffic roadsides where they have greater exposure to emissions. These women may 
be forced to choose between the increased incidence of respiratory disorders if they vend on busier 
streets or the increased risk of gender-based violent attacks on quieter streets.

3. 
SECTOR SPECIFIC ASSESSMENT 
As discussed above, the focus of the Program will be to provide financing to counterparties across the 
e-mobility value chain such as e-buses, 2/3/4 wheeler segment, support infrastructure & services like 
charging infrastructure, vehicle scrapping and after sales services.  
 
Through review of secondary literature and experience of projects within e-mobility space, it is 
understood that gender related issues in the e-mobility sector are common to those in the broader 
mobility sector in India, including gender-based violence and harassment, economic disparity, and 
appropriate and safe transport options.  
 
The recent flow of capital in safe and well-designed transport infrastructure can help increase 
economic empowerment of women by improving mobility, access to better avenues of employment, 
education, and healthcare. For example, introduction of new electric bus fleets over recent years is 
helping state governments enhance the safety measures for women and elderly on such buses by 
mandating inclusion of specific safety features such as provision of first aid kits and fire extinguishers 
or ensuring proper in-bus lighting at all times in the concession agreements with operators. 
  
As a result, there is a strong belief that growth of the e-mobility sector can lead to changes to gender 
dimensions by supporting safer public transportation options. Moreover, as a new industry there is 
potential to promote the implementation of gender inclusive design and practice, as well as applying 
broader workforce policies such as bridging the pay gap, involvement of more technically qualified 
women, opening more avenues of employment for women.   
 
However, there is lack of study and research in gender disaggregated use of sustainable 
transportation. For example, there is less data to understand if women use more electric vehicles than 
traditional fuel vehicles. This study therefore relies on data on general use of transportation and 
employment in the transportation sector.  
 
Subsequent sections describe in detail the gender issues prevalent in the urban mobility and 
transportation sectors. 
3.1 
Gender Dimensions of Urban Transport 
The recently announced Green Urban Mobility Scheme (GUMS) expects to invest around INR 
700,000 million over 2018–2023 to promote use of sustainable transportation. The central 
government has initiated schemes to invest in urban transport and infrastructure and created 
indicators and service level benchmarks to establish a city’s baseline and goal for improvement29.  
 
Transportation planning and design are commonly regarded as “gender neutral.” It is assumed that 
transport projects equally benefit both men and women and that there are no significant differences 
between travel needs and patterns of either gender30. However, for these same reasons urban 
transport investments are largely “gender blind” with a limited understanding of the interrelationships 
between gender and transport inequities. This is intensely rooted in community-driven gender roles 
with economic, social and livelihood influences in the Indian society where women are invariably 
responsible for household chores and taking care responsibilities of children and elders, making the 
mobility needs of women and men heterogeneous.  
 
Men and women have also been found to use public transportation very differently, noted in a 
December 2017 report, Women and Transport in Indian Cities, by the Institute for Transportation & 
Development Policy (ITDP) and Safetipin. For instance, women tend to combine multiple destinations 
29 Women and Transport in Indian Cities. A Policy Brief. December 2017.ITDP. Safetypin 
30 https://thecityfix.com/blog/why-it-is-key-to-include-gender-equality-in-transport-design-jyot-chadha-vishal-ramprasad/ 
                              109

=== PAGE 112 ===
within one trip, for example to pick up groceries on the way back from office or drop a child off to 
childcare on the way to work. While men use transport services largely to travel to work and return. 
The same study points out that, in metropolitan cities in India, women travel shorter distances, with 
most preferring to walk or take the bus to work. Men seem to have greater choice across modes of 
transport including bicycles, two-wheelers, and four-wheelers. Further across Indian cities, women 
form only 22% of people who travel for work.   
According to the National Sample Survey Office (NSSO) 2016 data, more than 60% of rural and 
urban households use the bus as their main mode of public transportation, followed by auto-rickshaw, 
taxi, railways, and cycle rickshaw. While women’s bus transport modal shares vary from 25% in 
Mumbai to 37% in Bhopal, women are more dependent on public transport than men are, especially 
when they are from lower-income groups. In Mumbai, women undertook 45% more trips by bus than 
by train, which increased to 67% for households with incomes less than INR 5,000 per month, as per 
a study by World Bank in 2011. In Delhi, 34% of women commuted to work by bus compared to 25% 
of men, whereas the corresponding figures for Chennai were 34% for women and 22% for men as per 
Census 2011 data. 
3.2 
Women safety in Public Transport 
Sexual harassment in public places is rampant and based on a 2010 study of 5,010 women and men 
in Delhi by the NGO Jagori an overwhelming concern of 85.4% women fear a violent physical attack 
and even rape. Almost two out of three women reported facing sexual harassment between two to 
five times over the previous year. The harassment occurred day and night, in places secluded and 
crowded with most being reported from buses, public transport and roadsides. 
Another report by ActionAid, released in 2016, found that 79% of women surveyed had experienced 
some form of harassment in public. 
Figure 3.1  Where Women Reported Sexual Harassment Takes Place 
Source: http://www.jagori.org/wp-content/uploads/2011/03/Baseline-Survey_layout_for-Print_12_03_2011.pdf 
110

=== PAGE 113 ===
Figure 3.2 
What Makes Women Feel Unsafe 
 
Source: http://www.jagori.org/wp-content/uploads/2011/03/Baseline-Survey_layout_for-Print_12_03_2011.pdf 
 
Ola Mobility Institute conducted a survey of 43,486 people in 20 cities around India in 2018 along 52 
criteria. 91% of women felt that public transport was very unsafe or somewhat unsafe. Though 
gruesome incidents of violence against women have received attention in mainstream media, this 
data suggests that most women may face harassment such as verbal abuse, staring, groping, 
catcalls, whistling and molestation. When women use public transport, 96% prioritize affordability, 
coverage, frequency, safety, and comfort31. 
The lack of safety in public transportation limits women's access to better educational and 
professional opportunities, ultimately reducing their quality of life as well as having a negative impact 
on the economic and social growth32. 
In India, the challenge of ensuring women’s safety using urban transport came to fore after the death 
of a woman in Delhi in December 2012, which is termed as ‘Nirbhaya Case’. The Government of India 
created the Nirbhaya Fund between 2013–16 to implement schemes for improving women’s security. 
Further, the Cabinet Committee on Economic Affairs has approved the setting up of a unified system 
at the national and state levels (City Command and Control Centre) for Global Positioning System 
(GPS) tracking through emergency buttons and video recording in public transport vehicles in 32 
cities33. It has been understood that majority of the funding by Government of India under the 
‘Nirbhaya Scheme’ was spent on CCTV cameras for improved surveillance. However, the limited 
success-rate of such measures has brought out other specific measures to improve women’s safety, 
such as better lighting at bus stops and terminals and gender-specific planning of infrastructure and 
services. It has been highlighted that there is a need to include women’s issues within the general 
decision-making framework and not as an additional feature or cost item for the transit agency34. 
The Ministry of Road Transport has mandated all taxis, buses, and public transport vehicles (barring 
three-wheelers) to install GPS tracking and panic buttons mandatorily from 1st April 2018.

32 https://unleash.org/solutions/asha-2/ 
 
33 https://www.researchgate.net/publication/322330719_Women_and_Transport_in_Indian_Cities

3.3 
Impact on Female Workforce Participation due to Public Transportation 
According to a 2017 study by the Institute for Transportation and Development Policy (ITDS), women 
in urban India turn down better employment opportunities further away from home in favour of lower-
paid local opportunities as the public transport system is often unreliable or unaffordable.  In a 
developing country like India, women often rely on some form of public transport as their only choice 
of motorised transport. It is therefore important that they perceive public transport as being safe and 
secure.  
 
In over two decades preceding 2013, female labour force participation in India fell from 34.8% to 
27%.35 As per a 2016 study by FIA Foundation,36 women are more affected than men when access to 
employment, education or basic services are located far away from their residences.  
 
Women are more dependent on public transport than men, especially when they are from lower-
income groups, according to the ITDS report. Across cities, on average, 37% of women, compared to 
27% of men, walk to work and 30% of women as compared to 25% of men use public transport.37 
 
3.4 
Gender and Employment in the Transportation Sector 
According to the International Labour Organization, transport is one of several sectors that has 
traditionally been regarded as having limited scope for women to participate in the workforce and 
largely considered a masculine profession. There is limited gender disaggregated data on the sectoral 
employment data which reflects that limited thought is being given towards improving the participation 
of women in the sector. The latest study from 2005 showed that 6.85% of women were employed in 
the transportation sector in India compared to 19% of men38. According to a World Bank report39, 
women constituted only 12.5% of the Brihanmumbai Electric Supply & Transport (BEST) Undertaking 
Committee and 1% of its engineers in 2010. When BEST attempted to induct female bus conductors, 
it was recorded that all of them requested to be shifted to desk jobs. Similarly, the Delhi Transport 
Corporation (DTC) has 245 women conductors and only one female driver40. 
Women face multiple challenges including accessing vocational training specific to transport sector 
jobs. For example, the requirements pertaining to driving licenses are passed on informally from older 
male family members to younger ones. Moreover, the upfront investment associated with training 
programs may be a barrier for resource-poor women. In DTC, drivers are required to hold a valid 
heavy motor vehicle license for at least three years and a public service vehicle (PSV) badge issued 
by the licensing authority, which is a major entry barrier. Additionally, when women are qualified for 
the jobs, insidious gendered assumptions regarding their suitability for the job can prevent them from 
breaking into the transport sector (Azad Foundation and University of Western Ontario 2014)41. 
Harassment and threats by commuters and colleagues, and lack of public toilet facilities are additional 
barriers discouraging them to participate in the transport sector. Encouraging women in non-
traditional occupations requires institutions to create a conducive environment. Simultaneously, 
women’s presence at different levels in public transport authorities has the potential of mainstreaming 
gender within the organization by bringing women’s issues to the fore in its services and 
infrastructure. 
Under-representation of women in the transport sector leads to a low visibility of women’s 
perspectives, and the differences in transport needs and patterns do not become apparent. Higher

participation of women in the planning and design process is therefore increasingly necessary. A 
commitment to gender mainstreaming must include gender awareness and capacity-building 
programs for policy and decision makers to completely understand and appreciate gender-related 
issues42. 
3.5 
Good Practice Examples in Transportation Sector 
The following provides some good practice examples in transportation sectors and various 
government interventions in gender and transportation. It should be noted that these interventions 
have been implemented directly by companies or governments and not by intermediaries (like the 
Platform) and so the activities cannot be copied by the Platform. However, these provide examples of 
where key issues have been identified for interventions. 
Box 3.1 
Good Practice Examples in Transportation Sector – Safetypin 
Description: SafetiPin is a map based mobile phone application, designed to make communities 
and cities safer by providing safety related information collected by users and by trained auditors. 
At the core of the app is the Women’s Safety Audit (WSA), a participatory tool for collecting and 
assessing information about perceptions of urban safety in public spaces. The audit is based on 
nine parameters: 
◼
Level of lighting
◼
Openness (sight lines)
◼
Visibility (overhanging trees, high walls etc)
◼
Level of crowding or emptiness
◼
Security (agents)
◼
Walk/ footpath
◼
Availability of public transport,
◼
Usage of the public space (for instance is it male dominated or mixed) and
◼
Feeling/ emotional response to the space (some time also called “eyes on the street”).
Safetipin conducted audits to assess the safety of the city of Delhi since August 2013 and collected 
more than 7,000 data points that can be used by the city to improve the safety, such as replace 
lightings that no longer work, repairing broken footpaths and cutting overhanging trees.  
Box 3.2 
Good Practice Examples in Transportation Sector – Gender 
Sensitization Trainings, India 
Gender Sensitization Trainings, India 
Description: Sensitization for women´s issues among public transportation employees as 
transportation plays an essential role for changing behaviour. 
An example, as described by ITDP (2017), is the training conducted with the Delhi Transport 
Cooperation that includes role-plays as a basis for discussions about gender issues in transport, as 
well as After Trainings Support. Since 2012, a mental health organization in Delhi conducts those 
trainings and has by now trained over 100,000 transportation employees. 
Box 3.3: 
Initiative by Government in India to Improve Public Transportation 
◼
In Kerala, She-Taxi, the all-woman taxi service is like other taxi aggregators but only hires
women drivers. The customer care centre tracks the vehicle with a GPS device. Police,
42 https://thecityfix.com/blog/why-it-is-key-to-include-gender-equality-in-transport-design-jyot-chadha-vishal-ramprasad/ 
113

=== PAGE 116 ===
relatives, and friends of the passenger can also monitor the cab using the system. There are 
separate switches in the car for drivers and passengers to send distress signals to the centre. 
The service is initiated by the state social justice department, which started it under Gender 
Park, a new institution launched by the department to empower women through livelihood 
creation. The drivers own their vehicles. (She Taxi 2013).Shimla City in Himachal Pradesh has 
new low-floor buses with each bus having three CCTV cameras. Two of them face two 
entrances from inside and one outside on the rear of the bus. The buses have been made more 
spacious with wider passageways, wider windowpanes, and lower entry/exit doors. 
◼ 
In 2016, Maharashtra introduced the Tejaswini buses, which were ladies’ special buses running 
during peak hours with women drivers and conductors 
 
Box 3.4:  
Driving employment opportunities – Safeway Concessions  
Safeway Concessions is currently a MAM-managed company acquired in 2018 by MAM 
investors from National Highways Authority of India (NHAI). 
Safeway Concessions owns and operates a portfolio of 9 toll roads (687 kms) across the states of 
Andhra Pradesh and Gujarat in India.  
Last year, it piloted a program to recruit women from local areas to work as toll collectors at toll 
plazas, a profession which has primarily been male dominated. Under the pilot program, more than 
40 females have been employed who operate during day shift at three of the ten toll collection 
plazas. The initiative has played pivotal role in empowering local women both in terms of providing 
access to additional income and improving their livelihood. 
In hiring this new demographic workforce, the company considered the following: 
◼ 
Put in place an all-female shift organized around daytime hours for enhanced safety  
◼ 
Male toll collectors were redeployed to comparable jobs to create open roles for the daytime 
shift. 
◼ 
The all-female shift ensured the new hires felt appropriately supported by female colleagues in 
a largely male dominated environment.  
◼ 
New facilities (e.g., female bathroom and changing rooms) were put in place to accommodate 
the new workforce demographic and is now a standard facility in the designs of new toll plaza 
constructions for the company.

APPENDIX A 
GENDER ASSESSMENT OF SUB-PROJECTS

The Platform will undertake specific gender assessment of sub-projects as part of the counterparty 
and sub-project due diligence (included in ESMS) prior to financing. The gender assessment will 
include review against equal access and benefits, identifying risks and mitigants, identifying 
opportunities for gender mainstreaming, what sex-disaggregated data will be collected and 
assessment of the counterparty. 
During screening and due diligence gender-related data will be collected and analysed to determine 
whether the sub-project qualifies under the Platform’s gender policies and identify business 
performance gender opportunities and risks. Gender-related data and information will be gathered 
from the potential counterparty for analysis to determine whether gender gaps present opportunities 
that may impact performance and affect the sub-project operations and financials.  
The following checklist is to be completed by counterparties during the due diligence stage. Its aim is 
to enable the Platform to understand the current level of internal gender diversity and inclusion, to 
identify initiatives that support women entrepreneurs, and acknowledge efforts to serve women clients 
and users. 
For the avoidance of doubt, any data collection, storage and utilisation of sex-disaggregated data will 
be compliant with local data privacy laws.

1.
INTRODUCTION
Macquarie Alternative Assets Management Limited (hereinafter referred to as ‘MAAML’ or ‘AE’), has 
developed a Funding Proposal (‘FP’) for submission to the Green Climate Fund (‘GCF’), to seek their 
investment in a dedicated electric mobility (e-mobility) program focusing on India (hereinafter referred 
to as the ‘Program’). AE is part of Macquarie Asset Management (‘MAM’), one of the 4 operating 
business groups of Macquarie Group.  
MAM is is a global specialist asset manager with assets under management at 31 December 2021 of 
AUD 750.1 billion, aiming to deliver positive impact for everyone. MAM provides investment solutions 
to clients across a range of capabilities, including infrastructure & renewables, real estate, agriculture 
& natural assets, asset finance, private credit, equities, fixed income and multi-asset solutions 
ERM India Private Limited (ERM) has been appointed by MAM, to conduct a Gender Assessment 
Study and to develop a Gender Action Plan. The Gender Assessment Study and Gender Action Plan 
for the Program forms part of GCF’s requirements for all Funding Proposals. This document presents 
the Gender Action Plan. 
1.1 
A brief overview of the ‘Dedicated E-Mobility Program’ 
MAM is looking to partner with GCF and other co-investors to establish an India-focused electric 
mobility program to facilitate decarbonisation of the road transport sector through rapid scale-up of the 
electric vehicles sector in the country.  
The Program will look to provide financing to private sector counterparties across the e-mobility value 
chain (BEV and FCEV) such as e-buses, 2/3/4 wheeler segments, supporting infrastructure & 
services like charging infrastructure, vehicle scrapping and after-sales services.  
The Program will comprise of three main activities as outlined in the Funding Proposal presented to 
the GCF: 
1)
Establishment of a financing platform in India (hereinafter referred to as the ‘Platform’), including
its legal entity, policies, procedures and broader governance.
2)
Providing financing by the Platform to corporate counterparties in the e-mobility space, which
involves upfront due diligence and structuring as well as ongoing monitoring.
3)
Sale of the Platform (to facilitate GCF and other investors exit) with the expectation that the
Platform will continue to operate in the market.
For the purposes of this Program, ‘sub-projects’ will refer to each transaction at the Platform level 
where a commitment of financing is made to a counterparty in the e-mobility space. Please see the 
Gender Assessment Report for more details.  
1.2 
Objective and Scope of work 
The objective of the assignment is to undertake a Gender Assessment and formulate a Gender Action 
Plan for the Program with consideration of its targeted sectors. The scope of the exercise includes:  
◼
assessment of general social baseline conditions in India in which the Platform will operate;
◼
focused interventions that are in practice for the transport and e-mobility sectors from a gender
perspective;
◼
identification of areas that can be best targeted by the Platform for gender mainstreaming
activities; and
◼
formulation of a Gender Action Plan for the Program that highlights key performance indicators
and timelines.
128

=== PAGE 131 ===
The reference framework for the exercise was taken to be the GCF Guidance Note (Form 09 updated 
1 February 2019). 
1.3 
Applicable Reference Framework 
The applicable reference framework for the assignment is presented below: 
■
applicable national legislation and regulations pertaining to social and gender aspects;
■
GCF Manual on Mainstreaming Gender in Green Climate Platform Projects1; and
■
GCF Gender Toolkit and Guidance Note (Form 09).
1.3.1 
Need for Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan 
Gender mainstreaming is central to the GCF’s objectives and guiding principles. The aim is to address 
it continually by engaging women and men of all ages as stakeholders in the design, development 
and implementation of strategies and activities to be financed. The GCF Governing Instrument states 
that: “The Fund [GCF] will strive to maximize the impact of its funding for adaptation and mitigation 
promoting environmental, social, economic, and development benefits, taking not only a gender-
sensitive, but a gender responsive approach.” 
Gender mainstreaming, as defined by the United Nations Economic and Social Council, refers to a 
strategy for making women’s, as well as men’s, concerns and experiences integral dimensions of 
policies and programs in all political, economic and social spheres. Gender mainstreaming is essential 
to ensure that women and men benefit equally from a certain system, and inequality is not 
perpetuated. 
There is still a heavy linking of the term “gender” with “women’s issues”; however, the evolved 
understanding on gender and gender mainstreaming is that Gender represents equality of both the 
sexes.  
However, GCF has established on the basis of prevalent practices and past experiences that women 
and girls are disproportionately affected by the impacts of climate change and associated severe 
weather events. Women’s mortality from climate-related disasters is higher than that of men, and 
climate-induced impacts due to natural disasters like floods and earthquakes exacerbate women’s 
poverty and unpaid domestic and care work burdens. Gender inequality, exacerbated by climate 
change, is linked, as are other development areas, to vulnerability and risks. The greater vulnerability 
of women to climate change stems from societal norms and discrimination that result in the 
imbalanced division of labour, lower incomes, and lesser livelihood opportunities; less access and 
control over land and other productive assets; fewer legal rights; lesser mobility and lesser political 
and professional representation.  
1.3.2 
GCF’s Gender Policy 
The GCF Board adopted the Gender Policy and Action Plan in March 2015 and an update to this 
policy was issued in February 2019, to respond to a fast-changing landscape related to gender in the 
context of climate change. Its rationale is rooted in the GCF’s mandate to encourage a paradigm shift 
towards low-emission and climate-resilient development pathways. The updated policy emphasizes 
gender responsiveness rather than gender sensitivity. Being gender-responsive means that instead of 
only identifying gender issues or ensuring a “do no harm” approach, a process will substantially help 
to overcome historical gender biases. Gender responsive approaches are essential to this, 
including decisions and actions by women and men.  
GCF’s Gender policy aims to: 
1 Published in 2017
129

=== PAGE 132 ===
◼ 
Achieve greater, more effective, sustainable, and equitable climate change results, outcomes, 
and impacts, through the adoption of a gender sensitive approach 
◼ 
Build resilience to climate change equally for men and women, as well as to ensure that men 
and women equally contribute and benefit from activities supported by the Fund 
◼ 
Address or mitigate risks for women and men associated with adaptation and mitigation 
activities financed by the Fund; and 
◼ 
Reduce the gender gap of social, economic, and environmental vulnerabilities exacerbated by 
climate change. 
 
For the purposes of this Gender Equality Policy, GCF requires that women and men, including 
marginalized and vulnerable groups, will be provided with an equal and equitable opportunity to be 
fully and effectively engaged in meaningful consultations and decision-making throughout the project 
cycle. 
 
The GCF’s Gender Policy is based on the following six Fundamental principles. 
Figure 1.1 
Principles of GCF's Gender Policy 
 
Source: Green Climate Platform Proposal Toolkit 2017 
 
The GCF’s paradigm shift is rooted in the acknowledgement of women’s critical role in climate 
responses, as agents of change sharing in the benefits, and, in several cases, disproportionately 
affected by its negative impacts. Marginalised communities also bear disproportionate negative 
outcomes and are also included in this shift. 
 
According to GCF, “Gender assessment refers to methods used to understand relationships between 
men and women, their access to resources, their activities, and the constraints they face relative to 
each other. A comprehensive gender analysis/assessment entails the examination of the different 
roles, rights, needs, and opportunities of women and men, boys and girls in a given project/context. 
Gender analysis needs to take place early in the planning process so that an understanding of gender 
roles and power relations is built into the project.” 
1.4 
Methodology 
In order to achieve the above-mentioned scope, the following methodology has been adopted. 
                              130

=== PAGE 133 ===
1.4.1 
Kick off call with MAM 
ERM organised an Inception call with the Client to understand the current investment context, get a 
general understanding of the planned working of the Platform and expected investments. ERM also 
discussed the role of MAM in the Program as the investment manager, applicability of an ESMS, a 
Gender Policy or any other ongoing/proposed interventions that will be undertaken by the Program 
that may particularly focus on addressing gender related aspects. 
1.4.2 
Detailed Gender Assessment 
A desk-based research of aspects related to women in India and specially in urban mobility has been 
undertaken to understand the nature and extent of data and studies capturing aspects like female 
urban ridership, challenges faced by urban females due to limited mobility and while using public 
transport, general scenario of employment of women and specifically in the urban transportation 
sector.  
1.4.2.1 National Context guided by GCF Guidance Note  
An assessment of the nationwide gender related baseline scenario and associated impacts, risks, 
opportunities and trends in this sector was undertaken using secondary data.   
Key steps followed for the assessment are: 
◼ 
Profiling the social and development status of women and men (education, literacy, work force 
participation, unemployment, role in formal/informal economy, division of labour, access to 
resources and opportunity, decision making roles and capacity, as relevant) based on 
secondary data at the national level 
◼ 
An assessment of the legal status, beliefs, perceptions, prevalent gender stereotypes, as relevant 
for the key sectors 
◼ 
An assessment of relevant issues like gender-gap, inequality, gender-specific vulnerabilities (with 
respect to climate change adaptation, social, socio-economic factors, technical capacity, and 
resource access etc.) has been undertaken based on relevant secondary literature and published 
research.  
Please note: In the absence of latest data on gender issues such as employment and literacy in the 
transport sector? the report has relied on Census of India, 2011 data and has supported the findings, 
wherever possible with other latest available studies. 
1.4.2.2 Establishing a Baseline Scenario for Gender Mainstreaming in E-mobility  
The assessment has sought to identify a baseline scenario for gender issues in the e-mobility sector 
and ultimately identify where the Program could best implement gender-based interventions.  
Given the lack of data on gender and e-mobility sector, especially in developing countries, the report 
has relied on secondary review, data and analysis available for gender and transportation sector in 
general. It should also be noted that there is a general lack of gender disaggregated data on urban 
mobility and hence also in the e-mobility sector.  
Consequentially, there is little available research on strategies, interventions and case studies on 
specific preferences (of women and men, young and old, or those with disabilities) regarding urban 
public transport usage, sector employment trends, health and safety, ergonomics, or accident risks. 
Sex-disaggregated data has been provided where available.  
Whilst this is one limitation of the Gender Assessment, collecting sex-disaggregated data is also an 
identified area for improving gender sensitivity in this sector. 
                              131

=== PAGE 134 ===
1.4.2.3 Stakeholder Engagement 
Specific stakeholder engagement as part of this gender assessment has not been completed. This is 
primarily because the proposed Platform has not yet been established and the broad scope of the 
proposed transactions involve multiple e-mobility sectors and are not based in a specific region in 
India. It is proposed that targeted, gender-focused stakeholder engagement is completed once the 
Platform is established to build upon the desk-based assessment and interventions outlined in the 
Gender Action Plan. Please refer to the Gender Action Plan for more details. 
1.4.3 
Formulation of the Program-level Gender Action Plan 
The Gender Action Plan, as presented in this document, has been formulated to include strategies 
and action items identified for the Platform. The action plan includes key indicators and timelines, for 
effective monitoring of outcomes. 
1.4.3.1 Limitations in development of Action Plan 
◼ 
The Platform is not yet established, including the defined policies, procedures and governance. 
Actions have been identified to integrate gender mainstreaming frameworks as part of this 
establishment. 
◼ 
There is limited ability to set baseline scenarios given the lack of data available in the sector, the 
broad mandate across sectors and geography, and the Platform is not yet established so there is 
no benchmarking applicable of its existing practices. Activities have been outlined to support 
establishment of baseline. 
 
 
                              132

=== PAGE 135 ===
2. 
GENDER ACTION PLAN 
This Gender Action Plan has been developed based on understanding developed through review of existing 
baseline scenario in India on gender related aspects, study of relevant research done in the field of urban 
public transport, especially from a gender perspective and discussions with MAM.  
The Action Plan is at Platform level and covers components of the program at high-level. As the Program is 
still in proposal stage and the electric urban mobility sector is also set to evolve rapidly from its current, 
nascent stage, there will be need for measures to adapt and lessons to be incorporated efficiently in 
subsequent stages. 
As a strategy, where secondary research has brought out the issue of lack of data on useful aspects, the 
Action Plan recommends generation of good quality data to make subsequent phases and projects in this 
area more impactful.  
2.1 
Implementation of Gender Action Plan 
The overall outcome of the Gender Action Plan is to develop greater knowledge of gender sensitivity and 
mainstreaming activities within the e-mobility sector, including greater skills developed by Platform staff, and 
improved attitudes towards integration of gender-related interventions by other stakeholders within the 
sector. 
The Plan will be implemented based on the timelines identified in Table 2.3.   
2.1.1 
Impact & Outcome Statement  
 
Impact Statement: 
 
E-mobility Financing Program aims to leverage the GCF and commercial capital to accelerate 
decarbonisation of the Indian economy by focusing on electrification of the road transport sector. This will be 
enabled by supporting faster adoption of electric vehicles and growth of associated infrastructure in the 
country. The focus of the program will be to provide capital to private sector business opportunities across 
the e-mobility value chain such as e-buses, 2/3/4 wheeler segment, support infrastructure & services like 
charging infrastructure. 
 
The Program aspires to:   
◼ 
Creation of new jobs and stimulate economic activity with a corollary of empowering women and 
reducing gender bias.  
◼ 
Enhancement of economic activities, livelihoods, and empowerment of women through increased 
generation of participatory and inclusive employment opportunities. 
◼ 
Improved access to safer transportation options for women due to design, adoption of inclusive and 
gender-responsive public transport systems and related solutions. 
 
Outcome Statement: 
 
Through the current proposed systems and mechanisms, the Platform will seek to contribute to achieving the 
following gender-responsive outcomes with gender-disaggregated sub-project level impacts: 
◼ 
Formal employment and improved livelihoods for women through generation of jobs by the Platform and 
counterparties at sub-project level line in with the growth of the e-mobility. 
◼ 
Empowerment of local communities, including women and girls, through training, awareness, and 
capacity building, to build more gender-inclusive and responsive sub-projects (transportation systems). 
                              133

=== PAGE 136 ===
◼ 
Building self-reliance among women and girls through increased use of public transportation by 
addressing safety concerns around using mass transport options and improving ‘gender blind’ 
transportation systems. 
◼ 
Reductions in gender violence, increase gender equity, and economic activities for women and girls 
through the Platform Level Environmental and Social Management System (ESMS), mandatory sub-
project safeguards, gender assessment and action plan, and gender-responsive project designs. 
2.1.2 
Definition of Sub-Projects and Counterparties 
The Platform will provide financing to counterparties in the e-mobility space (also referred to as a 
‘transaction’) across the value chain including buses, four-wheelers, two- and three-wheelers, charging 
infrastructure and others. Key characteristics of the sub-projects include: 
◼ 
physical assets in the e-mobility space are involved (e.g., financing a set number of electric buses). 
◼ 
vehicles or charging points will be operated by the counterparty. 
◼ 
the relationship between the Platform and the counterparty will be governed by a financing agreement 
that will place certain obligations on both parties. 
◼ 
vehicles financed may not be dedicated to a specific route but rather used by the counterparty generally. 
This may limit site specific diligence. 
For the avoidance of doubt: 
◼ 
the Platform is designed to be a financing intervention. Sub-projects will not involve the design or 
development of broader transportation interventions except (where relevant) for providing input on the e-
mobility sector development from a financing perspective (outside the scope of a sub-project). 
◼ 
The Platform will not be involved in the day-to-day operation of vehicles or charging infrastructure. 
◼ 
the Platform will not be involved in the design or manufacturing of vehicles. 
Given the financing nature of the Platform it will not be responsible for the primary development and 
implementation of each sub-project. Instead, the Platform will provide financing (governed by financing 
documentation such as lease or loan contracts) to Counterparties, defined as entities who will be responsible 
for the day-to-day operation of sub-projects.  
Examples of counterparties include: 
◼ 
A bus operating company which is bidding for a new electric bus tender issued by a state government 
for the provision of new e-buses to service an urban area, 
◼ 
A private bus company operating a route between two major cities, 
◼ 
A company constructing a number of new charging points to support the increased penetration of e-
vehicles in a new area, 
◼ 
A large corporate company providing a fleet of cars to their employees. 
2.1.3 
Limitations 
◼ 
Baseline data: There is no baseline provided as most activities require the Platform to be established. 
For other activities, there is limited data available in the sector and the mandate of the program is 
broad in geographic and sector scope across e-mobility making data available very general and not 
context specific. There are activities in the action plan which relate to increasing data availability and, 
where applicable, setting sex-disaggregated baselines and targets. For the avoidance of doubt, any 
data collection, storage, and utilisation of sex-disaggregated data will be compliant with local data 
privacy laws. 
                              134

=== PAGE 137 ===
◼ 
Stakeholder engagement: The Action Plan is designed based on the desk-based Gender Assessment. 
It is intended that stakeholder engagement will be undertaken once the Platform is established basis 
which further actions may be included in the Plan. 
◼ 
Intermediary role: As a financing intervention, the Platform will not be able to directly implement 
activities regarding the operation of the electric vehicles. However, this is mitigated through upfront 
diligence and structuring by the Platform as part of a transaction and by activities flowed through to the 
financing counterparty. 
◼ 
Sub-Projects not yet defined: The Platform has not yet identified specific sub-projects that will be 
financed by the Platform given it is not yet established. As a result, actions for sub-projects cannot be 
fully defined at this stage (such as gender assessment for each sub-project for which sex-
disaggregated data will be collected).  
◼ 
Existing skills, attitudes and practices: The impact of some activities may be impacted by the existing 
context which may limit the outcomes. For example, there may not be sufficient number of women 
skilled or interested in roles in the e-mobility sector due to cultural attitudes towards women working in 
male-dominated sectors. Whilst the Action Plan can implement activities to promote more 
opportunities and equality, the outcomes may be impacted.  
2.1.4 
Roles & Responsibilities  
The primary responsibility of implementation of the Gender Action Plan is with the Gender Specialist who will 
report to the Platform CEO and Board. However, other stakeholders will be involved in different parts of the 
Action Plan. This is outlined at a high level below however responsibilities for specific actions are outlined in 
Table 2.3.

2.2 
Gender Action Plan 
The Platform will initially focus on B2B transactions in the sub-sectors outlined however if the Platform provides financing to other sub-sectors within the e-
mobility value chain (including B2C, or B2SME as defined by Indian regulations) then some of the actions may not apply and applicable actions may be 
identified to apply to the sub-projects in line with the GCF Gender Policy and the impact and outcome statements in the Gender Action Plan. 
It is also recommended that Investment manager discuss these aspects with all its potential counterparties and other relevant business partners at the time of 
their initial interactions to understand how best to integrate these requirements as part of contractual agreements, for effective implementation of the Action 
Plan.  
The data on Gender impacts at the Platform level will be a collation of the reported data from the counterparties. Thus, it is important to ensure that the data 
from counterparties is consistent, temporally aligned and comparable, across reporting categories, based on the indicators selected by Platform. For the 
avoidance of doubt, any data collection, storage and utilisation of sex-disaggregated data will be compliant with local data privacy laws.

=== PAGE 151 ===
APPENDIX A 
GENDER ASSESSMENT OF SUB-PROJECTS  
 
 
                              149

=== PAGE 152 ===
The Platform will undertake specific gender assessment of sub-projects as part of the counterparty 
and sub-project due diligence (included in ESMS) prior to financing. The gender assessment will 
include review against equal access and benefits, identifying risks and mitigants, identifying 
opportunities for gender mainstreaming, what sex-disaggregated data will be collected and 
assessment of the counterparty. 
During screening and due diligence gender-related data will be collected and analysed to determine 
whether the sub-project qualifies under the Platform’s gender policies and identify business 
performance gender opportunities and risks. Gender-related data and information will be gathered 
from the potential counterparty for analysis to determine whether gender gaps present opportunities 
that may impact performance and affect the sub-project operations and financials.  
The following checklist is to be completed by counterparties during the due diligence stage. Its aim is 
to enable the Platform to understand the current level of internal gender diversity and inclusion, to 
identify initiatives that support women entrepreneurs, and acknowledge efforts to serve women clients 
and users. 
For the avoidance of doubt, any data collection, storage and utilisation of sex-disaggregated data will 
be compliant with local data privacy laws. 
Table A1
